Old and new structures on Ran spaces Length structures, completeness, and conicality
Sylvain Douteau, Marie Labeye

Abstract
We study topologies on Ran spaces. In the literature, two distinct topologies frequently appear: the Hausdorff topology, and a finer one constructed as a colimit, that we call the final topology. In this work, given a metric space M M , we construct new metric topologies on Ran ⁡ ( M ) \mathrm{Ran}(M) , called weighted topologies. They interpolate between the Hausdorff and final topologies, the later being recovered as a limit in the category of spaces. This structure equips the final topology with a uniformity, which we show to be complete. Finally we study the Ran spaces as stratified spaces. We show that, whenever M M is a Riemannian manifold, the weighted topologies are conically stratified, while the final topology is only so in a weak sense.



Introduction

Given a topological space, the spaces formed by considering collections of its finite subsets have been studied for a very long time. Already, in the 1930’s Borsuk and Ulam
[
5
]
, studied the spaces of subsets of bounded cardinality, under the names symmetric products
1
1

1

Today, symmetric products refer to the related, but quite distinct, notion where
SP_{n}(M)=M^{n}/\Sigma_{n}
. However in the original paper of Borsuk and Ulam, there is quite a bit of confusion between
SP_{n}(M)
and the truncations
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, and both intuitions seem to coexist.
, and later symmetric potency
[
6
]
. From then on, the study of those spaces continued in algebraic topology, under many names, though they were commonly called plainly "spaces of finite subsets", see for example
[
16
,
20
]
. When the cardinality of the subsets is fixed, this yield the (unordered) configurations spaces of
M
which have also been thoroughly studied (see e.g.
[
22
]
). More recently, the space of all finite subsets has played a central role in the theory of factorization algebras. In their foundational book
[
2
]
, Beilinson and Drinfeld attribute the introduction of this space in algebraic geometry to Ran
[
30
]
, and thus coined the name Ran space. As a set, the Ran space of a topological space
M
is given by

\mathrm{Ran}(M)=\{X\subset M\mid 0<\card(X)<\infty\}

and, as we have seen it has also been studied through its truncations:

\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)=\{X\subset M\mid 0<\card(X)\leq n\}.

Given its long history, it should come as no surprise that the Ran space appears in various context. First, in mathematical physics, factorization algebras can be understood as certain algebraic objects built on Ran spaces, as explained in
[
2
, Chapter 3.4]
. The Ran space has also been studied through the lens of its natural stratification,
\card\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, by studying constructible sheaves
[
29
]
, and its infinity category of exit paths
[
11
]
. Additionally, a surprising application to topological data analysis was outlined by Lazovskis in
[
24
]
, in which he constructs a poset stratification
\varphi\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\to P
, where
P
is a poset encoding all homotopy types of finite simplicial complexes, and the map
\varphi
sends a cluster
X\subset M
and a radius
r>0
to the associated Čech complex.

Ambiguous topologies

While all of those examples are discussing "the" Ran space, they are in fact considering varying (and sometimes, ambiguous) topologies. The article
[
10
]
shines some light on the situation. In their paper Čepek and Lejay study three distinct topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
(or rather
\mathrm{exp(M)}
the set of possibly empty finite subsets) and point out that each of them is used for the study of factorization algebras by one of the key reference in the field. First, Beilinson and Drinfeld, in their book Chiral algebra
[
2
]
, consider on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
the final topology with respect to the collection of maps
M^{n}\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
sending a
n
-tuple
(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})
to the set
X=\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}\}
, in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We will call this topology the
final
topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, see
Section

1.2
. Second, in Lurie’s Higher algebra
[
25
]
the topology considered on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a strictly coarser one called the Hausdorff topology. This is the topology induced by the Hausdorff distance whenever the underlying space
M
is equipped with a metric (see
1.15
). Third, an even coarser topology, that we won’t study in this text, is considered by Costello and Gwilliam in
[
13
]
.

This ambiguity between topologies was already present in the work of Borsuk and Ulam. Indeed, in
[
5
]
, they define
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
(implicitly) as the increasing union of the
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, and claim that its closure is the space of compact subsets of
M
. This suggests a confusion between the set-theoretic definition (
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is indeed the increasing union of its truncations as a set) and the topological definition (which would be needed to provide a precise statement). And while the statement
\overline{\mathrm{Ran}(M)}=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
is meaningful for the Hausdorff topology (see
1.17
),
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
equipped with the final topology, i.e. the topology induced by the increasing union, has the topology of a complete uniform space, see (
Section

6
), and cannot be identified with a subspace of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
.

This is not the only mistake in the history of Ran spaces. Notably, in 1949
[
6
]
, proves that
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 3}(S^{1})
is homeomorphic to
S^{1}\times S^{2}
, only for Bott to correct his mistake a few years later and prove that
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 3}(S^{1})\simeq S^{3}

[
7
]
. This particular computation has been revisited many times since, with improvements such as the identification of the chain of inclusions
S^{1}=\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 1}(S^{1})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 2}(S^{1})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 3}(S^{1})
with the inclusion of
S^{1}
as the boundary of a (triple twisted) Möbius band into
S^{3}
, whose image is a trefoil knot
[
27
]
.

What those results should serve to illustrate is that computations on Ran spaces are difficult, and rich. Whether they are computation in low cardinality, or on the overall space.

Differences at infinity

It is now time to turn to the heart of this paper, which is the study of topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. From now on, we will always assume that the space
M
is equipped with a metric
d
. Any reasonable topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
should allow for the merging of points. That is, if
x,y\in M
are two points that are close in
M
, i.e. if
d(x,y)
is small, then the configurations
\{x,y\}
and
\{x\}
should be close in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and a similar claim should hold for configurations of larger cardinality. This will be a feature of all the topologies we consider, and this intuition is sufficient to arrive at an unambiguous topology on
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
for all
n\geq 1
. The topology we will obtain on
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
will be that of the quotient
M^{n}\to\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. The ambiguity then arises "at infinity", and can be summarized by the following question.

Question 1
.

If
(X_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is a sequence of configurations, containing increasingly many points, which are increasingly closer to some point
x\in M
. Should the sequence converge to
\{x\}
, because of proximity, or diverge, because of cardinality? Or should it depend on the sequence?

A choice of topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is then, in particular, a choice of answer to this question. We already mentioned two possible choices of topology, let us recall some of their properties here.

A topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is that of the increasing union,
\mathrm{Ran}(M)=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. We called it the final topology, and we will denote it
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
. For this topology, the answer to
1
is: the sequence will diverge (see
1.12
).

On the other hand, there is a very direct way of equipping
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
with a metric, which is to consider the Hausdorff distance, defined directly on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
as follows:

d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)=\max\{\min_{x\in X}\{d(x,Y)\},\min_{y\in Y}\{d(X,y)\}\}

this turns
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
into a metric space, and we recover the quotient topology on the subspace
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. The answer to
1
is then: yes, the sequence converges to
\{x\}
.

We now have two topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, the final topology,
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, and the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
induced by the Hausdorff distance
d_{\mathrm{H}}
. Let us summarize in which way they are different, we omit the varying hypothesis on
M
, for clarity.

•

(\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})=(\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
,
\forall n\geq 1
.

•

Convergence in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
is very fine, while it is very coarse in
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
.

•

(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is a metric space, while
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is non-metrizable (
1.13
), though it admits the structure of an uniform space (
Section

6
).

•

(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is not complete, while
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, equipped with the uniformity defined in
Section

6
, is a complete uniform space (
6.19
).

•

(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is defined as a subspace
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
, while
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
can be defined both as a colimit of the
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, and as a limit of metric spaces (
5.9
).

•

The topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
refines
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
(
1.18
).

Once again, the first point should remind us that those two topologies are only distinct "at infinity",
however this distinction should not be taken lightly. In particular, while checking the continuity of maps
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\to N
is easy (one just needs to check that each restriction
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\to N
is continuous) checking that maps into
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
are continuous is highly non-trivial. On the other hand,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
being a metric space provides a systematic way of checking continuity of maps to and from it. While one may consider this observation as a good reason
not
to work with the final topology, we argue that, on the contrary, this rigidity is a good reason to
prefer it
to the Hausdorff topology. Indeed, if one wants to treat the space
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as an invariant of
M
, then one should be interested in
all
maps
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathrm{Ran}(N)
. Now, the answer to the question, "what are those maps?" will depend, a priori, on whether one considers the final or the Hausdorff topology. Intuitively, the fact that the final topology is very rigid at infinity will force those maps to be closer to maps coming from
M\to N
than could be the case for the Hausdorff topology.

Maps between Ran spaces and spaces of embeddings

One reason to be interested in the systematic study of maps between Ran spaces, is their relation to spaces of embeddings. Given two manifolds
M
and
N
, let us denote
\mathrm{emb}(M,N)
the space of embeddings from
M
to
N
. The study of the homotopy type of
\mathrm{emb}(M,N)
has a long history, that we will not attempt to retell here. However, a modern development in this history is the study of configuration categories.
Those were introduced by Andrade in his thesis
[
1
]
, and studied more systematically by Boavida and Weiss who showed
[
4
]
that under some conditions on
M
and
N
, the homotopy type of
\mathrm{emb}(M,N)
could be recovered from the homotopy type of some derived mapping space between
\mathrm{con}(M)
and
\mathrm{con}(N)
, the configuration categories associated to
M
and
N
(see also
[
3
]
, which illustrates how metric techniques can be leveraged to study configurations categories).

The configuration category associated to a manifold
M
is an
\infty
-category, of which Boavida and Weiss give several models. One of which, the "particle model"
[
4
, section 3.1]
, is closely related to (the opposite of) the
\infty
-category of exit paths associated to
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, which was studied by Lurie in
[
25
]
in the context of factorization algebras, and that we will discuss in a moment.

From this relation, we see that a possible topological translation for the derived mapping space
\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{con}(M),\mathrm{con}(N))
would be the (derived) mapping space
\mathrm{Map}(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathrm{Ran}(N))
, in the category of stratified spaces. And while we may expect that the latter derived mapping space does not depend on the choice of topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, it is to be expected that the strict mapping space does, and for that reason, we would prefer to consider a topology as fine as possible on
\mathrm{Ran}
.

Thus, we are left trying to understand continuous maps to
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, which is a very non-trivial endeavor. One way to alleviate this problem would be to find a basis for the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
. The search for such a basis was our initial motivation in introducing weighted distances and topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Weighted topologies as a technical tool

Weighted topologies arise when your choice of answer for
1
is "it depends". It should be immediately clear that there is more than a single way to make the answer depend on the sequence. Weights, which are non-decreasing sequences
\omega\colon\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}\to[1,\infty)
, each corresponds to a criterion to distinguish between those sequences that will converge and those that will not.

Intuitively, the criterion should be that
X_{n}
converges if and only if points in
X_{n}
are getting close to
x
faster than they are being added into
X_{n}
(or faster than
\card(X_{n})
grows), and how much faster is given by
\omega
. A very crude approximation of this idea would be that
X_{n}
converges to
\{x\}
if and only if
\card(X_{n})d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},\{x\})=o\left(\frac{1}{\omega(\card(X_{n}))}\right)
.

What we do is slightly more subtle, but the previous intuition should be a reasonable guide. Given two configurations
X
and
Y
, we may consider a way of going from one to the other as a relation
R\subset X\times Y
. A point
x\in X
may be split into several points in
Y
, say
y
and
y^{\prime}
, which will translate to
(x,y)
and
(x,y^{\prime})
both being in
R
, and symmetrically, several points in
X
may merge in
Y
. We should ask that points do not vanish nor appear out of thin air, and thus every point in
X
and
Y
should be in at least one of the pairs. To rephrase the previous condition, we may say that
R
must be the graph of a surjective multivalued function. One may think of it as an intermediary state, where some points in
X
have split, and some of them will merge in
Y
at the end of their trip. We may then define the length of
R
, with respect to
\omega
as

\ell^{\omega}(R)=\omega(\card(R))\sum_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y)

where the term
\omega(\card(R))
encodes the weight, together with the fact that, in between
X
and
Y
,
\card(R)
many "intermediary points" exist. We may then consider concatenations of such jumps which start at
X
and terminate at
Y
and define a distance
d^{\omega}(X,Y)
by taking it to be the minimum total length among such chains (see
2.5
and
2.7
).

This approach gives rise to infinitely many topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, that we denote
\tau^{\omega}
. They are not all distinct, for example if
\omega
and
\chi
are two weights, such that
\omega(n)=\chi(n)
for sufficiently large
n
, then
\tau^{\omega}=\tau^{\chi}
(see
5.3
). Nevertheless, the set of weighted topologies is uncountable, (see
5.4
and
5.5
)

This infinite collection of topologies (and their associated metrics) then allows us to answer our initial question, at least when
M
is locally compact (see
5.9
).

Theorem 1
.

Let
(M,d)
be a locally compact metric space. A basis for the final topology,
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is given by

\{B^{\omega}(X,r)\mid X\in\mathrm{Ran}(X),\ r>0,\ \text{$\omega$ is a weight}\}

It should be noted that we are not aware of any counter-example to this theorem when
M
fails to be locally compact, and we expect that a simpler proof of
5.9
should exist, without this hypothesis.

This theorem does two things. First, it gives us access to an explicit description of the open sets of the final topology. And then, we may reinterpret it as a statement that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is a limit in the category of topological spaces (see
5.9
). This second statement gives a very explicit condition for checking that maps
N\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
are continuous: we just need to check the continuity of
N\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
for all weights
\omega
. This is made easier by the fact that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is a metric space.

Besides, this theorem gives for free extra structure on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Indeed, provided
M
satisfies the hypothesis, then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
can be equipped with a uniformity, just by computing this limit in the category of uniform spaces (see
Section

6
). Reasoning on the completions of each of the
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
we can then prove the following (see (
6.19
).

Theorem 2
.

Let
M
be a locally compact and complete metric space. Then
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
equipped with the uniformity defined in
6.13
is a complete uniform space.

This somehow gives another reason to prefer working with the final topology rather than the Hausdorff topology. While the latter corresponds to a dense subset of the space of compact subspaces of
M
, the former gives an intrinsic description of the space of finite subsets, with no missing points.

Weighted distances as an object of study

The weighted distances carry some interesting structure of their own. First, the kind of reasoning that appears when computing distances between configurations involves thinking in terms of which points in a configuration are close to each other and to another target configuration. Leading to a kind of reasoning which merges points in order, which is reminiscent of what happens in topological data analysis, when studying persistent complexes. The computation of the distance in itself is very hard, and leads to optimization problems whose complexity quickly gets out of hand (see
Section

4
for a few examples where we do know how to compute the distance).

While the exact computation of the distances can be unachievable in some cases, we do understand it well enough to provide meta computations. Indeed, the distance
d^{\omega}(X,Y)
is defined as an infimum of length over chains between
X
and
Y
, but we prove that one only needs to consider chains where the intermediate configurations
X=X_{0},X_{1},\dots X_{n}=Y
appearing are such that the sequence
\card(X_{0}),\card(X_{1}),\dots,\card(X_{n})
is at first strictly decreasing, and then strictly increasing (see
2.16
and
2.14
for a precise statement). In particular, if
X
and
Y
are configurations in
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, then the distance can be computed inside
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, which considerably simplifies the computation.

Furthermore, if the metric space
(M,d)
satisfies the Heine-Borel property, that is, if the closed balls are compact, then we know that the infimum is reached (see
2.24
).

Theorem 3
.

Assume that
M
satisfies the Heine-Borel property.
Then, for any
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and any weight
\omega
, there exists a chain of relations
(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
between
X
and
Y
such that

d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})

It should be noted that unlike
1
, the hypothesis of
M
satisfying the Heine-Borel property does not appear to be entirely superfluous. In particular, in
4.1
, we show that, for
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{*\}
, some geodesic chains do not exist even though
M
is locally compact. On the other hand, satisfying the Heine-Borel property is certainly not a necessary condition in general, as can be seen by hand by reasoning on the open interval
M=(0,1)
. This suggest that there exist much sharper versions of this theorem, where the Heine-Borel property is replaced by some form of convexity condition.

Chains realizing this infimum are of the form discussed above, and deserve the name
geodesic chains
. They are a combinatorial version of the idea of a shortest path between two configurations. Their existence suggests the study of actual continuous paths which may embody this idea. This leads us to the study of length structures.

Length structures on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)

Let us now assume that the metric space
(M,d)
is equipped with a length structure. That is, a collection of continuous paths,
\mathcal{P}\subset\mathcal{C}^{0}([a,b],M)
which we will call
admissible paths
, together with a length function
\ell\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, satisfying some conditions, recalled in
Section

1.6
. The most notable of those condition is of course that this notion of length recovers the distance in the following way.

d(x,y)=\inf\{\ell(\gamma)\mid\gamma\in\mathcal{P},\gamma(a)=x,\gamma(b)=y\}

In this case we define a set of admissible path in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{Ran}}
, as well as a length
\ell^{\omega}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{Ran}}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
, for any weight
\omega
(see
3.2
,
3.3
,
3.5
and
3.6
). We then prove (see
3.8
and
3.9
).

Theorem 4
.

If
(M,d)
is a length space, then the metric on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
associated to the length structure given by
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{Ran}}
and
\ell^{\omega}
is
d^{\omega}
.

Moreover, we may now ask if geodesics exist in the usual sense of paths realizing the distance. We prove that they do under additional hypothesis on
M
(
3.21
).

Theorem 5
.

Assume that the metric space
(M,d)
is a length space, that it satisfies the Heine-Borel property, and that any two points in
M
are connected by a geodesic. Then, for any two configurations
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and any weight
\omega
, there exists a path
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
such that
\Gamma(a)=X
,
\Gamma(b)=Y
and

\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)=d^{\omega}(X,Y).

Note that, crucially, the path
\Gamma
realizing the distance may depend on the choice of
\omega
. And such a path is geodesic for a single choice of values for
\omega
up to some
n
in general, see
4.1
. We can prove once more, however, that their "shape" is always the same. That is, a geodesic path in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
for any weight
\omega
, must be a path
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, such that there exists some
c\in[a,b]
such that, when restricted to
[a,c]
,
\card(\Gamma)
is a non-increasing function and when restricted to
[c,b]
,
\card(\Gamma)
is a non-decreasing function. This is deeply related to the stratification of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
given by
\card\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, and its invariants.

The stratification of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)

Indeed, the cardinality map
\card\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
is a stratification on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, whether one considers the Hausdorff, weighted or final topology (See
Section

1.4
). As such, one may want to look at
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
not as a space, but as a stratified space, which is a noticeably distinct point of view, at least when doing homotopy theory.

In such a context, it is important to know if stratified spaces are geometrically well-behaved. Specifically, it is natural to ask of such spaces if they are conically stratified. We recall the precise definition at the beginning of section
Section

7
, but its core idea can be summarized as every point in the stratified space admitting a neighborhood homeomorphic to the product of a cone and a neighborhood in the strata. If they are, then a theorem of Lurie
[
25
, Theorem A.6.4]
states that a particular invariant, called the
\infty
-category of exit paths can be defined.

Whether
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a conically stratified space when equipped with the Hausdorff or the final topology has already been investigated. For the former, a proof that it is conical, when
M
is a Hausdorff space locally homeomorphic to some (possibly infinite dimensional) topological vector spaces is given in
[
10
, Theorem 4.12]
. On the other hand, for the final topology, it is easy to show that when
M
is a manifold of dimension at least
1
, then
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
cannot be conically stratified. Indeed, if it were, then the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
would automatically be first countable, yet it is not. A more detailed argument is given in the proof of
7.40
.

While true, the previous statement is somewhat misleading. First of all, even though
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is not conically stratified, it still admits an
\infty
-category of exit paths, for the reason that this invariant can be computed only from the data of the truncations (which coincide with those of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
). And secondly, as we show at the very end of
Section

7
, when
M
is a manifold,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
fails to be conically stratified on a technicality. Specifically, given a topological space
L
, there are two, a priori distinct, topologies one can equip the quotient
L\times[0,1]/L\times\{0\}
with.
The first is the quotient topology. The second, called the teardrop topology, is coarser in general. Whenever
L
is a compact space, those two topologies coincide. Since most geometrically occurring stratified spaces carry compact links, there are few occasions to be made aware of this subtlety. But it turns out that for the definition of conically stratified space to behave correctly, (in particular, for the proof of
[
25
, Theorem A.6.4]
to go through), one needs to use the teardrop topology. On the other hand, if one were to naively replace the teardrop topology by the quotient topology in the definition of a conically stratified space, then one gets a naive definition of conicality. We show that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
satisfies this naive definition whenever
M
is a manifold (
7.44
).

We also investigate whether the weighted topologies that we introduce turn
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
into a conically stratified space. We answer in
7.38
.

Theorem 6
.

Let
(M,d)
be a Riemannian manifold, and
\omega
be a weight. Then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is conically stratified.

It can be argued that the result holds in much more generality. Due to a result of Sullivan about Lipschitz manifolds
[
31
]
, provided
M
is a (topological) manifold of dimension other than
4
, we can meaningfully say that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is conically stratified without specifying a metric on
M
. This is a very subtle point and a more detailed discussion can be found in
7.39
.

The above theorem is really a simple corollary of the observation that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is conically stratified when
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a Euclidean space,
7.37
, which is what we spend most of
Section

7
proving. We do so in a very explicit way, providing concrete descriptions of the links, and of the local homeomorphisms in terms of geometric constructions in
\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
.

Surprisingly, some topological arguments are still needed to prove the naive conicality of
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, and it cannot be formally deduced from the conicality of the
\tau^{\omega}
. We discuss in
7.45
why abstract nonsense alone is not sufficient to conclude.

Geodesics and exit paths

For reasons discussed in the previous section,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
and
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
all admit an (
\infty
)-category of exit paths (and in fact,
the same one
). Let us briefly recall what this invariant is.

The motivation behind this invariant stems from the well-known result that, given a reasonable topological space
M
, locally constant sheaves on it are equivalent to functors from its fundamental groupoid,
\Pi_{1}(M)
. The category
\Pi_{1}(M)
can be succinctly described as the category whose objects are points in
M
, and whose morphisms between two points
x,y\in M
are continuous paths
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M
up to boundary preserving homotopies.

The category
\Pi_{1}(M)
is an homotopy invariant of
M
, but one cannot recover
M
from the data of
\Pi_{1}(M)
in general. However, one can recover
M
up to weak equivalence, from the data of
\Pi(M)
, the infinity groupoid of
M
. It is an infinity category in which objects are points of
M
, morphisms are continuous paths
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M
, but instead of quotienting by homotopies, one considers higher morphisms to be boundary preserving homotopies between morphisms of lower dimension.

The idea of following this construction for stratified spaces seems to date back to MacPherson, though the first published steps in defining the exit path infinity category are due to Treumann
[
32
]
and Woolf
[
33
]
. Given reasonable stratified spaces
\varphi\colon T\to P
, they define
2
-categorical and
1
-categorical versions of the category of exit paths, whose object are points of
T
and whose morphisms are (equivalence classes of) continuous paths
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to T
which are exit paths. That is, there must be some
p\leq q\in P
such that
\varphi(\gamma(0))=p
and
\varphi(\gamma(t))=q
for all
0<t\leq 1
. In particular, such a path must visit at most two strata.

Later, Lurie introduced an infinity categorical version of this category
[
25
, Appendix A]
. Several variations of homotopy theories for stratified spaces have since been introduced
[
28
,
14
,
19
]
. They all satisfy some version of the following statement

Statement
.

Let
T\to P
be a stratified space. Provided
\mathrm{Exit}(T)
is well-defined, then one can recover
T\to P
up to stratified weak equivalence from the data of
\mathrm{Exit}(T)
.

Thus, understanding the homotopy type of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a stratified space amounts to understanding the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Exit}(\mathrm{Ran}(M))
. The first step being understanding its
1
-morphisms, which are the exit paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. A first step in this direction can be deduced from results we have already discussed.

Theorem 7
.

Let
(M,d)
be a complete Riemannian manifold,
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
\omega
be a weight. Then there exists a geodesic
\Gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
between
X
and
Y
, and
c\in[0,1]
such that

•

\Gamma_{|[c,1]}
is a concatenation of exit paths.

•

\Gamma_{|[0,c]}
is a concatenation of opposites of exit paths.

Thus geodesics can be decomposed into exit paths, which are themselves shorter geodesics. We may attempt to study
\mathrm{Exit}(\mathrm{Ran}(M))
through subcategories formed by only considering exit paths that are geodesic for some weight
\omega
. While we leave this part of the study for future works, this was a motivation in attempting to prove the existence and characterization of geodesics in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Invariance properties of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a space

Studying Ran spaces, their truncations
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, or their strata
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
, as invariants of the underlying space
M
, is certainly not a new idea. Though, there are numerous immediate obstructions to doing so. First, and most obvious, if
f\colon M\to N
is an arbitrary continuous map between two topological spaces, then
f
does not induce a continuous map
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)\to\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(N)
. It does induce a continuous map
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathrm{Ran}(N)
, sending
X\subset M
to
f(X)\subset N
, which restricts properly to the truncations, but this map will not be a stratum preserving map in general.
Indeed, if
f
fails to be injective, then there are some
X\subset M
such that
\card(f(X))<\card(X)
.

Still, there have been attempts to interpret those objects as some sort of homotopy invariants of
M
. For configurations spaces in particular, it is immediately obvious that those are not homotopy invariants on the nose. Consider for example that
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{0})
is empty yet
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{1})
is non empty but contractible, and
\mathrm{Conf}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
is non contractible.

Yet, in
[
21
]
, Idrissi proved that provided
M
is a simply connected closed manifold, the real homotopy type of
\mathrm{Conf}^{\text{ord}}_{n}(M)
can be recovered from the real homotopy type of
M
. Here
\mathrm{Conf}^{\text{ord}}_{n}(M)
corresponds to the
ordered
configuration space, i.e. the open subset of
M^{n}
which covers
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
, and the real homotopy type roughly refers to the part of the homotopical information about
M
which survives when tensoring the homotopy groups with
\mathbb{R}
. Which is to say, part of the homotopical information of
\mathrm{Conf}^{\text{ord}}_{n}(M)
can be deduced only from homotopical information about
M
(of the same nature).

On the other hand, the case of the homotopical invariance of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is trivial. Indeed, when
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is equipped with the final topology,
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is weakly contractible as soon as
M
is Hausdorff and path connected. (see
[
20
, Corollary 4.3]
). This follows from the observation that any continuous map
S^{k}\to\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
can be homotoped to a constant map inside
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n^{\prime}}(M)
for some
n^{\prime}\geq n
. Since any map
S^{k}\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
which is continuous for the final topology must factor through some truncations
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, this implies that the homotopy groups of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
are all trivial.

The same result is known for the Hausdorff topology, and can be obtained by the method outlined in
[
2
, section 3.4.1]
, though the authors present their proof for the final topology (see
[
25
, Section 5.5.1]
for a version of this proof applied to the Hausdorff topology). It should be expected that the same holds for the weighted topologies, and that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is also contractible, though we do not investigate this here. One expects that a proof should follow from the observation made above that all of those topologies coincide on truncations, hence they correspond to weakly equivalent stratified spaces.

The last purely topological invariant one could hope for are the truncations of the Ran spaces, which are both homotopy invariant and non-trivial in general. Indeed, as we have seen
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 3}(S^{1})\simeq S^{3}
is non-contractible, and an immediate computation shows that homotopic maps
f,g\colon M\to N
induce homotopic maps between truncations
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\to\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(N)
. However, if one tries to assemble each of those invariants for
n\geq 1
, that is, if one takes the increasing union, then one recovers
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
which is trivial.
Thus, to produce a meaningful invariant out of all the truncations, one needs to consider
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a stratified space
2
2

2

One may instead choose to consider
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a filtered space. That is, equipped with the filtration given by the increasing union. This was, for a long time, the preferred point of view when discussing objects with singularity. See for example the case of Whitney stratifications. Nowadays, this point of view has been mainly replaced by that of poset stratifications, which we presented here. It should be emphasized that those two points of view do not lend themselves to the study of the same kinds of maps, and as such would not produce the same homotopy theory (if one were to construct one for filtered spaces). Thus, the reason we choose to see
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a stratified space instead of a filtered space is not because the points of view are equivalent (they are not), nor because the poset-stratified point of view is inherently better (it may or may not be) but because only the former homotopy theory has already been developed.
.

Properties of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a stratified space

The space
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
equipped with its stratification
\card\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
has some very peculiar properties as a stratified space, especially when
(M,d)
is a Riemannian manifold. Indeed, it is an example coming from geometry - in particular not a purpose-built counter example - which is conically stratified (naively so for the final topology) yet does not admit a regular stratum. This is not entirely unique, since any construction of an infinite dimensional object out of finite dimensional strata will fail to have a regular stratum, but is in stark contrast with the classical examples encoding the idea of "manifolds with singularities".

The fact that several topologies on the underlying space
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
are routinely used, all with the same geometrical intuition - that collision of points should be continuous - and sometimes for the same goal is also striking. Of course, this is in part due to the fact that a lot of focus lies on the truncations, which coincide from one topology to another. Another related explanation of this phenomenon is that, from the point of view of homotopy theory, those topologies are not different since they yield the same (weak) stratified homotopy type. Meaning, from the point of view of stratified homotopy theory, it is unambiguous to speak about the Ran space, without specifying the topology under consideration.

On the other hand, as we have seen, the final topology fails to be conically stratified, it is only naively so. Yet, we have proven that it is a limit of conically stratified space, through a reasonably mild functor (it is indexed by a poset, and all the maps are just refinements of topology). To our knowledge, this is the first example of this kind. Still, the final topology admits an
\infty
-category of exit paths (by virtue of being weakly equivalent to conically stratified spaces).

The stratified homotopy type of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
itself is interesting. It is non-trivial in general, even when
M
is a Euclidean space, yet it is known that the underlying space is contractible. This is absolutely not the first example of this kind, not even the first geometrically occuring, since any stratifications of the infinite dimensional sphere by increasing inclusions of finite dimensional ones shares this property. Yet, it is notable that non-trivial information about any space
M
can be stored in the contractible space
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
by recalling the stratification of the latter
3
3

3

Straying a bit from the philosophy of this example the stratified cone on any space
M
also shares those properties, in a somewhat tautological way. The stratified homotopy type of the cone is a strict invariant of the homotopy type of
M
however.
.

And, as discussed, if
M
is equipped with a length structure, we may hope to recover some description of the homotopical information contained in the stratified space
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
through the study of geodesics in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
.

Overall, this begs the following question.

Question 2
.

Given a topological space
M
, how much information about its homeomorphism type can be recovered from the (weak) stratified homotopy type of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
?

Clearly, the stratified homotopy type of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
fully recovers the homotopy type of
M
, since stratified homotopy equivalences preserve the homotopy type of strata. But since the stratified homotopy type of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is not itself an homotopy invariant of
M
we may hope to recover quantifiably more than that.

Now, if we look specifically at the spaces
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
for a given weight
\omega
, we may ask what it is an invariant of. The situation is even more drastic than it is for configuration spaces. Indeed, for a continuous map
f\colon(M,d)\to(N,d^{\prime})
to induce a continuous stratified map between the Ran spaces of
M
and
N
equipped with the topologies associated to
\omega
, it is not even enough to ask that
f
be injective (see
4.7
). One should ask in addition that
f
be locally Lipschitz, just to get a continuous map between the topologies
\tau^{\omega}
. We prove specifically
4.20
.

Theorem 8
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category of metric spaces and locally Lipschitz maps, then for any weight
\omega
, the following functor is well-defined

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}\colon\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle(M,d)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})

\displaystyle f\colon(M,d)\to(N,d^{\prime})

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{Ran}(f)\colon\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(M,d)\to\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(N,d^{\prime})

Furthermore, if
f\colon(M,d)\to(N,d^{\prime})
is a map in
\mathcal{C}
which is injective, then
\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(f)
is stratum preserving.

Which now suggests that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
recalls not only information about the space
M
, but also about its metric, even without recalling the induced metric
d^{\omega}
, otherwise this is tautological. Since existence of maps between weighted topologies is not a given for maps that would otherwise lift to the final topology, we may also ask:

Question 3
.

Given a metric space
(M,d)
, how much information about its metric can be recovered from the properties of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
and the (strict) stratified homotopy type of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
for
\omega
a weight ?

In particular, there is no reason to believe
a priori
that a Whitehead type theorem should be true for the stratified spaces
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
. Hence the strict stratified homotopy type of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
may contain extra information about the metric, compared to the weak stratified homotopy type of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
(which coincide whether one considers the weighted or the final topologies). While we do not attempt to address those questions here, we expect that the tool we developed will help make explicit computations easier for future work.

Organization of the document

Immediately after this introduction, a very brief summary of notations can be found. Since some of the proofs can be notationally heavy, it may be useful to refer back to
Table

1
to disambiguate between notations. Outside of possible mistakes, every object appearing in the document should be written in a way consistent with this table.

The rest of the document is organized as follows.

Section

1
contains known results and constructions about Ran spaces that we will need throughout the text, together with classical definitions, such as that of length structure on a metric space, or of stratified spaces. Any reader somewhat familiar with those subjects may choose to skip it, and possibly refer back to it later.

The weighted distances are introduced in
Section

2
. After working through the definition in section
Section

2.1
, the next two sections are spent proving
2.16
, which states that the weighted distance between two configurations
X
and
Y
can be computed by only considering chains which first merge points two at a time, and then, after a bijection, splits them two at a time. We leverage this structure in
Section

2.4
to prove that in any metric space satisfying the Heine-Borel property, the weighted distance, which is defined as an infimum, is in fact the length of a certain geodesic chain. This is
2.24
.

Section

3
then focuses on the particular case in which
(M,d)
carries a length structure. This is the case for example if
(M,d)
is a Riemannian manifold. In
Section

3.1
, we construct a length structure on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, given any weight
\omega
, and show that the distance it induces is
d^{\omega}
, the weighted distance introduced in
Section

2
(
3.8
). Then, in
Section

3.2
, we show
3.21
, a continuous analogue of
2.24
. It states that, if we assume in addition to the hypothesis of the latter theorem that any two points in
M
are connected by a geodesic, then any two configurations in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
are also connected by a geodesic, for any distance
d^{\omega}
.

Section

4
is then focused on explicit computations of weighted distances. In
Section

4.1
, we first go through several illustrated examples, to underline which intuitions are correct, and dissipate some incorrect assumptions. It should be fairly accessible, and readers may find it helpful to read those examples while working through
Section

2
. Examples should make it clear why some apparently trivial statements about the weighted distances do need non-trivial proofs.

Section

4.2
is then concerned with several lemmas related to computation of distances in the neighborhood of a configuration. Those results will be needed in later sections, and help shed some light on the techniques that are available to compute weighted distances.
Finally,
Section

4.3
is concerned with the invariance of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
with respect to maps of metric spaces
f\colon(M,d)\to(N,d^{\prime})
.

The characterization of the final topology is then the main focus of
Section

5
. After examining how the weighted topologies compare to each other in
Section

5.1
, we prove
5.9
in
Section

5.2
. This states that the final topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is obtained as the limit of the weighted topologies in the category of topological spaces, provided that the space
M
is locally compact.

When the previous theorem holds,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
then inherits a uniform structure from being a limit of uniform spaces (
6.14
), which we investigate in
Section

6
. Specifically, this section is devoted to proving that whenever
M
is a complete and locally compact metric space, then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is a complete uniform space (
6.19
). To work through the proof, we first extend the definitions of
Section

2
to also apply to infinite chains in
Section

6.1
. We use this in
Section

6.2
, to characterize non-converging Cauchy sequences in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
. This allows us to identify the completion of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
, as a set, with a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
, which we leverage in
Section

6.3
to prove that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is complete, by expressing it as a limit of complete spaces.

Finally,
Section

7
focuses on conicality. We first prove that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
is a conically stratified space, whenever
(M,d)
is a Riemannian manifold (
7.38
), and then that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is conically stratified in the naive sense, (
7.43
). The latter follows easily from the techniques introduced to prove the former, and is done at the very end in
Section

7.6
. The proof of the former is very long, in part due to the fact that we do not prove the mere existence of a local homeomorphism, but provide an explicit construction in terms of geometric maps which we define for this purpose. Given the length and technicality of this proof, we provide a summary in
Section

7.2
, which follows a reminder about conical stratifications in
Section

7.1
.

Notations and terminology

About the name "final topology"

The topology of the increasing union
\mathrm{Ran}(M)=\cup_{n\geq 1}\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
could be given many names. The most natural thing, for any algebraic topologist, or anyone familiar with CW complexes would be to call it the "weak topology". However, this topology is actually the
finest
topology among all we will consider (and possibly, the finest topology one may reasonably want to consider on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
), thus the name would be very misleading. Another possibility would be to call it the "colimit topology", which is factually correct since an increasing union is nothing more than a colimit in the category of topological spaces. However, we will prove that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is also a limit in the category of uniform spaces (see
5.9
and
Section

6
) we thus fear that this name would lead to confusion. In the end, the name "final topology" refers to the properties of the maps
\pi_{n}\colon M^{n}\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
which play a central role throughout this paper, and has the added advantage that the "fin" in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
stands for both "final topology" and "finest topology".

Notational conventions

This brief section aims to help the reader navigate through the notations of this work. Most of the notations are summarized in
Table

1
.
