Table 1:
Summary of notations

We will always consider a metric space
(M,d)
. In rare cases, the index
M
will be specified on the distance
(M,d_{M})
and balls
B_{M}(x,r)
to avoid confusion. In the case where
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
for some
p\in\mathbb{N}
, we will write
x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{p})
the coordinates of its elements in the canonical basis, while the elements of the Cartesian product will be written
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in M^{n}
.

We will consider the following topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, from finest to coarsest:

•

The final topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
which is the final topology for the quotient applications
\pi_{n}\colon M^{n}\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, see
1.8

•

The weighted topologies
\tau^{\omega},\tau^{\chi}
induced by the weighted distances
d^{\omega},d^{\chi}
for various weights
\omega,\chi\in\mathbb{W}
, introduced in
Section

2

•

The Hausdorff topology
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
induced by the Hausdorff distance
d_{\mathrm{H}}
, see
1.15

All these topologies coincide on the truncations
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
of the Ran space (see
1.25
) and thus when discussing those, their will be no need to specify the topology.

1
Preliminaries

This work aims at defining and studying new distances and topologies on Ran spaces. In this section we recall some known facts about Ran spaces and some useful notions of general topology. We first define configuration spaces (
Section

1.1
) before introducing the Ran space together with two of its topologies. The final topology in
Section

1.2
, which is induced by the description of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as an increasing union, and the Hausdorff topology in
Section

1.3
, which is induced by the Hausdorff distance. We recall the notion of a stratified space in
Section

1.4
, and describe the stratified structure of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.
In
Section

1.5
we recall some properties of the truncations of the Ran space that will be useful throughout this document. Finally,
Section

1.6
discusses length structures in preparation of
Section

3
which will discuss how such structures are inherited by
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
whenever
M
is itself a length space.

Unless otherwise stated
(M,d)
will always be a metric space, and, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we always equip
M^{n}
with the following distance, which is compatible with the product topology,

\displaystyle d_{\Sigma}\colon M^{n}\times M^{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle((x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}),(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)}))

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})

1.1
Configuration spaces

We begin by defining the configuration spaces, both the ordered and unordered ones.

Definition 1.1
.

Let
M
be a topological space, and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. The
n^{\text{th}}
ordered configuration space of
M
, denoted
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
, is the set of
n
-tuples of
M^{n}
whose elements are pairwise distinct, i.e.:

\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)=\{(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\mid x^{(i)}\neq x^{(j)},\forall i\neq j\in\{1,\dots,n\}\}\subset M^{n},

equipped with the topology induced by the inclusion.

Remark 1.2
.

If
M
is a topological manifold of dimension
\geq 1
, one can check that
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
is a dense subset of
M^{n}
for any
n\geq 1
.

Definition 1.3
.

Let
M
be a topological space, and let
m,n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
f\colon\{1,\dots,m\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
. Then, for all
(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in M^{n}
, write
f\cdot(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})=(x^{(f(1))},\dots,x^{(f(m))}))\in M^{m}
.

Remark 1.4
.

If
n=m
, this defines an action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}=\{f\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}\mid f\text{ bijective}\}
on
M^{n}
which restricts to an action on
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
.

Definition 1.5
.

The
n^{\text{th}}
configuration space of
M
, denoted
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
is the quotient of
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
by the action of the permutation group
\Sigma_{n}
, equipped with the quotient topology. As a set, it is explicitly described as

\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)=\{X\subset M\mid\card(X)=n\}.

We will need the following proposition in
Section

3
to understand paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
by studying lift through
M^{n}
.

Proposition 1.6
.

If
M
is Hausdorff, then the quotient application
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)\to\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
is a covering.

Proof.

Since
M
is Hausdorff, for any
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
we have a set
(U_{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,n\}}
of disjoint neighborhoods of the
x^{(i)}
in
M
. The product
U=\prod_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}
is a neighborhood of
\underline{x}
in
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
such that, for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{n}
, we have
\sigma\cdot U\cap U=\emptyset
. In particular, the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
is properly discontinuous. Since
\Sigma_{n}
acts by homeomorphism, the quotient application is a covering.
∎

1.2
Ran space and final topology

We are now able to define the underlying set of Ran spaces as the disjoint union of the configuration spaces. But we have to define a topology on this set
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We recall two definitions commonly found in the literature: the final topology in this subsection, then the Hausdorff distance and its associated topology in the following one.

Definition 1.7
.

Let
M
be a topological space, the
Ran space
of
M
, denoted
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, has an underlying set composed of the union of the configuration spaces of
M
:

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(M)=\bigsqcup_{i\geq 1}\mathrm{Conf}_{i}(M)=\{X\subset M\mid 0<\card(X)<\infty\}.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, the
n^{\text{th}}
truncation of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, denoted
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
is the subset defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Conf}_{i}(M)=\{X\subset M\mid 0<\card(X)\leq n\}.

Definition 1.8
.

Let
M
be a topological space, and
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. We define

\displaystyle\pi_{n}\colon M^{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})

\displaystyle\mapsto\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}\}.

The final topology for the
\pi_{n}
is called the
final topology
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and written
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Remark 1.9
.

We can give another definition of the final topology. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, we equip
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, the
n^{\text{th}}
truncation of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, with the quotient topology associated to the quotient map
\pi_{n}^{|\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)}\colon M^{n}\to\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. We then remark that, for these topologies, the inclusion
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\to\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n+1}(M)
is the inclusion of a closed subspace. The final topology is then that of the increasing union

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)=\bigcup_{n\geq 1}\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M).

(1)

We easily check that the two definitions are equivalent. If
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a subset of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, it is open for the final topology if and only if
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
is open in
M^{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Besides
U\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
is open if and only if
\left(\pi_{n}^{|\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)}\right)^{-1}(U\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M))
is open in
M^{n}
. Since
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
is the image of
\pi_{n}
, the two conditions are identical, and the topologies coincide. In particular,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is directly obtained as the colimit of its truncations.

Remark 1.10
.

The restriction of
\pi_{n}
coincides with the quotient application
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)\to\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
which corresponds to the quotient by the action of the symmetric group
\Sigma_{n}
on
\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)
. We emphasize however that the application
\pi_{n}
do
not
correspond to the quotient by the action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
M^{n}
. Indeed, for
n=3
and for any
x\neq y\in M
,
\pi_{3}(x,x,y)=\pi_{3}(x,y,y)
but
(x,x,y)
and
(x,y,y)
are
not equivalent
under the action of
\Sigma_{3}
. The quotient of
M^{n}
with respect to the action of
\Sigma_{n}
is actually the
n^{\text{th}}
symmetric product of
M
, and is not, in general, isomorphic to
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. See
[
12
]
for a discussion of the difference between those two spaces in the case where
M=S^{1}
. Note that Chinen and Koyama use the terminology of symmetric hyperspaces for what we call the truncations of the Ran space. We will just mention here the illustrative example that all the
n^{\text{th}}
symmetric products of
S^{1}
are homotopy equivalent to
S^{1}
, and so is their colimit. In particular, the
3^{\text{rd}}
symmetric product of
S^{1}
is a solid torus
[
26
]
. On the contrary
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq 3}(S^{1})
is homeomorphic to
S^{3}
, and
\mathrm{Ran}(S^{1})
is a contractible space.

We recall here a few known properties on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, that we will show can be reinterpreted as direct consequences of
5.9
.

Lemma 1.11
.

Let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. If the sequence
\card(X_{k})
is strictly increasing, then
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\{X_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is open in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Proof.

Let
K=\{X_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
and
U=\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus K
. For
n\geq 1
, we have
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)=M^{n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(K)=M^{n}\setminus\pi_{n}^{-1}(K\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M))
. Since the cardinality of
X_{k}
is increasing,
K
contains finitely many configurations of cardinality less than
n
. Since
\pi_{n}
has finite fibers,
\pi_{n}^{-1}(K)
is a finite subset of
M^{n}
, and thus
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
is open for all
n\geq 1
. This proves that
U
is an open set for
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
∎

Proposition 1.12
.

Let
(X_{k})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Assume that
\card(X_{k})
is unbounded for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then the sequence
(X_{k})
does not converge for the final topology.

Proof.

By contradiction, assume that
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges toward some limit
X
. We may assume that
\card(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a strictly increasing sequence by extracting an appropriate subsequence. Similarly, we may assume that
X\not\in K=\{X_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Then, by
1.11

U=\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus K
is an open set for the final topology. In particular,
U
is an open neighborhood of
X
, but
X_{k}\not\in U
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
which contradicts the assumption that
X_{k}
converges to
X
.
∎

Proposition 1.13
.

Let
M
be a metric space. Let us suppose that there exists
x\in M
such that for any
\epsilon>0
the ball
B(x,\epsilon)
contains infinitely many points, i.e.
x
is an accumulation point. Then, the space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is not first countable. In particular,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is not metrizable.

Proof.

Let us fix
(U_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
a countable family of neighborhood of
\{x\}
in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
and let us show that it cannot be a basis of neighborhood. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the preimage
\pi_{k+2}^{-1}(U_{k})\subset M^{k+2}
is an open neighborhood of
\underline{x_{k}}=(x,\dots,x)
in
M^{k+2}
. Therefore, there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x_{k}},\epsilon)\subset\pi_{k+2}^{-1}(U_{k})
. By hypothesis, we can find
k+2
distinct elements
y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(k+2)}
in
B(x,\frac{\epsilon}{k+2})\subset M
, so that the
k+2
-tuple
(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(k+2)})\in B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x_{k}},\epsilon)\subset\pi_{k+2}^{-1}(U_{k})
. We thus have constructed a configuration
X_{k}=\pi_{k+2}(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(k+2)})\in U_{k}
of cardinality
\card(X_{k})=k+2
. By
1.11
the set
U=\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\{X_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is open in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Moreover, since
\card(X_{k})=k+2>1
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we have
\{x\}\in U
, so that
U
is an open neighborhood of
\{x\}
. But since, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
X_{k}\not\in U
then
U_{k}\not\subset U
. Therefore,
(U_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
cannot be a basis of neighborhood.
∎

Finally, the following property will be useful to demonstrate the continuity of maps defined on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Proposition 1.14
.

Let
M,B
be two spaces,
A\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
a clopen of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
equipped with the subspace topology, and
f\colon A\to B
an application. Then
f
is continuous if and only if its restrictions
f_{\leq n}\colon A_{\leq n}\to B
to the truncations
A_{\leq n}=\{X\in A\mid\card(X)\leq n\}
are continuous for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

There is nothing to prove for the "only if" case. Let us suppose that the restrictions
f_{\leq n}
are continuous for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. First, let us write
A=U\cap F
, with
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
open and
F\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
closed. Let
V\subset B
and let us show that
f^{-1}(V)\subset A
is open. Let
n\geq 1
. Then
f_{n}^{-1}(V)\subset A_{\leq n}
is open in
A_{\leq n}
. There exists
W\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
open such that
f_{n}^{-1}(V)=W\cap A_{\leq n}=W\cap U\cap F\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, so that
f_{n}^{-1}(V)
is open in
F\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. Therefore
F\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\setminus f_{n}^{-1}(V)
is closed in
F\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
and thus in
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. But since
F\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\setminus f_{n}^{-1}(V)=\left(F\setminus f^{-1}(V)\right)\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, this proves that
\left(F\setminus f^{-1}(V)\right)\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
is closed in
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
for all
n\geq 1
. Since the final topology is also induced by the increasing union in
Eq.

1
, this proves that
F\setminus f^{-1}(V)
is closed in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and thus it is also closed in
F
. So that finally
f^{-1}(V)
is open in
F
and therefore it is also open in
A
.
∎

1.3
Ran spaces and Hausdorff topology

The Hausdorff topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
can be defined for any topological space
M

[
25
, section 5.5.1]
. Nevertheless, when
M
is a metric space, the Hausdorff topology is induced by a distance,
d_{\mathrm{H}}
, called the Hausdorff distance. Since we mainly focus on distances, we will only give the definition in that case, which is considerably simpler.

Definition 1.15
.

The Hausdorff distance on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, denoted
d_{\mathrm{H}}
is defined as

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\times\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle(X,Y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\max\left\{\max_{x\in X}\{d(x,Y)\},\max_{y\in Y}\{d(X,y)\}\right\},

where
d(x,Y)=\min_{y\in Y}\{d(x,y)\}
. We call Hausdorff topology, denoted
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
, the topology induced by the Hausdorff distance on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.
We will write, for any
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
r>0

\displaystyle B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)=\left\{Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)\mid d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)<r\right\}.

Remark 1.16
.

The Hausdorff distance can be defined more generally on the power set
\mathcal{P}(M)
, although it is only a pseudo-distance in this context. We get a distance by restraining to the compact sets of
M
. We will write
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
the set of non-empty compact subsets of
M
. Note that, if
M
is a complete metric space, then
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is a complete metric space
[
15
, Theorem 2.5.2, p72]
. This is the subject of the following proposition.

Proposition 1.17
.

If
M
is a complete metric space, then
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is the completion of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
.

Proof.

Let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
.
As shown in
[
15
, Theorem 2.5.2, p72]
, the set
X=\{x:\text{ there is a sequence }(x_{k})\text{ with }x_{k}\in X_{k}\text{ and }x_{k}\to x\}
is a non-empty compact subset of
M
such that
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X)
goes to zero. Which shows that the completion of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is a subspace of
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
.

Let
K\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
. Since it is compact, it is precompact, so that for any
k\geq 1
there exists
X_{k}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
such that
K\subset\bigcup_{x\in X_{k}}B(x,\frac{1}{k})
, and
K\cap B(x,\frac{1}{k})\neq\emptyset
for any
x\in X_{k}
. We thus have
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},K)\leq\frac{1}{k}
, so that
(X_{k})
converges to
K
, and
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is a subspace of the completion of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
.
∎

Let us compare the two topologies that we just defined.

Proposition 1.18
.

The final topology is finer that the Hausdorff topology.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
r>0
. Let us show that
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
is open in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
. To this end, let us fix
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let us show that
\pi^{-1}_{n}(B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r))
is open in
M^{n}
. Let
\underline{y}=(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)})\in\pi^{-1}_{n}(B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r))
, and
Y=\pi_{n}(\underline{y})
. Let us define
\epsilon=r-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)>0
and let us show that
B(y^{(1)},\epsilon)\times\dots\times B(y^{(n)},\epsilon)
is an open neighborhood of
\underline{y}
in
\pi^{-1}_{n}(B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r))
. Let
\underline{z}=(z^{(1)},\dots,z^{(n)})\in B(y^{(1)},\epsilon)\times\dots\times B(y^{(n)},\epsilon)
. For any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle d(y^{(i)},Z)\leq d(y^{(i)},z^{(i)})<\epsilon,

and

\displaystyle d(z^{(i)},Y)\leq d(z^{(i)},y^{(i)})<\epsilon.

So that

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(Y,Z)=\max\{\max_{y\in Y}\{d(y,Z)\},\max_{z\in Z}\{d(Y,z)\}\}<\epsilon

and finally

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)+d_{\mathrm{H}}(Y,Z)<d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)+\epsilon<r,

which proves that
\underline{z}\in\pi^{-1}_{n}(B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r))
. We thus found an open neighborhood of
\underline{y}
in
\pi^{-1}_{n}(B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r))
, i.e.
\pi^{-1}_{n}(B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r))
is open in
M^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and thus
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
is open in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

In particular, we showed that the identity
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
is continuous.

∎

Remark 1.19
.

As soon as
M
has an accumulation point, we showed in
1.13
that the final topology is not first countable, in particular it is not metrizable. Therefore, the two topologies differ, and the final topology is strictly finer that the Hausdorff topology. Besides, note that the proof of
1.13
defines a sequence of increasing cardinality in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, which cannot converge for the final topology, by
1.12
. Nevertheless, it does converge to a singleton for the Hausdorff topology. This important distinction between the final topology and the metric topologies that we can define on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
will be at the heart of
Section

6
.

1.4
The canonical stratification

Now that we have topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
we can discuss continuous map to and from Ran spaces. The first one we are interested in is the canonical stratification on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
given by the cardinality function. Let us first recall what we mean by a stratification.

Definition 1.20
.

Let
P
be a partially ordered set. It can be equipped with a topology, usually called the Alexandrov topology, whose opens are exactly those sets
U\subset P
satisfying the property

\left(p\in U\text{ and }q\geq p\right)\Rightarrow q\in U

A stratification on a topological space
T
is the data of a poset
P
together with a map
f\colon T\to P
which is continuous for the Alexandrov topology. Given such a structure, we may say that
T
is a stratified space, or that
T
is stratified over the poset
P
.
Given any
p\in P
, the preimage
f^{-1}(p)\subset T
will be called the
p
stratum of
T
.

In the case of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
the stratification we will be interested in is given by the poset of positive integers,
\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
, together with the map
\card\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
sending a configuration
X\subset M
to its cardinality. In that example, the open sets of
\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
can be seen to be exactly the sets of the form
\mathbb{N}_{\geq p}=\{n\in\mathbb{N}\mid n\geq p\}
, for all
p\in\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
.

We may now precisely state in what way
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a stratified space.

Proposition 1.21
.

The cardinality map
\card\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
is continuous on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
and thus also on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. In particular, they both are stratified spaces.

Proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let us show that
\card^{-1}(\mathbb{N}_{\geq n})=\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n-1}(M)
is open for
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
. If
n=1
, there is nothing to prove, otherwise, let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n-1}(M)
. We have
\card(X)\geq n>1
, so that we can define
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}\min_{x\neq y\in X}\{d(x,y)\}>0
. Let us show that
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n-1}(M)
. Let
Y\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\epsilon)
. For any
x\in X
, there exists
y_{x}\in Y
such that

\displaystyle d(x,y_{x})=d(x,Y)\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)<\epsilon.

Moreover, if
x^{\prime}\neq x\in X
, we have

\displaystyle d(x^{\prime},y_{x})\geq d(x^{\prime},x)-d(x,y_{x})>\min_{z\neq z^{\prime}\in X}\{d(z,z^{\prime})\}-\epsilon=\epsilon,

and thus
y_{x}\neq y_{x^{\prime}}
. As a result
\card(Y)\geq\card(X)
, so that
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n-1}(M)
and thus
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)\setminus\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n-1}(M)
, which concludes this proof.
∎

Remark 1.22
.

Since the weighted topologies we will introduce in
Section

2
also refine the Hausdorff topology, it will also be the case that the corresponding
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
are stratified spaces.

Remark 1.23
.

The configuration spaces
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
are the strata of the stratified space
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
. Note that the topology on these strata is the same whether we consider the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
or
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

We also deduce from the previous proposition the following result

Corollary 1.24
.

For any
n\geq 1
, the truncation
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
is closed in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

We will discuss in much more detail the properties of those stratifications in
Section

7
, but for now, let us detail some properties of the truncations.

1.5
Truncations of the Ran space

Even though the Hausdorff and final topologies are quite different in general, they happen to coincide on the truncations of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, which is given by the following proposition.

Proposition 1.25
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. The subspace topologies induced by
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
on
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
coincide.

Proof.

Since
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
refines
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
, it is enough to show that open subsets of
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
restricts to open subsets of the subspace topology induced by
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
on any truncation.
Let
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be an open in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and let us show that
U_{\leq n}=U\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
is open for the topology induced by
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
on
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. Let
X\in U_{\leq n}
, and
r=\min(\{\frac{d(x,x^{\prime})}{2}\mid x\neq x^{\prime}\in X\}\cup\{1\})
. By definition of
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
,
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
is open in
M^{n}
, so that, for any
\underline{x}\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(X)
, there exists
0<\epsilon_{\underline{x}}\leq r
such that
B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\epsilon_{\underline{x}})\subset\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
. Now let us define
\epsilon=\min\{\epsilon_{\underline{x}}\mid\underline{x}\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(X)\}
and let us show that
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\epsilon}{n})\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\subset U_{\leq n}
.

Let
Y\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\epsilon}{n})\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, and let
\underline{y}=(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)})\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(Y)
. For all
1,\leq i\leq n
there exists
x^{(i)}\in X
such that
d(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})=d(X,y^{(i)})\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)
. Now observe that, since
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)<r
,
x^{(i)}
must be unique. Indeed if
x\neq x^{(i)}\in X
then

\displaystyle d(x,y^{(i)})\geq d(x,x^{(i)})-d(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\geq d(x,x^{(i)})-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)>r>d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y).

Besides, remark that, for any
x\in X
, there exists
y\in Y
such that
d(x,y)\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)
, so that
x=x^{(i)}
for some
1\leq i\leq n
, and thus
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(X)
. Finally, we have

\displaystyle d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{y})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\leq nd_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)<\epsilon,

so that
\underline{y}\in B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\epsilon)\subset\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
and thus
Y=\pi_{n}(\underline{y})\in U_{\leq n}
which proves that
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\epsilon}{n})\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)\subset U_{\leq n}
and thus that
U_{\leq n}
is open for the topology induced by
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
on
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
.
∎

Remark 1.26
.

This result illustrates that most of the interesting subtleties appear only when the cardinality of configurations tends to infinity. In particular, as seen in
1.12
sequences of unbounded cardinality cannot converge for the final topology but may do so for the Hausdorff topology. This intuitively explains why the comparison of different topologies will often involve sequences of increasing cardinality in the following.

Finally, we will need, in
Section

6
, the following result to discuss the completeness of the different topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Proposition 1.27
.

Let
M
be a complete metric space, and let
n\geq 1
. The truncation
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
equipped with the Hausdorff distance is a complete metric space.

Proof.

Let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
.
Since
(\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M),d_{\mathrm{H}})
is complete
[
15
, Theorem 2.5.2, p72]
, there exists a compact subset
X\subset M
such that
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X)
goes to zero.
Let us now show that
\card{X}\leq n
. By contradiction, suppose that
\card(X)\geq n+1
. But then, there exists
Y\subset X
such that
\card(Y)=n+1
, and, by
1.28
, there exists
\delta_{Y}>0
such that, for any
k\geq 0

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X)

\displaystyle\geq\sup_{y\in X}\{d(X_{k},y)\}

\displaystyle\geq\max_{y\in Y}\{d(X_{k},y)\}

\displaystyle\geq\delta_{Y}

which is absurd since
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X)
converges to zero. We finally deduce that
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
X
in
(\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
.
∎

Lemma 1.28
.

Let
n\geq 1
and let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
such that
\card(X)=n+1
. Then there exists
\delta_{X}
such that, for any
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{x\in X}\{d(Y,x)\}\geq\delta_{X}.

Proof.

We set
\delta_{X}=\frac{1}{2}\min_{x\neq x^{\prime}\in X}\{d(x,x^{\prime})\}>0
. Let
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. For any
x\in X
there exists
y_{x}\in Y
such that
d(y_{x},x)=d(Y,x)
. Since
\card(Y)<\card(X)
there exists
x\neq z\in X
such that
y_{x}=y_{z}
. But then
d(x,y_{x})+d(y_{z},z)\geq d(x,z)\geq 2\delta_{X}
. Hence

\displaystyle\delta_{X}\leq\max\{d(x,y_{x}),d(z,y_{z})\}\leq\max_{w\in X}\{d(Y,w)\}.

∎

Remark 1.29
.

Even though all its truncations are complete,
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
equipped with the Hausdorff distance is not, in general, complete. We give later in
4.5
an example of a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
which is Cauchy for the Hausdorff distance, and that converges to a Cantor set in
\mathcal{P}(M)
.

1.6
Length structures and length spaces

We recall the notions of length structures and length spaces from metric geometry. We follow
[
9
, chapter 2]
, in which numerous examples can be found.

Definition 1.30
.

Given a topological space
M
, a
length structure
on
M
is the data of

•

a set of admissible paths
\mathcal{P}
, whose elements are continuous maps
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to M
, with
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
,

•

a map
\ell\colon\mathcal{P}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

such that, for any continuous path
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to M
, we have

•

given any
c\in[a,b]
, we have

\gamma\in\mathcal{P}\Leftrightarrow\gamma_{|[a,c]}\in\mathcal{P}\text{ and }\gamma_{|[c,b]}\in\mathcal{P},

and in that case,

\ell(\gamma)=\ell(\gamma_{|[a,c]})+\ell(\gamma_{|[c,b]})

•

given any affine homeomorphism
\varphi\colon[c,d]\to[a,b]
, we have

\gamma\in\mathcal{P}\Leftrightarrow\gamma\circ\varphi\in\mathcal{P}

and in that case
\ell(\gamma)=\ell(\gamma\circ\varphi)

•

Given
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
in
\mathcal{P}
, the following map is continuous

\displaystyle[a,b]

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\ell(\gamma_{|[a,t]})

•

Given
x\in X
, and
U\subset X
a neighborhood of
X
, there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that, for any
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to X
in
\mathcal{P}
such that
\gamma(a)=x
and
\gamma(b)\not\in U
,
\ell(\gamma)>\epsilon
.

Example 1.31
.

If
M
is a Riemmanian manifold, then
\mathcal{P}
can be taken to be the set of piecewise
C^{1}
paths, and
\ell(\gamma)
is the integral of the norm of the derivative along the path (i.e. its length in the usual sense).

Definition 1.32
.

Let
M
be a Hausdorff topological space equipped with a length structure
\mathcal{P}
,
\ell
, then we define the distance

d^{\ell}(x,y)=\inf\{\ell(\gamma)\mid\gamma\in\mathcal{P},\ x,y\in\Ima(\gamma)\}

Remark 1.33
.

The fact that
d^{\ell}
is a distance, instead of a pseudo-distance is guaranteed by the last condition in
1.30
, which will further impose that the metric topology refines the topology we started with. Conversely, if we drop this last condition, and assume that
d^{\ell}
is indeed a distance, and that it refines the topology on
M
we started with, we can recover this condition as follows. Observe that by hypothesis, any neighborhood of
x
must contain an open ball of some radius
\epsilon>0
around
x
for the distance
d^{\ell}
, but this immediately implies the condition, since any path leaving such a ball must have length at least
\epsilon
. We will use this observation in
Section

3
to define a length structure on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Definition 1.34
.

A
length space
is a topological space
M
equipped with a length structure
\mathcal{P}
,
\ell
, such that
d^{\ell}
is a distance which induces the topology of
M
. In that case, we say that
d^{\ell}
is a length metric.

Example 1.35
.

Any Riemannian manifold is a length space for the length structure outlined in the previous example, since the topology induced by the Riemannian metric is indeed the underlying topology of the manifold.

Definition 1.36
.

Let
M
be a topological space equipped with a length structure,
\mathcal{P}
,
\ell
. For
n\geq 0
, write
\mathcal{P}^{n}=\{(\gamma^{(1)},\dots,\gamma^{(n)})\colon[a,b]\to M^{n}\mid\gamma_{i}\in\mathcal{P},\ 1\leq i\leq n\}
and
\ell^{n}(\gamma^{(1)},\dots,\gamma^{(n)})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ell(\gamma^{(i)})
.

We immediately get, from the definition, the following proposition, which will be the starting point of
Section

3
.

Proposition 1.37
.

Let
M
be a length space, with length structure
\mathcal{P}
and
\ell
. Let
n\geq 1
, then
M^{n}
is a length space for the length structure
\mathcal{P}^{n}
and
\ell^{n}
. We write
d_{\Sigma}=d^{\ell^{n}}
and remark that

d_{\Sigma}((x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}),(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)}))=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d^{\ell}(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})

2
Combinatorial distance

In this section we define new distances on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
indexed by some non-decreasing sequence
\omega\colon\mathbb{N}\to[1,\infty)
that we call a weight. First we define surjective relations between two configurations in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, to which we attribute a weighted length
\ell^{\omega}
. Then we define combinatorial chains between two configurations as the concatenation of surjective relations, to which we also attribute a weighted length by summing the weighted length of the relations. Finally the weighted distance between two configurations is defined as the infimum of the lengths of combinatorial chains between the two configurations. We show that this indeed defines a distance on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and then, the remaining of the section is dedicated to refining the computation of the distance. Eventually we show in
2.16
that we obtain the same infimum by restricting to chains of a specific shape, that we call MBS (merge-bijection-splitting), see
2.14
, where we first merge points together, then possibly move them around and finally split the points. This result allows us to show in
2.24
that, if
M
satisfies the Heine-Borel property, then this infimum is computed on a compact space, and is thus attained for a given chain that we call geodesic.

Throughout,
(M,d)
is a metric space.

2.1
Definitions

In this section, we define the weighted distance on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and show that it is a distance. First we define the combinatorial notions that will be central in the following of this work: surjective relations and combinatorial chains.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations. A
relation
between
X
and
Y
is the data of a subset
R\subset X\times Y
. We will write
xRy
for
(x,y)\in R
. It is said to be
surjective
, if

•

\forall x\in X
,
\exists y\in Y
, such that
xRy
, and

•

\forall y\in Y
,
\exists x\in X
, such that
xRy
.

The
cardinality
of the relation
R
, denoted
\card(R)
, is simply the cardinality of the set
R\subset X\times Y
. We will write
R^{\mathrm{op}}\subset Y\times X
for the relation defined by
yR^{\mathrm{op}}x\Leftrightarrow xRy
.

Definition 2.2
.

Given an integer
k\geq 0
, a
combinatorial chain
in
M
(with
k
terms) is the data of

•

for all
0\leq i\leq k
a configuration
X_{i}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,

•

for all
0\leq j\leq k-1
, a surjective relation
R_{j}
between
X_{j}
and
X_{j+1}
.

We will write such a chain
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
, or simply
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
. And we will say that it is a chain between
X_{0}
and
X_{k}
, or from
X_{0}
to
X_{k}
.

We can now assign very naturally a
length
to relations and combinatorial chains, as if they were some sort of generalized "paths", but with their length computed "as the crow flies".

Definition 2.3
.

Given a relation
R
between
X
and
Y
in
M
, we define the
length
of the relation
R
as

\ell(R)=\sum\limits_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y).

Given
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
, a combinatorial chain on
M
, its length is defined as

\ell(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\ell(R_{j})

Recall that, as explained in the introduction when discussing possible answers to
1
, we want some way to modulate our distances depending on the cardinality of the configurations. This is achieved by weighting the length by a cardinality-dependent weight.

Definition 2.4
.

A
weight
is a sequence
\omega\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[1,\infty)
, such that

•

\omega(1)=1
,

•

\omega
is non-decreasing.

Definition 2.5
.

Given a relation
R
between
X
and
Y
in
M
, and a weight
\omega
, we define the
length
of the relation
R
,
relative to
\omega
as

\ell^{\omega}(R)=\omega(\card(R))\ell(R).

Given
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
, a combinatorial chain on
M
, its length relative to
\omega
is defined as

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}\ell^{\omega}(R_{j})

Remark 2.6
.

If a relation
R\subset X\times Y
is the graph of a map
f\colon X\to Y
, then
2.5
gives

\ell^{\omega}(R)=\ell^{\omega}(f)=\omega(\card(X))\sum_{x\in X}d(x,f(x)).

Finally, the weighted distance is computed, quite intuitively, as the weighted length of the "shortest generalized path" between configurations.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations, and
\omega
some weight. We define the
weighted distance
between
X
and
Y
as the following infimum

d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\inf\{\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\}

Where the
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
ranges over all combinatorial chains between
X
and
Y
.

There remains to be proven that this indeed defines a distance on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Proposition 2.8
.

Given some weight
\omega
, the combinatorial distance
d^{\omega}
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a distance.

Proof.

Let
X
,
Y
and
Z
be three configurations in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

•

Given
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
, a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
,
(R_{k-1},\dots,R_{0})
is a combinatorial chain between
Y
and
X
, with
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=\ell^{\omega}(R_{k-1},\dots,R_{0})
. Hence,
d^{\omega}(X,Y)=d^{\omega}(Y,X)
.

•

The identity relation on
X
gives
d^{\omega}(X,X)=0
. Conversely, assume that
X\not=Y
. By symmetry, assume that there exists
x\in X
such that
x\not\in Y
, let
y\in Y
be such that
d(x,y)\leq d(x,y^{\prime})
for any
y^{\prime}\in Y
. Consider a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
,
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
. Surjectivity of each of the
R_{j}
implies the existence of a sequence
x_{0},\dots,x_{k}
such that
x=x_{0}
,
x_{i}\in X_{i}
, and
x_{i-1}R_{i-1}x_{i}
, for
1\leq i\leq k
. But then,
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\geq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}d(x_{i},x_{i+1})\geq d(x,x_{k})\geq d(x,y)
. Hence,
d^{\omega}(X,Y)\geq d(x,y)>0
, which proves that
d^{\omega}(X,Y)=0\Leftrightarrow X=Y
.

•

Finally, given two combinatorial chains
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
and
(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
between
X
and
Y
and between
Y
and
Z
respectively, the concatenation
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1},P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
is a chain between
X
and
Z
of length

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1},P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})+\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})

Hence,
d^{\omega}(X,Z)\leq d^{\omega}(Y,Z)+d^{\omega}(X,Y)
. Which concludes the proof.

∎

2.2
Refining the computation

We now have a definition for the weighted distance in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. However, its definition is, at the same time, very general, but very impractical, so that the distance is difficult to evaluate, or manipulate. Since it is defined as an infimum, finding a "relevant" upper bound is as easy as finding a "relevant" combinatorial chain. On the contrary, finding a relevant lower bound can be much harder, since the set of combinatorial chains between two configurations is quite large. Over the next two subsections, we will show that one may actually restrict the computation of the infimum to very specific combinatorial chains, (see
2.16
). Since we want to reduce the set of combinatorial chains that we need to consider, the strategy is always to construct, from a surjective relation or a combinatorial chain between two configurations, another, shorter, surjective relation or combinatorial chain between the same configurations, and with better properties. In particular, we want the "good" chains to be decomposed into relations that either merge points together, and reduce cardinality, or that do the opposite. These relations are either the graph of a surjective map, or their opposite is the graph of a surjective map. In addition, we also want to control the cardinality of the relations, this is thus another central aspect in the following.

For the rest of this section, we fix
\omega
a weight.

We first show a quite intuitive lemma that will be useful throughout this article.

Lemma 2.9
.

Let
R\subset X\times Y
be a surjective relation between two configurations
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. If
R^{\prime}\subset R
is a surjective relation between
X^{\prime}\subset X
and
Y^{\prime}\subset Y
then
\ell^{\omega}(R^{\prime})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)
.

Proof.

Since
R^{\prime}\subset R
, we have
\card(R^{\prime})\leq\card(R)
. So that we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\omega(\card(R^{\prime}))\sum_{(x,y)\in R^{\prime}}d(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(R))\sum_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R).

∎

Remark 2.10
.

This result being very intuitive, we will most often omit the explicit call to this lemma.

We then show that we can get an upper bound for the cardinality of the relations of the considered chains.

Lemma 2.11
.

Let
R\subset X\times Y
be a surjective relation between two configurations
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. If
\card(R)>\max\{\card(X),\card(Y)\}
, then there exists a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
,
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}),(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1}))
, such that

•

\card(P_{j})<\card(R)
,
0\leq j\leq k-1
,

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)
,

•

\card(Y_{i})\leq\max(\card(X),\card(Y))
, for all
0\leq i\leq k
.

Proof.

By assumption, since
\card(R)>\card(X)
,
there must exist
x_{0}\in X
and
y_{1}\not=y_{2}\in Y
such that
x_{0}Ry_{1}
and
x_{0}Ry_{2}
.
Similarly, since
\card(R)>\card(Y)
,
there must also exist
x_{1}\not=x_{2}\in X
and
y_{0}\in Y

such that
x_{1}Ry_{0}
and
x_{2}Ry_{0}
. By symmetry, we distinguish three cases :

•

If
x_{0}=x_{1}
and
y_{0}=y_{1}
, then
P=R\setminus\{(x_{0},y_{0})\}
is still a surjective relation, since
x_{1}Py_{2}
and
x_{2}Py_{1}
, and we can interpret it as a combinatorial chain with a single relation. Furthermore, by construction
\card(P)=\card(R)-1<\card(R)
, and, since
P\subset R
, we have, by
2.9
,
\ell^{\omega}(P)\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)
.

•

x_{0}\not=x_{1},x_{2}
and
y_{0}=y_{1}
. This case is similar to the previous one, and
P=R\setminus\{(x_{0},y_{0})\}
fits.

•

x_{0}\not=x_{1},x_{2}
, and
y_{0}\not=y_{1},y_{2}
. We first consider the subcase where
x_{0}\in Y\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
. In that case, surjectivity of
R
implies the existence of some
x_{3}\in X
such that
x_{3}Rx_{0}
. Define
Q\subset X\times Y
as the relation
Q=R\setminus\{(x_{3},x_{0}),(x_{0},y_{2})\}\cup\{(x_{0},x_{0}),(x_{3},y_{2})\}
. Note that
Q
is still a surjective relation, by construction, and that we have
\card(Q)\leq\card(R)
, hence

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(Q)

\displaystyle=\omega(\card(Q))\sum_{(x,y)\in Q}d(x,y)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Q))\left(\left(\sum_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y)\right)+d(x_{3},y_{2})-d(x_{3},x_{0})-d(x_{0},y_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Q))\left(\sum_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(R))\left(\sum_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)

Now, either
\card(Q)<\card(R)
and we are done, or we reduced to the case where
x_{0}\not\in Y\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
by considering
Q
instead of
R
and
y_{1},x_{0}
instead of
y_{1},y_{2}
.

In the latter case, where
x_{0}\not\in Y\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2}\}
, we may freely assume that there exists no
x_{3}\not=x_{0}\in X
such that
x_{3}Ry_{1}
or
x_{3}Ry_{2}
. Indeed, if such an element of
X
exists, then up to relabelling the corresponding
y_{i}
by
y_{0}
and
x_{0}
and
x_{3}
as
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
, the situation is covered by the first case. So, we can set
Z=Y\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2}\}\cup\{x_{0}\}
, and define two relations,
P_{0}\subset X\times Z
and
P_{1}\subset Z\times Y
by :

\displaystyle xP_{0}z

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(z\in Y\setminus\{y_{1},y_{2}\}\text{ and }xRz)\text{ or }(x=z=x_{0})

\displaystyle zP_{1}y

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow(z=y)\text{ or }\left((z=x_{0})\text{ and }(y=y_{1}\text{ or }y=y_{2})\right)

Note that, by construction,
\card(Z)\leq\card(Y)
,
and that
\card(P_{0})\leq\card(R)-1
, and
\card(P_{1})=\card(Y)<\card(R)
. By the above remark,
P_{0}
is still a surjective relation, and we compute

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},P_{1})

\displaystyle=\omega(\card(P_{0}))\left(\sum_{(x,z)\in P_{0}}d(x,z)\right)+\omega(\card(P_{1}))\left(\sum_{(z,y)\in P_{1}}d(z,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(R))\left(\sum_{(x,z)\in P_{0}}d(x,z)\right)+\omega(\card(R))\left(d(x_{0},y_{1})+d(x_{0},y_{2})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(R))\left(\sum_{(x,y)\in R}d(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R).

This concludes the proof. See
Fig.

1
, for an illustration.

∎

x_{1}=x_{0}
x_{2}
y_{2}
y_{1}=y_{0}
x_{1}
x_{2}
y_{2}
y_{1}=y_{0}
x_{0}
x_{0}
x_{3}
x_{2}
