y_{0}
y_{0}

Figure 1:
An illustration of the proof of Lemma
2.11
. The top two pictures represent the first two cases, where the dashed line is the pair
(x,y)
to be deleted in
R
to obtain
P
. The second line represent the passage from
R
to
Q
in the particular case where
x_{0}\in Y
. On the last line, the left part is the relation
R
, and the right part is the concatenation of
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
.

We deduce that we can indeed decompose the considered chains into relations that either merge points together, or that split some points, while keeping a control over the cardinality of the intermediate configurations of the chain.

Corollary 2.12
.

Let
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
be a combinatorial chain, and
\omega
be a weight. There exists a combinatorial chain
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{l}),(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1}))
such that:

•

Y_{0}=X_{0}
and
Y_{l}=X_{k}
,

•

For all
0\leq i\leq l-1
, either
P_{i}
or
P_{i}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the graph of a surjective map,

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
,

•

\card(Y_{i})\leq\max\{\card(X_{j})\mid 0\leq j\leq k\}
for all
0\leq i\leq l-1
.

Proof.

It is sufficient to prove the case where
k=1
,
since the general result will be obtained by concatenation, so let
R\subset X\times Y

be a surjective relation,
n=\max(\card(X),\card(Y))
. We proceed by induction on
n\geq 1
. If
n=1
, then
X
,
Y
and
R
are all singletons, and
R
must be already be the graph of a bijection and there is nothing to prove.

Let us suppose the result known for all value
\leq n_{0}
and let suppose
n=n_{0}+1
.
We prove the induction step on
n
by induction on
m=\card(R)
. Since
R
must be surjective, we have
\card(R)=m\geq n
. If
m=n
, then either
\card(R)=\card(X)
or
\card(R)=\card(Y)
. In the first case,
R
is the graph of a map (which is necessarily surjective), and in the later
R^{\mathrm{op}}
is. In any case, there is nothing to prove.
Now let us assume that the result is known for all integers
\leq m_{0}
, and that
m=m_{0}+1>n
.
Then by
2.11
, there exists a combinatorial chain
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{l}),(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1}))
such that

•

\card(P_{j})<\card(R)
for all
0\leq j\leq l-1
,

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)
,

•

Y_{0}=X
,
Y_{l}=Y
,

•

\card(Y_{i})\leq\max(\card(X),\card(Y))

But then, since each
P_{i}
has cardinality strictly smaller than
m
, and each
Y_{i}
has cardinality smaller that
n
applying both induction hypotheses gives the desired result.
∎

2.3
Merges, bijections and splitting

In this subsection, we continue to restrict the set of combinatorial chains over which we compute the weighted distance, to arrive at the result of
2.16
. We start by formalizing properly the relations that we want in our "good" combinatorial chains: merges, splittings and bijections.

Definition 2.13
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations, and
R\subset X\times Y
a surjective relation between
X
and
Y
. The relation
R
is said to be :

•

a
merge
(from
X
to
Y
) if
R
is the graph of a map
f\colon X\to Y
such that

–

there exists
y_{0}\in Y
such that
\card(f^{-1}(y_{0}))\geq 2
,

–

for all
y\not=y_{0}
,
f^{-1}(y)=\{y\}\subset X
.

The merge is said to be
simple
if
\card(f^{-1}(y_{0}))=2
. In that case, we will say that
R
is the merge of
f^{-1}(y_{0})
at
y_{0}
.

•

a
bijection
(from
X
to
Y
) if
R
is the graph of a bijection
\phi\colon X\to Y
.

•

a
splitting
(from
X
to
Y
) if
R^{\mathrm{op}}
is a merge (from
Y
to
X
). It is said to be a
simple
splitting, if
R^{\mathrm{op}}
is a simple merge.

We can finally define the specific shape that we want for the combinatorial chains.

Definition 2.14
.

Let
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
be a combinatorial chain. We say that it is a
Merge-Bijection-Splitting-chain
(or
MBS-chain
) if there exists
0\leq l\leq k-1
such that

•

R_{i}
is a merge, for
0\leq i\leq l-1
,

•

R_{l}
is a bijection,

•

R_{j}
is a splitting, for
l+1\leq j\leq k-1

or if there exists
0\leq l\leq k
such that

•

R_{i}
is a merge, for
0\leq i\leq l-1
,

•

R_{j}
is a splitting, for
l\leq j\leq k-1

It is said to be a
simple
MBS-chain if in addition each of the merges and splittings are simple.

Remark 2.15
.

We may always assume that a MBS-chain is of the first form, by adding the identity bijection to a chain of the second form, since this will not change its
\omega
-length. Thus, in the following proofs, we may implicitly assume that we are in the first case without loss of generality.

We will now spend the rest of the section to prove the following theorem.

Theorem 2.16
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations, and
\omega
a weight. Then, the combinatorial distance can be computed as

d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\inf\{\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\}

where the infimum is computed on simple MBS-chains between
X
and
Y
.

We still need some extra lemmas in order to complete the proof. Note that all of them are symmetric in terms of merge/splitting, so that we only prove them for merges. First we prove that we can decompose a relation that "merges" points together into an chain of actual merges, followed by a bijection.

Lemma 2.17
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations, and
R
be the graph of a surjective map
f\colon X\to Y
. Then, there exists a combinatorial chain
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
from
X
to
Y
such that

•

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)
,

•

R_{i}
is a merge, whenever
0\leq i\leq k-2
,

•

R_{k-1}
is a bijection.

Proof.

Consider the following partitions of
X
and
Y
:

\displaystyle Y_{-}=\{y\in Y\mid\card(f^{-1}(y))=1\},

\displaystyle X_{-}=f^{-1}(Y_{-}),

\displaystyle Y_{+}=\{y\in Y\mid\card(f^{-1}(y))>1\},

\displaystyle X_{+}=f^{-1}(Y_{+}).

The map
f
restricts to a (possibly empty) bijection
f_{-}\colon X_{-}\to Y_{-}
and a nowhere injective, surjective map
f_{+}\colon X_{+}\to Y_{+}
. We will show the claim by induction on
\card(Y_{+})
.

If
\card(Y_{+})=0
, then
f
is a bijection, and there is nothing to prove. Now, let
l\geq 0
, be such that the result is known whenever
\card(Y_{+})\leq l
. Assume
\card(Y_{+})=l+1
, and fix some
y\in Y_{+}
. We distinguish two cases.

•

If
y\not\in X\setminus f^{-1}(y)
, define
\widetilde{X}=X\setminus f^{-1}(y)\cup\{y\}
, together with a map
f_{0}\colon X\to\widetilde{X}
such that
f_{0}(x)=x
for all
x\not\in f^{-1}(y)
and
f_{0}(x)=y
for all
x\in f^{-1}(y)
. By construction,
R_{0}
, the graph of
f_{0}
is a merge, and we have

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})=\omega(\card(X))\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}d(x,y)

On the other hand, we define
\widetilde{f}\colon\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{Y}=Y
via
\widetilde{f}(x)=f(x)
for
x\not=y
, and
\widetilde{f}(y)=y
, and we have for
\widetilde{R}
the graph of
\widetilde{f}
,

\ell^{\omega}(\widetilde{R})=\omega(\card(\widetilde{X}))\sum_{x\in X\setminus f^{-1}(y)}d(x,f(x))

Now, just as before, define
\widetilde{Y}_{+}\subset\widetilde{Y}
to be the subset of points at which
\widetilde{f}
is non-injective, and observe that
\widetilde{Y}_{+}=Y_{+}\setminus\{y\}
, thus
\card(\widetilde{Y}_{+})\leq l
, and we can apply the induction hypothesis to produce a combinatorial chain
(R_{1},\dots,R_{k-1})
of the desired form, and satisfying

\ell^{\omega}(R_{1},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(\widetilde{R})

Now, the concatenation
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
is of the desired form, and we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(R_{1},\dots,R_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(\widetilde{R})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}d(x,y)+\omega(\card(\widetilde{X}))\sum_{x\in X\setminus f^{-1}(y)}d(x,f(x))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))\sum_{x\in X}d(x,f(x))

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)

Which concludes the proof in this case.

•

If
y\in X\setminus f^{-1}(y)
, choose some
z\in f^{-1}(y)
, and define
\widetilde{X}=X\setminus f^{-1}(y)\cup\{z\}
, together with a map
f_{0}\colon X\to\widetilde{X}
which satisfies
f_{0}(x)=y
if
x\in f^{-1}(y)\setminus z
and
f_{0}(x)=x
otherwise. Observe that
f_{0}
is a merge, since
f_{0}^{-1}(y)=f^{-1}(y)\cup\{y\}\setminus\{z\}
is of cardinality at least
2
. Now, let
\widetilde{f}\colon\widetilde{X}\to\widetilde{Y}=Y
be the map defined by
\widetilde{f}(x)=f(x)
for all
x\in\widetilde{X}
. Observe that
\widetilde{Y}_{+}=Y_{+}\setminus\{y\}
, since
\widetilde{f}
is injective at
y
, and we can thus apply the induction hypothesis to
\widetilde{f}
. Furthermore, observe that we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(f_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(\widetilde{f})

\displaystyle=\omega(\card(X))\left(\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)\setminus\{z\}}d(x,y)\right)+\omega(\card(\widetilde{X}))\left(\sum_{x\in X\setminus f^{-1}(y)\cup\{z\}}d(x,f(x))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))\sum_{x\in X}d(x,f(x))

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(f)

This gives the induction step.

∎

Then we prove that we can essentially change the order of merges and bijections to always start with the merges, and end with a bijection.

Lemma 2.18
.

Let
((X,Y,Z),(R_{0},R_{1}))
be a combinatorial chain where
R_{0}
is a bijection and
R_{1}
is a merge, then there exists a combinatorial chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1})
between
X
and
Z
such that

•

P_{i}
is a merge, for all
0\leq i<k-1
,

•

P_{k-1}
is a bijection,

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},R_{1})

Proof.

Since
R_{0}
is the graph of a bijective map
f_{0}\colon X\to Y
and
R_{1}
is the graph of a surjective map
f_{1}\colon Y\to Z
, we may consider the composition
f_{1}\circ f_{0}
, whose graph we will denote
R
. It is a surjection, by construction, and since
f_{0}
is a bijection, we have
\card(R)=\card(X)=\card(R_{0})=\card(Y)=\card(R_{1})
. Thus, we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R)

\displaystyle=\omega(\card(X))\sum_{(x,z)\in R}d(x,z)

\displaystyle=\omega(\card(X))\sum_{(x,y)\in R_{0}}d(x,f_{1}(y))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))\sum_{(x,y)\in R_{0}}\left(d(x,y)+d(y,f_{1}(y))\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))\left(\sum_{(x,y)\in R_{0}}d(x,y)+\sum_{(y,z)\in R_{1}}d(y,z)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},R_{1})

We conclude by applying
2.17
to
R
.
∎

By iterating this lemma, we can get the following corollary for an arbitrary number of merges and bijections.

Corollary 2.19
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations and
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
be a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
, such that
R_{i}
is a bijection or a merge for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
. Then there exists a chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
between
X
and
Y
such that

•

P_{i}
is a merge, for all
0\leq i<l-1
,

•

P_{l-1}
is a bijection,

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

Proof.

We just need to iterate
2.18
to get a shorter combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
that starts with merges, and ends with bijections. But then, composing all the bijections into one gives the desired result.
∎

Now we also need to switch the order of merges and splitting, this is the object of the following lemma.

Lemma 2.20
.

Let
((X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}),(R_{0},R_{1}))
be a combinatorial chain where
R_{0}
is a splitting, and
R_{1}
is a merge, then there exists a combinatorial chain
((X_{0},Y_{1},X_{2}),(P_{0},P_{1}))
such that
P_{0}
is either a merge or a bijection,
P_{1}
is either a bijection or a splitting, and
\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},P_{1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},R_{1})
.

Proof.

Since
R_{0}
is a splitting, its opposite
R_{0}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the graph of a map
f_{0}\colon X_{1}\to X_{0}
, such that there exists
x_{0}\in X_{0}
such that
X_{1}^{\prime}=f_{0}^{-1}(\{x_{0}\})
has a cardinality
>1
and
f_{0}
restricts to the identity on
X_{1}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime}
. Symmetrically,
R_{1}
is the graph of a map
f_{1}\colon X_{1}\to X_{2}
, and there is a point
x_{2}\in X_{2}
such that
X_{1}^{\prime\prime}=f_{1}^{-1}(\{x_{2}\})
has a cardinality
>1
and
f_{1}
restricts to the identity on
X_{1}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}
. Remark that we thus have
\card(R_{0})=\card(R_{1})=\card(X_{1})\geq\card(X_{0}),\card(X_{2})

Now, we distinguish three cases

•

If
X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\subset X_{1}^{\prime}
, then observe that we can define a surjective relation
P_{1}\subset X_{0}\times X_{2}
as follows

xP_{1}y\iff(x=x_{0}\text{ and }(y\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\text{ or }y=x_{2}))\text{ or }(x\in X_{0}\setminus\{x_{0}\}\text{ and }x=y)

and that it is either a bijection or a splitting, so that
\card(P_{1})=\card(X_{2})\leq\card(X_{1})
. We can thus define
P_{0}
as the graph of
\mathrm{Id}_{X_{0}}
and
((X_{0},X_{0},X_{2}),(P_{0},P_{1}))
is of the desired form. Moreover

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},P_{1})=\ell^{\omega}(P_{1})=

\displaystyle\card(P_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d(x_{0},y)+\card(P_{1})d(x_{0},x_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d(x_{0},y)+\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d(x_{0},x_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d(x_{0},y)+\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}(d(x_{0},y)+d(y,x_{2}))

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime}}d(x_{0},y)+\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d(y,x_{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},R_{1})

•

If
X_{1}^{\prime}\subset X_{1}^{\prime\prime}
the case is the symmetric of the preceding one.

•

Otherwise there exists
y_{0}\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime}\subset X_{0}\cap X_{1}
and
y_{2}\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\subset X_{1}\cap X_{2}
. Remark that we must have
y_{0}\neq x_{0}
because if
x_{0}\in X_{1}
then we must have
x_{0}\in X_{1}^{\prime}
. Symmetrically we have
y_{2}\neq x_{2}
. We can thus define
Y_{1}=X_{1}\setminus\left(X_{1}^{\prime}\cup X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\right)\cup\{x_{0},x_{2}\}
and
P_{0}
as the graph of

\displaystyle g_{0}\colon X_{0}

\displaystyle\to Y_{1}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}x_{2}\quad&\text{if }x\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{x_{0}\}\\
x&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Observe that
P_{0}
is surjective: if
y\in Y_{1}
, then either
y=x_{0}
and
g_{0}(y)=y
, or
y=x_{2}
and
g_{0}(y_{0})=y
or
y\in Y_{1}\setminus\{x_{0},x_{2}\}\subset X_{1}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime}
, so that
y=f_{0}(y)\in X_{0}
. In the latter case, we have furthermore,
y\in Y_{1}\setminus\{x_{0},x_{2}\}\subset X_{1}\setminus X_{1}^{\prime\prime}
so that
y=f_{0}(y)\in X_{0}\setminus X^{\prime\prime}_{1}
and finally
g_{0}(y)=y
. It is indeed a merge or a bijection, so that
\card(P_{0})=\card(X_{0})
. Symmetrically, we can define
P_{1}
such that
P_{1}^{\mathrm{op}}
is the graph of

\displaystyle g_{1}\colon X_{2}

\displaystyle\to Y_{1}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}x_{0}\quad&\text{if }x\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus\{x_{2}\}\\
x&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

so that
P_{1}
is a splitting or a bijection and
\card(P_{1})=\card(X_{2})
. Finally

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},P_{1})

\displaystyle=\card(X_{0})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}\setminus\{x_{0}\}}d(y,x_{2})+\card(X_{2})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime}\setminus\{x_{2}\}}d(x_{0},y)

\displaystyle\leq\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime\prime}}d(y,x_{2})+\card(X_{1})\sum_{y\in X_{1}^{\prime}}d(x_{0},y)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},R_{1})

∎

We can now iterate
2.18
and
2.20
to get the following.

Corollary 2.21
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations and
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
be a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
, such that
R_{i}
is a bijection or a splitting for all
0\leq i\leq k-2
and
R_{k-1}
is a merge. There exists
0\leq m\leq l
and a combinatorial chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
between
X
and
Y
such that

•

P_{i}
is a merge for all
0\leq i\leq m-1

•

P_{i}
is a bijection or a splitting for all
m\leq i\leq l-1

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

Then again, we can iterate
2.21
to get the following corollary.

Corollary 2.22
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations and
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
be a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
such that
R_{i}
is either a merge, a splitting or a bijection for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
. Then there exists
0\leq m\leq l
and a combinatorial chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
between
X
and
Y
such that

•

P_{i}
is a merge for all
0\leq i\leq m-1

•

P_{i}
is a bijection or a splitting for all
m\leq i\leq l-1

•

\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

Finally we show that we can decompose any merge into a chain of simple merges.

Lemma 2.23
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations and
R
a merge from
X
to
Y
. Then, there exists a combinatorial chain from
X
to
Y
,
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
, such that

•

R_{i}
is a simple merge for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
,

•

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\card(X)-\card(Y)
. Since
R
is a merge, we must have
\card(X)>\card(Y)
. Now, if
\card(X)=\card(Y)+1
, then
R
is a simple merge, and there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, let
f\colon X\to Y
be the map whose graph is
R
, and let
y\in Y
be the unique element at which
f
is non-injective.
f^{-1}(y)
is of cardinality
\card(X)-\card(Y)+1\geq 3
. We distinguish two cases.

•

If
y\not\in f^{-1}(y)
, let
x_{1},x_{2}\in f^{-1}(y)
, and define
\widetilde{X}=X\setminus\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\cup\{y\}
, together with a map
f_{0}\colon X\to\widetilde{X}
which satisfies
f_{0}(x_{1})=f_{0}(x_{2})=y
and
f_{0}(x)=x
otherwise. In particular, the graph of
f_{0}
,
R_{0}
, is a simple merge, and satisfies

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})=\omega(\card(X))(d(x_{1},y)+d(x_{2},y)).

On the other hand, define
\widetilde{f}\colon\widetilde{X}\to Y
which satisfies
\widetilde{f}(x)=f(x)
whenever
x\not=y
, and
\widetilde{f}(y)=y
. Then,
\widetilde{R}
, the graph of
\widetilde{f}
is again a merge and satisfies,
\card(\widetilde{X})-\card(Y)=\card(X)-\card(Y)-1
, we may thus apply the induction hypothesis, to produce a chain of simple merges
(R_{1},\dots,R_{k-1})
. Finally, we have a chain of simple merges,
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
, and we compute its length :

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(R_{1},\dots,R_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(\widetilde{R})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))(d(x_{1},y)+d(x_{2},y))+\omega(\card(\widetilde{X}))\left(\sum_{x\in\widetilde{f}^{-1}(y)}d(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(X))\left(\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}d(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R).

Which concludes the proof in this case.

•

if
y\in f^{-1}(y)
, pick some
x_{0}\not=y\in f^{-1}(y)
, and define
\widetilde{X}=X\setminus\{x_{0}\}
. The proof is analogous to the previous case, with the first step being a simple merge between
x_{0}
and
y
.

∎

We are now ready to prove
2.16

Proof of
2.16
.

By combining
2.12
and
2.17
, we see that any combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
, can be shortened by passing to a chain of the form
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
, where
R_{i}
is either a merge, a bijection or a splitting. Then we can apply
2.22
to obtain
0\leq m\leq l
and a shorter combinatorial chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
such that
P_{i}
is a merge for all
0\leq i\leq m
, and a splitting or a bijection for all
m\leq i\leq l-1
. By symmetry of merge and splittings, we can apply
2.19
to
(P_{m},\dots,P_{l-1})
to get a shorter chain
(Q_{m},\dots,Q_{l^{\prime}-1})
such that
Q_{m}
is a bijection and
Q_{i}
is a splitting for all
m<i\leq l^{\prime}-1
. By concatenating we get a MBS-chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{m-1},Q_{m},\dots,Q_{l^{\prime}-1})
between
X
and
Y
. We then conclude by applying
2.23
to all
P_{i}
,
0\leq i\leq m-1
and to all
Q_{i}
,
m<i\leq l^{\prime}-1
to get a shorter simple MBS-chain between
X
and
Y
.
∎

2.4
Existence of geodesic chains

Finally, in this section, we use
2.16
to prove that if
M
satisfies the Heine-Borel property, then the infimum in
2.7
is computed over a compact set and is thus attained for a given MBS-chain. This is the subject of the following theorem.

Theorem 2.24
.

Assume that
M
is a metric space, satisfying the Heine-Borel property (i.e. in which bounded and closed sets are compact). Then, for any configurations
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and any weight
\omega
, there exists a combinatorial chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
between
X
and
Y
such that

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=d^{\omega}(X,Y)

Furthermore, the chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
may be assumed to be a simple MBS-chain, in which case, we call it a
geodesic chain
between
X
and
Y
.

Proof.

By
2.16
, the infimum defining
d^{\omega}(X,Y)
can be computed over simple MBS-chains, thus it is enough to show that there exists a simple MBS-chain between
X
and
Y
with minimal length. Now observe that if
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
is a simple MBS-chain between
X
and
Y
, then there exists some
0\leq m\leq k-1
, such that
\card(R_{i})=\card(R_{i+1})+1
for all
i\leq m-1
, and
\card(R_{i})=\card(R_{i-1})+1
for all
m<i\leq k-1
. In particular, the value of
k
determines
\card(R_{i})
for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
. We deduce that for any simple MBS-chain between
X
and
Y
, we must have
k\leq\card(X)+\card(Y)-1
. This gives

d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\min_{1\leq k\leq\card(X)+\card(Y)-1}\{\inf\{\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\}\}

Thus it is enough to show that for any fixed
k\geq 1
, there is a simple MBS-chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
between
X
and
Y
of minimal length among simple MBS-chains with
k
relations.

Given
k\geq 1
, there is a unique
k
-tuple of integer
(n_{0},\dots,n_{k-1})
satisfying
\card(R_{i})=n_{i},\forall i
, for any simple MBS-chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
between
X
and
Y
. Now, by
2.27
, such a chain can be encoded as the data of a
2k
-tuple
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
satisfying conditions
(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
, and its length can be recovered as

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(n_{i})d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}}).

Thus, if we define
M^{(n_{0},\dots,n_{k-1})}=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}(M^{n_{i}}\times M^{n_{i}})
, we are left with proving that the function
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(n_{i})d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}})
, considered on the subset of
M^{(n_{0},\dots,n_{k-1})}
of
2k
-tuples
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
satisfying conditions
(b)
and
(c)
, admits a minimum. Now, let
\epsilon>0
and set
r=d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\epsilon
. Then, there must exist a simple MBS-chain of
\omega
-length less than
r
between
X
and
Y
. We may assume that there exists one with
k
terms, otherwise there is nothing to prove. But now, by
2.28
if
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
is such a simple MBS-chain, the corresponding
2k
-tuple
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
is such that

(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})\in K=\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\left((\overline{B}_{M}(X,r))^{n_{i}}\times(\overline{B}_{M}(X,r))^{n_{i}}\right)

Finally, observe that
\overline{B}_{M}(X,r)=\cup_{x\in X}\overline{B}_{M}(x,r)
is an union of closed balls. Since we assumed that
M
satisfies the Heine-Borel property,
\overline{B}_{M}(X,r)
is thus a finite union of compact subsets of
M
, thus it is compact, and
K
is compact, as a product of compact. Now, observe that the subset of
K
of
2k
-tuple satisfying conditions
(b)
and
(c)
, call it
K^{\prime}
, is a closed subset (it is the locus of solutions of equations specified by continuous maps), hence it is also compact. Finally, since the function
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})\mapsto\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(n_{i})d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}})
is continuous, and bounded below by zero, it must reach a minimum on the compact
K^{\prime}
. By the converse of
2.27
, this produces the geodesic chain we are looking for.
∎

The proof of the previous theorem proceeds by exhibiting a compact set over which we compute the minimum. Since
M
satisfies the Heine-Borel property, we construct this compact set from closed balls in
M
. We thus translate our optimization problem over the set of configuration chains into an another optimization problem over a set of tuples in a product of
M
.

Definition 2.25
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})
and
\underline{y}=(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)})
be two points in
M^{n}
. Write
\pi_{n}(\underline{x})=X
and
\pi_{n}(\underline{y})=Y
, and define the relation
R_{\underline{x},\underline{y}}\subset X\times Y
as follows. For
(x,y)\in X\times Y
,

xR_{\underline{x},\underline{y}}y\Leftrightarrow\exists i,\ x=x^{(i)}\text{ and }y=y^{(i)}

Lemma 2.26
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations and
R
be a surjective relation between
X
and
Y
. Write
n=\card(R)
. Then, there exists
\underline{x},\underline{y}\in M^{n}
such that

•

\pi_{n}(\underline{x})=X
,

•

\pi_{n}(\underline{y})=Y
,

•

R_{\underline{x},\underline{y}}=R

In that case, we have

\ell^{\omega}(R)=\omega(n)d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{y})=\omega(n)\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})

Proof.

Fix some bijection
\phi\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to R\subset X\times Y
and write
\phi(i)=(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})
. Surjectivity of
R
guarantees that
\pi_{n}(\underline{x})=X
and
\pi_{n}(\underline{y})=Y
. Finally, the last equality follows from the definition of
\ell^{\omega}
.
∎

Lemma 2.27
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations. If
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
is a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
, write
n_{i}=\card(R_{i})
, for
0\leq i\leq k-1
. There exists a
2k
-tuple
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
such that

(a)

\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}}\in M^{n_{i}}
, for all
0\leq i\leq k-1

(b)

\pi_{n_{0}}(\underline{x_{0}})=X
and
\pi_{n_{k-1}}(\underline{x_{2k-1}})=Y

(c)

\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{x_{2i}})=\pi_{n_{i-1}}(\underline{x_{2i-1}})
,
1\leq i\leq k-1

(d)

R_{i}=R_{\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}}}
,
0\leq i\leq k-1
.

(e)

\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(n_{i})d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}})

Conversely, given two tuples
(n_{0},\dots,n_{k-1})
and
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
which verifies conditions
(a)
,
(b)
and
(c)
, there exists a chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
between
X
and
Y
which satisfied
(d)
and
(e)
.

Proof.

Given a chain
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
, we can apply
2.26
to each of the
R_{i}
to produce a
2k
-tuple
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
. By construction, this tuple satisfies conditions
(a)
,
(b)
and
(d)
. Furthermore,
\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{x_{2i}})=X_{i}=\pi_{n_{i-1}}(\underline{x_{2i-1}})
, thus condition
(c)
is also satisfied. Finally, condition
(e)
also follows from
2.26
.

For the converse, observe that condition
(d)
unambiguously characterizes the relations
R_{i}
, which are surjective by construction. On the other hand, condition
(c)
guarantees that
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
forms a combinatorial chain and condition
(b)
implies that it is a chain between
X
and
Y
. Finally, condition
(e)
is simply a direct computation following from
2.26
.
∎

Finally, we exhibit an upper bound for the distance of intermediate configurations to one of the end of a combinatorial chain. This will be necessary to prove that we can compute the distance over a closed and bounded set in a product of
M
, hence a compact set.

Lemma 2.28
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations, and
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
a combinatorial chain between
X
and
Y
, with
\card(R_{i})=n_{i}
. Then, if
(\underline{x_{0}},\dots,\underline{x_{2k-1}})
is a
2k
-tuple obtained from
2.27
, we have, for all
0\leq i\leq k-1

\underline{x_{2i}},\underline{x_{2i+1}}\in\left(\overline{B}_{M}(X,\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))\right)^{n_{i}},

Where, for
X\subset M
and
r\geq 0
,

\overline{B}_{M}(X,r)=\{x\in M\mid d(x,X)\leq r\}

Proof.

Since, for all
1\leq i\leq k-1
, every coordinate of
\underline{x_{2i}}
is equal to one of the coordinates of
\underline{x_{2i-1}}
, it is enough to prove the claim for the terms
\underline{x_{0}}
and
\underline{x_{2i+1}}
,
0\leq i\leq k-1
. Since
\pi_{n_{0}}(\underline{x_{0}})=X
, the claim is clear for
\underline{x_{0}}
. Now, it remains to be proven that for any
0\leq i\leq k-1
, and
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
,
d\left(x_{2i+1}^{(j)},X\right)\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
.
Now, write
X_{l}=\pi_{n_{l}}(\underline{x_{2l}})
, for
0\leq l\leq k-1
. Since the relations
R_{l}
are surjective, there must exist
y_{0},\dots,y_{i}
, such that
y_{l}\in X_{l}
, for
0\leq l\leq i
, and
y_{i}=x_{2i+1}^{(j)}
, and such that
y_{l}R_{l}y_{l+1}
, for all
0\leq l\leq i-1
. But then, we have

\displaystyle d\left(x_{2i+1}^{(j)},X\right)

\displaystyle\leq d(y_{i},y_{0})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{i-1}d(y_{l},y_{l+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=0}^{i-1}\left(\omega(n_{l})\sum_{xR_{l}y}d(x,y)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{i-1})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

∎

3
Length structure

The goal of this section is to show that the weighted distances on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
that we introduced in
Section

2
are in fact length metrics (see
Section

1.6
), provided
M
is a length space itself. This is the content of
3.8
. This allows for an interpretation of the distances in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as being induced by a set of geodesics, for which we provide an explicit description in
Section

3.2
.

In the following
M
is a length space (see
Section

1.6
) with length structure
\mathcal{P}
and
\ell
, and
\omega
is a weight.

3.1
Paths and lifts

The goal of this section is to define a length structure on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. One may wonder what a continuous path on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
may be depending on which topology is chosen. Indeed, the set of continuous path on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
are distinct if one considers the Hausdorff topology, or the final topology, or even one of the topologies induced by the weighted distances. However, paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
that we consider to be admissible will be continuous for all of those topologies (see
3.4
), thus the ambiguity on what continuous means will be alleviated immediately.

Example 3.1
.

Before specifying to which paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
we will associate a length, let us first give a few examples of what continuous paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
may look like. Consider
M=\mathbb{R}
, in that case, any path can be plotted as the graph of a multi-valued function, as displayed on
Fig.

2
. Given a path
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathbb{R}
, we also plot the integer-valued function
t\mapsto\card(\gamma(t))
. Note that, even over a closed interval, path of unbounded cardinality may be continuous for the Hausdorff topology, however they will be excluded from the definition of admissible paths. All paths pictured here will in fact be admissible, for the usual length structure on
\mathbb{R}
, induced by its structure of Riemannian manifold.

Figure 2:
Examples of continuous paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R})
and their cardinality.

Definition 3.2
.

Let
a\leq b\in\mathbb{R}
, and
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
a continuous path. A lift of
\Gamma
is given by

•

a subdivision of
[a,b]
,
a=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}=b
, with
k\geq 0
,

•

for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
, a continuous path,
\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n_{i}}
, with
n_{i}\geq 1
, such that
\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}}(t))=\Gamma(t)
for all
t\in[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
.

Such a lift is written
((t_{0},\dots,t_{k}),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
, or just
(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})
. This lift is said to be admissible if all the
\underline{\gamma_{i}}
are admissible paths in
M^{n_{i}}
(see
1.37
).

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We say that
\Gamma
is admissible, if it is continuous and admits an admissible lift.

Remark 3.4
.

The assumption of continuity for an admissible path is redundant, though we keep it for clarity. Indeed, if
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a function, not necessarily continuous, and if there exits a lift of
\Gamma
,
((t_{0},\dots,t_{k}),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
such that every
\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n_{i}}
,
0\leq i\leq k-1
is continuous, then, by construction,

\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is continuous for the final topology and thus for the Hausdorff topology. Indeed, continuity of
\Gamma
on the intervals
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
is clear, and continuity at
t_{i}
follows from the condition the
\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}}(t_{i}))=\pi_{n_{i-1}}(\underline{\gamma_{i-1}}(t_{i}))
, which is the definition of
(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})
being a lift.

In particular, the set of continuous paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
which admit a lift (admissible or otherwise), does not depend on whether we consider the final topology or the Hausdorff topology. Note however that this is no longer the case if we drop the hypothesis of admitting a lift. Indeed, there exist continuous paths whose cardinality is unbounded both for the Hausdorff topology and for weighted topologies.
Let us show how results that we will prove latter allow one to construct such a path. Consider the sequence of unbounded cardinality,
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which converges to
\{0\}
in
\mathrm{Ran}([0,1])
for
d^{\omega}
,
introduced in
4.4
. Up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume that
\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}d^{\omega}(X_{n},X_{n+1})<\infty
.
Since this is a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}([0,1])
,
3.21
applies, and up to reparametrization,
we may find continuous paths between
X_{n}
and
X_{n+1}
,
\Gamma_{n}\colon[1-\frac{1}{2^{n}},1-\frac{1}{2^{n+1}})\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

whose length will be
d^{\omega}(X_{n},X_{n+1})
.
Concatenating those paths gives a continuous map
\Gamma\colon[0,1)\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
of unbounded cardinality, but the convergence of the sequence
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
to
\{0\}
(together with the fact that
\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{k})
tends to
0
) implies that we can extend this map by continuity by defining
\Gamma(1)=\{0\}
.
This is an example of the desired form for the weighted topology, but since it refines the Hausdorff topology (
5.7
) it is also an example for the Hausdorff topology. Such a path cannot be continuous for the final topology however, since if it were, we could deduce that
(X_{n})
converges to
\{0\}
for the final topology, which is impossible by
1.12
.

Furthermore, path of the above form do not admit any lift (admissible or otherwise). Since we are only interested in admissible paths here, which admit a lift by definition, we may thus unambiguously speak of their continuity without specifying the choice of topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

The previous comment also extends to the topologies induced by the weighted distances. In fact, observe that, by definition, admissible paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
must factor through
M^{n}
for some large enough
n
, and this factorization must in fact be continuous. Now, observe that the projection
M^{n}\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is continuous for every topology we considered on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Thus, admissibility of a path reduces to the existence of a continuous (global) lift to
M^{n}
for some
n
. The reason we did not define admissibility this way from the start is that the above definition of a lift is better suited for the definition of the length of an admissible path.

Definition 3.5
.

Let
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and
((t_{0},\dots,t_{k}),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
be an admissible lift of
\Gamma
. The length of the lift relative to
\omega
is defined as

\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(n_{i})\ell^{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}})

Definition 3.6
.

Let
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be an admissible path. Its length relative to
\omega
is defined as

\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)=\inf\{\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})\}

where the infimum is taken over the admissible lifts of
\Gamma
.

Remark 3.7
.

Observe that the length of a lift is conserved when refining the subdivision. In particular, the length of a concatenation of lifts is the sum of the lengths of each concatenated piece. Similarly the length of a concatenation of paths is the sum of the paths length.

We can now show that the weighted distance
d^{\omega}
is in fact a length metric, for the length structure where
\mathcal{P}
is the set of paths admitting an admissible lift, and the length is
\ell^{\omega}
.

Theorem 3.8
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. The weighted distance, relative to
\omega
, between
X
and
Y
is equal to

d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\inf\{\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\mid\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M),\Gamma(a)=X,\Gamma(b)=Y\}

where the infimum is computed over all admissible paths from
X
to
Y
.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and let us write

I=\{\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)\mid a<b\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and $\Gamma$ admissible path from $X$ to $Y$}\}.

Let us first show that
\inf_{\Gamma\in I}\{\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\}\geq d^{\omega}(X,Y)
. Let
\epsilon>0
and
\Gamma\in I
such that

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\leq\inf_{\Lambda\in I}\{\ell^{\omega}(\Lambda)\}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Let
((a=t_{0},\dots,t_{k}=b),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
be an admissible lift of
\Gamma
such that

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})\leq\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

For all
0\leq i\leq k-1
, we write explicitly

\displaystyle\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]

\displaystyle\to M^{n_{i}}

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto(\gamma_{i}^{(1)}(t),\dots,\gamma_{i}^{(n_{i})}(t))

Let us define
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
as

\displaystyle X_{i}=\Gamma(t_{i}),\quad\text{for all }0\leq i\leq k

\displaystyle R_{i}=\left\{\left(\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i}),\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i+1})\right)\mid 1\leq j\leq n_{i}\right\},\quad\text{for all }0\leq i\leq k-1.

One easily checks that, since
(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})
is a lift of
\Gamma
, this defines a combinatorial chain from
X
to
Y
. Let
0\leq i\leq k-1
. For any
1\leq j\leq n_{i}
we have

\displaystyle d\left(\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i}),\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i+1})\right)

\displaystyle=\inf\{\ell(\lambda)\}\leq\ell(\gamma_{i}^{(j)}),

where is the infimum is taken over all admissible paths in
M
from
\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i})
to
\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i+1})
. Hence

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})

\displaystyle=\omega(n_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}d\left(\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i}),\gamma_{i}^{(j)}(t_{i+1})\right)\leq\omega(n_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}\ell(\gamma_{i}^{(j)})=\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{i}}).

We finally get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}})\leq\inf_{\Lambda\in I}\left\{\ell^{\omega}(\Lambda)\right\}+\epsilon.

Since this is true for any
\epsilon>0
, we have the desired inequality.

Let us now show that
d^{\omega}(X,Y)\geq\inf_{\Lambda\in I}\left\{\ell^{\omega}(\Lambda)\right\}
. Let
\epsilon>0
. By definition, there exists
((X=X_{0},\dots,X_{k}=Y),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
a combinatorial chain from
X
to
Y
such that

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

Let
0\leq i\leq k-1
, write
n_{i}=\card(R_{i})
and let us fix an order on
R_{i}=\{(x^{(j)},y^{(j)})\}_{1\leq j\leq n_{i}}
. We then choose an admissible path in
M^{n_{i}}
,
\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[i,i+1]\to M^{n_{i}}
from
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n_{i})})
to
\underline{y}=(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n_{i})})
which we may assume to satisfy

\displaystyle\ell^{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}})\leq d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{y})+\frac{\epsilon}{2k\omega(n_{i})}.

Then, we directly have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{i}})=\omega(n_{i})\ell^{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}})\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2k}+\omega(n_{i})\sum_{j=1}^{n_{i}}d(x^{(j)},y^{(j)})=\frac{\epsilon}{2k}+\ell^{\omega}(R_{i}).

Moreover, since
R_{i}
is surjective, we also have
\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}}(i))=X_{i}
and
\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}}(i+1))=X_{i+1}
, so that
((0,\dots,k),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
defines an admissible lift of the path
\Gamma
defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma\colon[0,k]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\pi_{n_{i}}(\underline{\gamma_{i}}(t))\quad\text{if }i\leq t\leq i+1,

which is by construction an admissible path from
X
to
Y
.
We finally have

\displaystyle\inf_{\Lambda\in I}\left\{\ell^{\omega}(\Lambda)\right\}\leq\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\leq\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})+\frac{\epsilon}{2}\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\epsilon.

Since this is true for any
\epsilon>0
, this concludes this argument, and this proof.
∎

Corollary 3.9
.

The metric space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
is a length space.

Proof.

We have checked all but the last two conditions in
1.30
. The continuity assumption follows from that of the length structure on
M
. And by
1.33
, the last assumption follows from the fact that
d^{\ell^{\omega}}
is indeed a distance refining the Hausdorff topology.
∎

3.2
Geodesic paths

In this section we show that the infimum in
3.8
can be taken over a smaller class of path that have a piecewise constant cardinality, and for which the infimum in
3.6
is realized (that is, they admit a lift with the correct length). We call these paths crenelated paths. Then we show that we can restrict even further to paths whose cardinality first decreases and then increases. By similarity with MBS-chains, we call these paths MBS-paths. Finally, we use these results to show that, provided geodesic chains exist in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and geodesics between any two points of
M
exist, then the infimum in
3.8
is realized by a MBS-path.

We start by showing that path with a constant cardinality always admit a lift of minimal dimension.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
n\geq 1
,
I
an interval (closed, open or semi-open), and
\Gamma\colon I\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
a continuous path, such that
\card(\Gamma(t))=n
, for all
t\in I
. Then there exists a continuous lift of
\Gamma
,
\underline{\gamma}\colon I\to M^{n}
. Moreover, if
\underline{\lambda}\colon I\to M^{m}
is such that
\pi_{m}(\underline{\lambda}(t))=\Gamma(t)
,
\forall t\in I
, then there exists a surjection
f\colon\{1,\dots,m\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
\lambda=f\cdot\gamma
.

Proof.

The path
\Gamma
has constant cardinality, so that its image is a subset of
\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
. Since
\pi_{n}\colon\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)\to\mathrm{Conf}_{n}(M)
is a covering, there exists a lift of
\Gamma
,
\underline{\gamma}=(\gamma^{(1)},\dots,\gamma^{(n)})\colon I\to\mathrm{Conf}^{\mathrm{ord}}_{n}(M)\subset M^{n}
.

Let
\underline{\lambda}=(\lambda^{(1)},\dots,\lambda^{(m)})\colon I\to M^{m}
such that
\pi_{m}(\underline{\lambda}(t))=\Gamma(t)
,
\forall t\in I
. Since the cardinality of
\Gamma(t)
is equal to
n
for all
t\in I
, we must have
m\geq n
.

Let us fix
t\in I
. Since
\pi_{m}(\underline{\lambda}(t))=\Gamma(t)
, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\lambda^{(i)}(t)\in\Gamma(t)
and since
\Gamma(t)=\pi_{n}(\underline{\gamma}(t))
and
\card(\Gamma(t))=n
, there exists a unique
j_{i}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
\lambda^{(i)}(t)=\gamma^{(j_{i})}(t)
.
We can thus define a map
f\colon\{1,\dots,m\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
f(i)=j_{i}
, which will automatically be surjective. Then,
\lambda(t)=f\cdot\gamma(t)
.

We then define
\Omega=\{s\in I\mid\lambda(s)=f\cdot\gamma(s)\}
. Let
s\in I
, we have
s\in\Omega
if and only if,

(a)

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
\lambda^{(i)}(s)=\gamma^{(f(i))}(s)
,

(b)

for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
j\neq f(i)\Rightarrow\lambda^{(i)}(s)\neq\gamma^{(j)}(s)
.

Note that both conditions are actually equivalent. Indeed, if
s\in I
satisfies the first condition, then, as the
\gamma^{(j)}(s)
are pairwise distinct,
s
also satisfies the second condition. And if
s\in I
satisfies the second condition then, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,m\}
, since
\lambda^{(i)}(s)\in\pi_{n}(\underline{\gamma}(s))
, there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
\lambda^{(i)}(s)=\gamma^{(j)}(s)
, which implies
\lambda^{(i)}(s)=\gamma^{(f(i))}(s)
.

Let us consider the following maps:

\displaystyle g\colon I

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{m}d(\lambda^{(i)}(s),\gamma^{(f(i))}(s))

\displaystyle h\colon I

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}

\displaystyle s

\displaystyle\mapsto\prod_{i=1}^{m}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}j=1\\
j\neq f(i)\end{subarray}}^{n}d(\lambda^{(i)}(s),\gamma^{(j)}(s))

The maps
g
and
h
are continuous. Moreover, using the previous remarks, we have
\Omega=g^{-1}(0)=h^{-1}((0,+\infty))
. We deduce that
\Omega
is both open and closed. Since
t\in\Omega
by definition, and since
\Omega\subset I
which is connected, we have
\Omega=I
.
∎

Since we want to consider paths that have a piecewise constant cardinality, we want to extend the previous result to path whose cardinality is only constant over an open interval.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let
n\geq 0
,
a<b
and
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
an admissible path such that
\card(\Gamma(t))=n
for all
t\in(a,b)
. Then there exists an admissible lift of
\Gamma
,
\underline{\gamma}\colon[a,b]\to M^{n}
. Moreover, if
\underline{\lambda}\colon[a,b]\to M^{n}
is another admissible lift of
\Gamma
, then there exists a permutation
\phi\in\Sigma_{n}
such that
\underline{\lambda}=\phi\cdot\underline{\gamma}
.

Proof.

We start by showing unicity. Let
\underline{\lambda},\underline{\mu}\colon[a,b]\to M^{n}
two admissible lifts of
\Gamma
. By
3.10
, there exists a continuous lift of
\Gamma_{|(a,b)}
,
\underline{\gamma}\colon(a,b)\to M^{n}
.
Moreover, since
\underline{\lambda}_{|(a,b)}
and
\underline{\mu}_{|(a,b)}
are two continuous lifts of
\Gamma_{|(a,b)}
, there exist two bijections
\phi,\psi\colon\{1,\dots,n\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
such that

\underline{\lambda}_{|(a,b)}=\phi\cdot\underline{\gamma}\quad\text{ and, }\quad\underline{\mu}_{|(a,b)}=\psi\cdot\underline{\gamma}

We thus get
\underline{\lambda}_{|(a,b)}=(\phi\psi^{-1})\cdot\underline{\mu}_{|(a,b)}
. By continuity of
\underline{\lambda},\underline{\mu}
, and of the group action of
\Sigma_{n}
on
M^{n}
, we can extend the equality on the closed interval
[a,b]
, which is the desired result.

For the existence property, since
\Gamma
is an admissible path, there exists an admissible lift,
((a=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}=b),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
, where for any
0\leq i\leq k-1
,
\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n_{i}}
is an admissible lift of
\Gamma_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]}
. Now let us construct the desired lift on each subset
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
. Let
0\leq i\leq k-1
.

Observe that, for any
1\leq i\leq k-2
, the restriction of
\Gamma
to the interval
[t_{i},t_{i+1}]
is of constant cardinality,
n
. Thus, by
3.10
, we may find lifts
\underline{\lambda_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n}
, for all
1\leq i\leq k-2
.
On the other hand, the restriction of
\Gamma
to the interval
(t_{0},t_{1}]
is of constant cardinality,
n
, thus by the same lemma, we may find a lift
\lambda_{0}\colon(t_{0},t_{1}]\to M^{n}
, together with a surjective map
f\colon\{1,\dots,n_{0}\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
\gamma_{0}^{(i)}(t)=\lambda_{0}^{f(i)}(t)
for all
t\in(t_{0},t_{1}]
. Continuity of
\underline{\lambda_{0}}
, together with the fact that
M
is Hausdorff implies that if
i,i^{\prime}\in\{0,\dots,n_{0}\}
are such that
f(i)=f(i^{\prime})
, then
\lambda_{0}^{(i)}=\lambda_{0}^{(i^{\prime})}
on the entire closed interval
[t_{0},t_{1}]
. In particular, we may unambiguously define the continuous lift
\underline{\lambda_{0}}\colon[t_{0},t_{1}]\to M^{n}
via
\lambda_{0}^{(j)}(t)=\gamma_{0}^{(i)}(t)
for all
t\in[t_{0},t_{1}]
for any
i
such that
f(i)=j
. This will still be a lift of the restriction of
\Gamma
to
[t_{0},t_{1}]
by surjectivity of
f
, and it will be admissible since each of its component is a component of
\underline{\gamma_{0}}
which was assumed to be admissible.
The symmetric construction yields a lift
\underline{\lambda_{k-1}}\colon[t_{k-1},t_{k}]\to M^{n}
. Then, up to choosing a permutation for each subdivided lift, we may concatenate the
\underline{\lambda_{l}}
to yield a global lift
\underline{\lambda}\colon[t_{0},t_{k}]\to M^{n}
.

∎

Then, we show that the lift of minimal dimension is also minimal in length.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let
n\geq 0
,
a<b\in\mathbb{R}
,
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
an admissible path such that
\card(\Gamma(t))=n
for all
t\in(a,b)
. Then there exists an admissible lift of
\Gamma
,
\underline{\gamma}\colon[a,b]\to M^{n}
. In addition, any such lift satisfies

\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)=\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma}).

Proof.

The existence is given by
3.11
.
The same proposition, ensures that any such pair of lifts
\underline{\gamma},\underline{\lambda}\colon[a,b]\to M^{n}
there exists a permutation
\phi\in\Sigma_{n}
such that
\underline{\gamma}=\phi\cdot\underline{\lambda}
.
Since
\Sigma_{n}
acts isometrically on
M^{n}
, we get
\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma})=\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\lambda})
.

Now let
((t_{0},\dots,t_{k}),(\underline{\mu_{0}},\dots,\underline{\mu_{k-1}}))
an admissible lift of
\Gamma
.
Let us show that
\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\mu_{0}},\dots,\underline{\mu_{k-1}})\geq\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma})
. By
3.7
, we just have to show that
\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\mu_{i}})\geq\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma}_{[t_{i},t_{i+1}]})
for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
.
For some given
i
, we have the lift
\underline{\mu_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n_{i}}
.
Using
3.10
, and extending by continuity, we get the existence of a surjection
f\colon\{1,\dots,n_{i}\}\to\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
\underline{\mu_{i}}=f\cdot\underline{\gamma}_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]}
.
We thus have :

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\gamma}_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]})

\displaystyle=\omega(n)\sum_{j=1}^{n}\ell(\gamma^{(j)}_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n_{i})\sum_{k=1}^{n_{i}}\ell(\gamma^{(f(k))}_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n_{i})\sum_{k=1}^{n_{i}}\ell(\mu_{i}^{(k)})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(\underline{\mu_{i}})

which concludes this proof.
∎

We can now concatenate paths whose cardinality is constant over an open interval to obtain a path whose cardinality is piecewise constant. This is the object of the following definition.

Definition 3.13
.

Let
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
an admissible path. We say that
\Gamma
is
crenelated
if there exists a subdivision
a=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}=b
, and integers
n_{0},\dots,n_{k-1}
such that, for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
, and for all
t\in(t_{i},t_{i+1})
,
\card(\Gamma(t))=n_{i}
.

We can thus concatenate minimal lifts to obtain a minimal lift of crenelated paths.

Proposition 3.14
.

Let
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
a crenelated path. The infimum in
3.6
is realized.

Moreover, if
a=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}=b
is a subdivision of
[a,b]
such that
\card(\Gamma(t))=n_{i}
for all
t\in(t_{i},t_{i+1})
and
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, then any lift of the form
((t_{0},\dots,t_{k}),(\underline{\gamma_{0}},\dots,\underline{\gamma_{k-1}}))
, with
\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n_{i}}
, for
0\leq i\leq k-1
, realizes the infimum.

Proof.

By hypothesis, there exists
a=t_{0}<\dots<t_{k}=b
, a subdivision of
[a,b]
, and
(n_{i})_{i\in\{1,\dots,k-1\}}
integers, such that for all
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, and for all
t\in(t_{i},t_{i+1})
,
\card(\Gamma(t))=n_{i}
.
Now, by definition of
\ell^{\omega}
on admissible paths in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, we have the equality

\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]}).

By
3.12
the length
\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{|[t_{i},t_{i+1}]})
is realized by an (unique up to permutation) admissible lift
\underline{\gamma_{i}}\colon[t_{i},t_{i+1}]\to M^{n_{i}}
. Concatenating the
\underline{\gamma_{i}}
gives the desired lift.
∎

Now that we know how to compute the length of admissible paths, we want to restrict the set of paths over which we compute the infimum in
3.8
. As in
Section

2.4
, we will use this result to show that the infimum can, under some hypotheses, be realized. First we define properly the path equivalent of MBS-chains.

Definition 3.15
.

Let
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a path in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. It is called a MBS-path if it is crenelated and if there exists
a\leq c\leq b
such that
\card(\Gamma)_{|[a,c]}
is non increasing and
\card(\Gamma)_{|[c,b]}
is non decreasing.

Then we show that we can restrict the computation of the infimum in
3.8
over MBS-paths.

Proposition 3.16
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. The infimum in
3.8
can be computed over all MBS-paths.

The proof relies on the construction, from a combinatorial chain, of a crenelated path with a shorter length (this relies on
3.19
and
3.20
). To control the cardinality of the constructed path, it is easier to work with the following definition.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
R
a relation between
X
and
Y
. We say that
R
is
atomic
if it is surjective and if there exists at most a unique
(x,y)\in R
such that
x\neq y
. We say that
R
is an atomic relation from
x
to
y
.

Remark 3.18
.

If
R
is an atomic relation, then
R
is either (i) the graph of the identity, or (ii) the graph of a map that is the identity everywhere except in one point, which is either a bijection or a simple merge, or (iii)
R^{\mathrm{op}}
is the graph of a map that is the identity everywhere except in one point, so that
R
is either a bijection or a simple split.

Furthermore, observe that if
R
is an atomic relation from
x
to
y
, then the
\omega
-length of
R
is equal to
\ell^{\omega}(R)=\omega(\card(R))d(x,y)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
. By
2.16
there exists a simple MBS-chain
((X=X_{0},\dots,X_{k}=Y),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
from
X
to
Y
such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\frac{\epsilon}{2}
.
By
3.19
, we may replace the chain by a chain
(A_{0},\dots,A_{l-1})
with the same length or shorter, but such that for some
0\leq j\leq l
,
A_{i}
is either an atomic merge or an atomic bijection for all
i<j
and either an atomic split or an atomic bijection for all
i\geq j
. By
3.20
, each of those atomic relation can be realized by an admissible path
\Gamma_{i}
, with
\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{i})\leq\ell^{\omega}(A_{i})+\frac{\epsilon}{2l}
. For convenience, assume that
\Gamma_{i}
is parametrized by the interval
[i,i+1]
. We know that for
0\leq i\leq j
,
\card(\Gamma_{j}(t))
is non increasing on
[i,i+1]
, thus the concatenation of the
\Gamma_{i}
for
0\leq i\leq j
gives a map
[0,j+1]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
whose cardinality is non increasing. Similarly, the concatenation of the
\Gamma_{i}
for
j<i\leq l
gives a map
[j+1,l]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
whose cardinality is non decreasing. Thus, the overall concatenation
\Gamma\colon[0,l]\to\mathrm{Ran}(X)
is a MBS-path. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{l-1}\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{l-1}\left(\ell^{\omega}(A_{i})+\frac{\epsilon}{2l}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\ell^{\omega}(A_{0},\dots,A_{l-1})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}+\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\epsilon

Thus, there exists MBS-path in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
between
X
and
Y
of
\omega
-length arbitrary close to
d^{\omega}(X,Y)
.
∎

We need to decompose the relations of MBS-chains into atomic relations, without increasing their length. This is object of the following lemma.

Lemma 3.19
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
be a MBS-chain between
X
and
Y
. Then, there exists another combinatorial chain,
(A_{0},\dots,A_{l-1})
, between
X
and
Y
, together with some
0\leq j\leq l
such that:

•

\ell^{\omega}(A_{0},\dots,A_{l-1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
,

•

A_{i}
is either an atomic merge or an atomic bijection, for all
i<j
,

•

A_{i}
is either an atomic split or an atomic bijection, for all
i\geq j

Proof.

We will prove the result in two parts. First, we will show that any simple merge
R_{i}
can be decomposed, without increasing its length, into an atomic bijection followed by an atomic merge. This will imply the corresponding statement for splits. Secondly, we will show that any bijection
R
between configurations
Z
and
W
must satisfy one of the following two properties

•

R
can be decomposed, without increasing its length into a sequence of atomic bijections.

•

There exists a MBS-chain between
Z
and
W
,
(Q_{0},\dots,Q_{l-1})
, with
l\geq 2
and which is shorter than
R
.

Then, to obtain the statement, first consider the bijection step in
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
. Either it can be decomposed into atomic bijections, or it can be shortened by replacing it by some MBS-chain
(Q_{0},\dots,Q_{l-1})
. In the latter case, since
l\geq 2
, the bijection step in
(Q_{0},\dots,Q_{l-1})
must be of strictly smaller cardinality than that in
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
. Since any bijection of cardinality
1
is an atomic bijection, the previous construction always yields, in finitely many steps, a MBS-chain
(R^{\prime}_{0},\dots,R^{\prime}_{k-1})
whose bijection step decomposes into atomic bijections. Then, decomposing all merges (resp. splits) into atomic merges (resp. atomic splits) and atomic bijections gives the desired result.

Now, for the first step, assume that
R
is a simple merge between
X
and
Y
. Then there exists
x\neq x^{\prime}\in X
and
y\in Y
such that
(x,y),(x^{\prime},y)\in R
. If either
x=y
or
x^{\prime}=y
then
R
is already atomic. Otherwise,
y
is not in
X
(since if it were, the merge would not be simple) and we can decompose
R
into two atomic relations as follows. Let us define
X^{\prime}=X\setminus\{x\}\cup\{y\}
and
P=R\setminus\{(x^{\prime},y)\}\cup\{(x^{\prime},x^{\prime})\}
and
P^{\prime}=R\setminus\{(x,y)\}
. Then
P
is an atomic bijective relation from
X
to
X^{\prime}
, and
P^{\prime}
is an atomic merge from
X^{\prime}
to
Y
. Furthermore, if
n=\card(R)
, then
\ell^{\omega}(R)=\omega(n)(d(x,y)+d(x^{\prime},y))
and on the other hand,
\ell^{\omega}(P)=\omega(n)d(x,y)
and
\ell^{\omega}(P^{\prime})=\omega(n)d(x^{\prime},y)
, which gives
\ell^{\omega}(R)=\ell^{\omega}(P,P^{\prime})
.

For the second step, let
R
be a bijection between
X
and
Y
, and write
\phi\colon X\to Y
for the corresponding map. Let us first assume that for all
x\in X\cap Y
,
\phi(x)=x
. Let us fix an order on the elements in
X\setminus(X\cap Y)=\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
. For
0\leq i\leq n
, let
X_{i}=(X\cap Y)\cup\{\phi(x_{1}),\dots,\phi(x_{i}),x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n}\}
, and define
\phi_{i}\colon X_{i}\to X_{i+1}
by
\phi_{i}(x_{i+1})=\phi(x_{i+1})
and
\phi_{i}(z)=z
for all
z\in X_{i}\setminus\{x_{i+1}\}
. Then, by construction,
X=X_{0}
,
Y=X_{n}
, and each of the
X_{i}
is of the same cardinality as
X
, and each of the
\phi_{i}
is a bijection, whose graph we will denote
R_{i}
. Furthermore,
\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})=\omega(\card(R))d(x_{i+1},\phi(x_{i+1}))
and hence,
\ell^{\omega}(R)=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
, which completes the proof in that case.

Now, assume that there exists some
x\in X\cap Y
such that
\phi(x)\not=x
. Let
\psi\colon Y\to X
denote the inverse of
\phi
, and consider the sets
X_{1}=X\setminus\{\psi(x)\}
and
X_{2}=Y\setminus\{\phi(x)\}
, together with
X_{0}=X
and
X_{3}=Y
. We define
R_{0}\subset X_{0}\times X_{1}
as the merge of
x
and
\psi(x)
at
x
, and
R_{2}\subset X_{2}\times X_{3}
as the splitting of
x
into
x
and
\phi(x)
. Finally, define
R_{1}\subset X_{1}\times X_{2}
as follows. For
(y,z)\in X_{1}\times X_{2}
we have
(y,z)\in R_{1}
if either
(y,z)\in R
or
(y,z)=(x,x)
. Note that
R_{1}
is still the graph of a bijection. Thus, we have decomposed
R
into the MBS-chain
(R_{0},R_{1},R_{2})
. Furthermore, setting
n=\card(R)
, we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},R_{1},R_{2})

\displaystyle=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(R_{1})+\ell^{\omega}(R_{2})

\displaystyle=\omega(n)d(\psi(x),x)+\omega(n-1)\left(\sum_{x\in X\setminus\{x,\psi(x)\}}d(x,\phi(x))\right)+\omega(n)d(x,\phi(x))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)\sum_{x\in X}d(x,\phi(x))=\ell^{\omega}(R),

which completes the proof.
∎

Finally, this last technical lemma allows to construct, from an atomic relation, a crenelated path whose length is as close as wanted from the length of the relation, and with well-behaved cardinality. The technicality resides in avoided crossings between distinct components of the constructed path. The proof is illustrated in
Fig.

3
.

Lemma 3.20
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and let
R
be an atomic relation from
X
to
Y
. For any
\epsilon>0
, there exists a crenelated path
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
from
X
to
Y
such that
\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)+\epsilon
, and such that either

•

The cardinality of
\Gamma
is constant, if
R
is a bijection.

•

The cardinality of
\Gamma
is non increasing, if
R
is a simple merge.

•

The cardinality of
\Gamma
is non decreasing, if
R
is a simple split.

Proof.

Let us fix
\epsilon>0
and let us denote
n=\card(R)
. Let us start with the case where
R
is an atomic bijection. If
R
is the graph of the identity, then any constant path works. Otherwise, there exists a unique
(x,y)\in R
such that
x\neq y
. Let us fix an order
X=\{x,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
, and
Y=\{y,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
.

There exists an admissible path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M
from
x
to
y
such that
\ell(\gamma)\leq d(x,y)+\frac{\epsilon}{\omega(n)}
. Note that we cannot simply define our desired path
\Gamma
as

\displaystyle[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto X\setminus\{x\}\cup\{\gamma(t)\}

because there could be "crossings", i.e. times
t\in[0,1]
such that there exists
1\leq i\leq n-1
such that
\gamma(t)=x_{i}
. Note however that we can chose
\gamma
such that, for any
z\in X\cup Y
, there exists at most one
t\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(t)=z
. Indeed, if there is more that one, they form a closed set of
[0,1]
, so that we can take their minimum
t_{\mathrm{min}}=\min\{t\in[0,1]\mid\gamma(t)=z\}
and similarly for the maximum
t_{\mathrm{max}}
, and define a shorter, continuous path, with a single crossing with
z
.

\displaystyle[0,1]

\displaystyle\to M

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\gamma(2t_{\mathrm{min}}t)&\text{if }0\leq t\leq\frac{1}{2}\\
\gamma\Big(2(1-t_{\mathrm{max}})(t-1)+1\Big)&\text{if }\frac{1}{2}\leq t\leq 1\end{cases}.

By repeating this process for every
z\in X\cup Y
, we obtain a path
\gamma
which crosses each of the
z\in X\cup Y
at most one time.

Let
m
be the number of "crossings", i.e. the number of times
t\in(0,1)
such that there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
such that
\gamma(t)=x_{j}
. Up to a reordering of the
x_{j}
, we can suppose that for all
1\leq j\leq m
there exists a unique
t_{j}\in(0,1)
such that
\gamma(t_{j})=x_{j}
, and that
0<t_{m}\leq\dots\leq t_{1}<1
.
If
m=0
, then we simply define

\displaystyle\Gamma\colon[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\gamma(t),x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}

which verifies that
\card(\Gamma(t))=n
for all
t\in[0,1]
. Moreover, we have an admissible lift of
\Gamma
of which we know the length, so that

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\leq\omega(n)\ell(\gamma)\leq\omega(n)d(x,y)+\epsilon=\ell^{\omega}(R)+\epsilon.

Otherwise, we define

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}\colon[0,1-t_{1}]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\{x,\gamma(t+t_{1}),x_{2},\dots,x_{n-1}\}

\displaystyle\Gamma_{m}\colon[1-t_{m},1]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\{y,x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1},\gamma(t+t_{m}-1),x_{m+1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}

and, for all
1\leq i\leq m-1
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{i}\colon[1-t_{i},1-t_{i+1}]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\{x,y,x_{1},\dots,x_{i-1},\gamma(t+t_{i}+t_{i+1}-1),x_{i+2},\dots,x_{n-1}\}

One can indeed check that for any
0\leq i\leq m
, the path
\Gamma_{i}
has a constant cardinality. Furthermore,
\Gamma_{0}
gives a path between
X
and
\{x,y,x_{2},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
,
\Gamma_{m}
gives a path between
\{x,y,x_{1},\dots,x_{m-1},x_{m+1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
and
Y
, and
\Gamma_{i}
gives a path between
\{x,y,x_{1},\dots,\widehat{x_{i}},x_{i+1},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
and
\{x,y,x_{1},\dots,x_{i},\widehat{x_{i+1}},\dots,x_{n-1}\}
, so that they can be concatenated into a path
\Gamma
between
X
and
Y
of constant cardinality. Furthermore, each of the
\Gamma_{i}
is admissible by construction, and thus so is
\Gamma
, and we have:

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)=\sum_{i=0}^{m}\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{i})=\omega(n)\ell(\gamma)\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)+\epsilon.

Let us now treat the case where
R
is an atomic merge. There exists a unique
(x,y)\in R
such that
x\neq y
, but this time we have
y\in X
, so that
Y\subset X
and
\card(Y)=n-1
. As before, there exists an admissible path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to M
from
x
to
y
such that
\ell(\gamma)\leq d(x,y)+\frac{\epsilon}{\omega(n)}
. Furthermore, we may repeat the previous construction to ensure that, for any
z\in X
, there exists at most one
t\in[0,1]
such that
\gamma(t)=z
. Order the elements of
X
so that
X=\{x,y,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-2}\}
, and let
m
be the number of crossings occurring in
(0,1)
. If
m=0
, then we may simply define

\displaystyle\Gamma\colon[0,1]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\{\gamma(t),y,x_{1},\dots,x_{n-2}\}

which is a crenelated path, and satisfies
\card(\Gamma(t))=n
for all
t\in[0,1)
and
\card(\Gamma(1))=n-1
, which is indeed non increasing. Moreover, we have as before

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)\leq\omega(n)\ell(\gamma)=\ell^{\omega}(R)+\epsilon.

Otherwise,
m>0
, and let
0<t_{m}<\dots<t_{1}<1
be such that
\gamma(t_{i})\in X
. Note that we must have
\gamma(t_{i})\not=x,y
by construction, and we may once more reorder the elements in
X
such that
\gamma(t_{i})=x_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq m
.

Once more, let us define

\displaystyle\Gamma_{0}\colon[0,1-t_{1}]

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\{x,y,\gamma(t+t_{1}),x_{2},\dots,x_{n-2}\}

which verifies that
\card(\Gamma_{0}(t))=n
for all
t\in[0,1-t_{1})
and
\card(\Gamma(1-t_{1}))=n-1
. Furthermore, it is admissible by construction, and
\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{0})\leq\omega(n)\ell(\gamma_{|[t_{1},1]})
. Now we can define
X_{1}=\Gamma_{0}(1-t_{1})=\{x,y,x_{2},\dots,x_{n-2}\}
and
\phi\colon X_{1}\to Y
the atomic bijection sending
x
to
x_{1}
. The restriction
\gamma_{|[0,t_{1}]}
provides an admissible path from
x
to
x_{1}
, thus, by the first part of this proof, there exists an admissible path
\Gamma_{1}
from
X_{1}
to
Y
which satisfies
\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{1})\leq\omega(n-1)\ell(\gamma_{|[0,t_{1}]})
. Concatenating
\Gamma_{0}
and
\Gamma_{1}
provides an admissible path
\Gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
between
X
and
Y
, such that
\card(\Gamma(t))
is
n
if
t\in[0,1-t_{1})
and
n-1
if
t\in[1-t_{1},1]
.
Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)

\displaystyle=\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{0})+\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma_{1})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)\ell(\gamma_{|[t_{1},1]})+\omega(n-1)\ell(\gamma_{|[0,t_{1}]})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)\ell(\gamma)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)d(x,y)+\epsilon

\displaystyle=\ell^{\omega}(R)+\epsilon,

which concludes the proof in the case of an atomic merge. The case of an atomic split follows by symmetry.
∎

Figure 3:
Sketch of the proof of
3.20
in the case
M=\mathbb{R}
. The upper panels show the case of an atomic bijection from
X
to
Y
in
\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R})
. The upper left panel shows the path
\gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
from
x
to
y
that crosses other points in
X
. The upper right panel shows the path
\Gamma
from
X
to
Y
in
\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R})
constructed from
\gamma
. Its cardinality is constant and equal to 4 on
[0,1]
. The lower panels show the case of an atomic merge from
X
to
Y
. The lower left panel shows the path
\gamma
from
x
to
y
that crosses other points in
X
. The lower right panel shows the first step in constructing a path
\Gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, with
\Gamma_{0}\colon[0,1-t_{1}]\to\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R})
from
X
to
X_{1}
, and
\Gamma_{1}
constructed by applying the bijection case to the remainder of the path.

Theorem 3.21
.

Let
M
be a length space satisfying the Heine-Borel property, and such that any for any two points
x,y\in M
there exists an admissible path
\gamma
between
x
and
y
which realizes the distance between
x
and
y
.

Then, for any
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and any weight
\omega
, there exists a MBS-path between
X
and
Y
,
\Gamma\colon[a,b]\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, such that
\ell^{\omega}(\Gamma)=d^{\omega}(X,Y)
.

Proof.

Since
M
satisfies the Heine-Borel property,
2.24
gives the existence of a simple MBS chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
between
X
and
Y
such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})=d^{\omega}(X,Y)
. By
3.19
, we may consider instead a chain
(P_{0},\dots,P_{l-1})
with the same length (since it can’t possibly be shorter) but such that the
P_{i}
are all atomic merges, bijections or splits. Now, for each
P_{i}
, consider the unique pair
x_{i}\not=y_{i}
such that
(x_{i},y_{i})\in P_{i}
. By assumption, there exists an admissible path in
M
,
\gamma_{i}
between
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
whose length is exactly
d(x_{i},y_{i})
. Observe that providing such a path in the proof of
3.20
yields a path in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
whose length is exactly
\ell^{\omega}(P_{i})
. Concatenation of those paths thus yields the desired path in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.
∎

Remark 3.22
.

The hypothesis in
3.21
are certainly not sharp. Consider for example the space
M=(0,1)
and
N=[0,1]
, both with the expected metric and length structure, and some weight
\omega
. Geodesics exist in both
M
and
N
, yet
M
does not satisfy the Heine-Borel property, hence
3.21
does not apply to it. On the other hand, if
X,Y\subset M
are two configurations, then applying
3.21
to
N
gives a geodesic path
\Gamma\colon[0,1]\to\mathrm{Ran}(N)
between
X
and
Y
. It is clear that such a geodesic is in fact contained in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and in turn that it is a geodesic in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, since distances in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
are bounded below by distances in
\mathrm{Ran}(N)
. This shows that geodesics exist in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
despite
M
not satisfying the Heine-Borel property. This hypothesis is certainly already too strong in
2.24
, which we use in the proof of
3.21
, though in the former it is unclear what weaker hypothesis could take its place. In
3.21
however, we conjecture that it is already enough for geodesics to exist in the underlying space
M
.

4
Examples and computations

This section aims to be less abstract and more practical. We illustrate the definitions and results of the previous sections on some examples, and with some drawings. Finding the actual infimum in the definition of the weighted distance may be a non-trivial optimization problem in some cases. We thus present simple cases, for which we can compute the distance and find the geodesic chain/path that realises it, or at least give a combinatorial chain that reaches a reasonable upper bound of the distance. Note that, even if identifying a geodesic chain in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
may be quite intuitive in some case, showing that it is indeed geodesic often turns out to be a bit more difficult.

In the following sections, we will assess the topological properties of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
based on the convergence properties of sequences in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We thus spend some time in this section to define some illustrative sequences in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and assess their convergence properties with respect to various topologies. In particular,
4.4
will allow to distinguish between distinct weighted topologies, and
4.5
shows that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
is not complete, as a metric space.

Eventually, we discuss the topological space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
equipped with the topology induced by
d^{\omega}
, seen as an invariant of
M
. In particular, we show that, unlike the Hausdorff or final topology, the weighted topology does not allow to lift arbitrary continuous maps
f\colon(M,d_{M})\to(N,d_{N})
to continuous maps between the corresponding Ran spaces. Therefore
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is not necessarily an invariant of the homeomorphism type of
M
, but a finer invariant. Besides, this also shows that
(\mathrm{Ran}(-),\tau^{\omega})
is not functorial on Top, the category of topological spaces and continuous maps. Nevertheless, we show in
4.19
that a locally Lipschitz applications
f\colon(M,d_{M})\to(N,d_{N})
lifts to a locally Lipschitz application
\mathrm{Ran}(f)\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(N),\tau^{\omega})
, showing that
(\mathrm{Ran}(-),\tau^{\omega})
does define a functor on the category of metric spaces and locally Lipschitz maps. To prove this, we define the notion of locality, that simplifies the computation of distances in the neighborhoods of configurations of cardinality
n\geq 2
. This notion of locality will be very useful in
Section

7
.

4.1
Examples

We first give examples where we can compute the
\omega
-distance and find the associated geodesic chain/path, or a combinatorial chain that gives a reasonable upper bound of the distance. Through these practical computations and drawings, we hope to give the reader an intuitive picture of the important properties of the space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
, and of the distance
d^{\omega}
.

We first illustrate, on a very simple example for
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, that the geodesic chain that realises the distance may depend on the chosen weight.

Example 4.1
.

0
1
1
-1
X
Y
Y
Z_{1}
Z_{2}
Z_{10}

Figure 4:
Sketch of
4.1
. Configurations
X=\{(0,0)\}
is in red, configuration
Y=\{(1,1),(1,-1)\}
is in black, and the intermediate configuration
Z_{\omega(2)}
is in blue for three values of
\omega(2)=1
,
2
and
10
. Dotted lines indicate the corresponding geodesic paths between
Y
and
X
.

This example illustrates that, given two configurations
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, the geodesic chain between
X
and
Y
, if it exists, may depend on the weight
\omega
chosen to compute the distance. Let us illustrate this for
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
X=\{(0,0\}
,
Y=\{(1,1),(1,-1)\}
. By
2.24
, for any weight
\omega
, the distance
d^{\omega}(X,Y)
can be computed as the minimum over the
\omega
-length of MBS-chains between
Y
and
X
, i.e. a merge
R_{\omega}
followed by a, possibly constant, bijection
P_{\omega}
:
((Y,Z_{\omega},X),(R_{\omega},P_{\omega}))
.
Writing
Z_{\omega}=(z_{1},z_{2})
, we get the following (recall that
\omega(1)=1
by definition).

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{\omega},P_{\omega})=\omega(2)\left[\sqrt{(1-z_{1})^{2}+(1-z_{2})^{2}}+\sqrt{(1-z_{1})^{2}+(1+z_{2})^{2}}\right]+\sqrt{z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}}

First note that we can take
Z_{\omega}
in the convex hull of
X\cup Y
. Second, note that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{\omega},P_{\omega})
is even in
z_{2}
, and thus has an extremum when
z_{2}=0
. It is easy to see that it is indeed a minimum (one can for example compute the partial second derivative with respect to
z_{2}
and check that it is non negative). We can thus restrict ourselves to the case
z_{1}\in[0,1]
and
z_{2}=0
to get

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{\omega},P_{\omega})=2\omega(2)\sqrt{(1-z_{1})^{2}+1}+z_{1}

We finally get a minimum when

\displaystyle z_{1}=1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\omega(2)^{2}-1}}

This example is illustrated on
Fig.

4
, where the intermediate configuration
Z_{\omega}
is displayed for
\omega(2)=1,2,10
. We see that, as
\omega(2)
increases, the intermediate singleton
Z_{\omega}
gets closer and closer to
(1,0)
.

This example is also useful to better understand the importance of the Heine-Borel hypothesis in
2.24
and
3.21
. Indeed, if, for a given
\omega
, we remove the point of
Z_{\omega}
from
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, then
N=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus Z_{\omega}
is still locally compact, but it does not satisfy the Heine-Borel property. And we directly see that no geodesic chain exist in
\mathrm{Ran}(N)
between
X
and
Y
for
d^{\omega}
, although one exists for any other weight
\chi
such that
\chi(2)\neq\omega(2)
. Similarly, no geodesic path exist in
\mathrm{Ran}(N)
between
X
and
Y
for
d^{\omega}
, although one exists for any other weight
\chi
such that
\chi(2)<\omega(2)
. Now if we remove the entire
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{*}
axis, then
N=\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus(0,1]\times\{0\}
is no longer locally compact nor does it satisfy Heine-Borel, and this time, no geodesic chain nor path exist between
X
and
Y
for any choice of weight. A similar case is obtained for the locally compact space
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus Z_{1}
, with
Z_{1}
the singleton corresponding to the minimum weight
\omega(2)=1
.

We then illustrate that, even in one of the most simple examples one could think of, i.e.
M=[0,1]
, it is not always straightforward to compute the
\omega
-distance between configurations.

Example 4.2
.

Let
\omega
be a weight, and consider
M=[0,1]
, equipped with the distance given by
d(x,y)=|x-y|
. Let us estimate the distance of a configuration
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
to the singleton
X_{0}=\{x_{0}=0\}
. Let us define
n=\card(X)
, and
X=\{x_{1}<\dots<x_{n}\}
.

Let us first consider the MBS-chain,
(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})
, where
R_{i}
merges
\{x_{i},x_{i-1}\}
at
x_{i-1}
for any
2\leq i\leq n
, and
R_{1}=\{(x_{1},0)\}
is a, possibly constant, bijection.
This allows us to give an upper bound of
d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})
, as

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega(i)\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega(i)d(x_{i},x_{i-1})

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)d(x_{n},x_{0})=\omega(n)x_{n}.

In the case where
\omega
is the constant weight equal to
1
, let us show that we actually have
d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})=x_{n}
. Indeed, by
2.24
, there exists a geodesic chain
((X=Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}=\{0\}),(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1}))
from
X
to
\{0\}
. By surjectivity of the
P_{i}
, for
0\leq i\leq k-1
, there must exist a sequence of
y_{i}\in Y_{i}
, such that
y_{0}=x_{n}
,
y_{k}=0
and for
0\leq i\leq k-1
,
y_{i}P_{i}y_{i+1}
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})=\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1})\geq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(\card(P_{i}))d(y_{i},y_{i+1})\geq d(x_{n},0)=x_{n}

So that the MBS-chain
(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})
defined above is actually a geodesic chain.

This does not hold in the general case. Consider the case
X=\{0,0.1,1\}
. The previously defined MBS-chain gives

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{3},R_{2},R_{1})

\displaystyle=0.9\omega(3)+0.1\omega(2).

with
R_{1}
being the identity.
Now let us define the chain
(Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3})
where
Q_{1}
merges
\{0,0.1\}
at 0.1,
Q_{2}
merges
\{1,0.1\}
at 0.1, and
Q_{3}
is the bijection sending 0.1 to 0.

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3})=0.1\omega(3)+0.9\omega(2)+0.1.

Note that, as soon as
\omega(3)>\omega(2)+0.125
, then
\ell^{\omega}(Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3})<\ell^{\omega}(R_{3},R_{2},R_{1})
, and the latter is not a geodesic chain anymore.

Nevertheless, if the points of the configuration
X\in\mathrm{Ran}([0,1])
are such that their pairwise distances are non increasing, i.e. if they get closer to each other as they get further away from
0
, as sketched on
Fig.

5
, then we can compute the distance to the singleton
\{0\}
. This is the subject of the following example.

Example 4.3
.

Consider the previous example, in the particular case where
x_{2}-x_{1}\geq x_{3}-x_{2}\geq\dots\geq x_{n}-x_{n-1}
. We will show that the chain
(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})
previously defined is indeed a geodesic chain. So that we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(k)d(x_{k-1},x_{k}).

By
2.24
there exists a geodesic chain
((X=Y_{n},\dots,Y_{0}=\{0\}),(P_{n},\dots,P_{1}))
from
X
to
\{0\}
, where
P_{k}
is a simple merge for any
2\leq k\leq n
and
P_{1}
is a possibly constant bijection. Let us first show that, for any
0\leq m\leq n-1
we have
