\displaystyle\sum_{k=n-m}^{n}\ell(R_{k})\leq\sum_{k=n-m}^{n}\ell(P_{k}).

The case
m=n-1
corresponds to the constant weight equal to
1
, and was covered in the previous example. Let
0\leq m<n-1
. Since the
P_{k}
are all merges for
n-m\leq k\leq n
, they all are graphs of surjective maps
p_{k}\colon Y_{k}\to Y_{k-1}
. Let us denote
p=p_{n-m}\circ\dots\circ p_{n}
, and remark that
\{p^{-1}(y)\mid y\in Y_{n-m-1}\}
forms a partition of
X
. For
y\in Y_{n-m-1}
let us define
x_{\mathrm{min}}(y)=\min\{p^{-1}(y)\}
and
x_{\mathrm{max}}(y)=\max\{p^{-1}(y)\}
, and observe that if
y\in X
and
y
has not been merged with any other points of
X
after
m
step, then
x_{\mathrm{max}}(y)=x_{\mathrm{min}}(y)=y
.
Now, by definition, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\ell(P_{j})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\sum_{x\in Y_{j}}d(x,p_{j}(x))

\displaystyle=\sum_{x\in Y_{n}=X}d(x,p_{n}(x))+\sum_{x\in Y_{n-1}}d(x,p_{n-1}(x))+\dots+\sum_{x\in Y_{n-m}}d(x,p_{n-m}(x)).

Let us construct a very coarse bound on this sum. Consider the elements in
Y_{n}
of the form
x_{\mathrm{max}}(y)
and
x_{\mathrm{min}}(y)
, for some
y\in Y_{n-m-1}
. If
x_{\mathrm{max}}(y)=x_{\mathrm{min}}(y)
, then nothing has merged at
y
yet, and
y
does not contribute in any way to the sum.
Otherwise, we may consider the sequences
x_{\mathrm{max}}(y)=z_{n},\dots,z_{m-n-1}=y
and
x_{\mathrm{min}}(y)=w_{n},\dots,w_{m-n-1}=y
, where
p_{j}(z_{j})=z_{j-1}
and
p_{j}(w_{j})=w_{j-1}
, for
n\geq j\geq n-m
. Observe that, if
z_{j}\not=w_{j}
, then both
d(z_{j},z_{j-1})
and
d(w_{j},w_{j-1})
appear in the sum. Furthermore, for some
j
, at the latest for
j=n-m-1
, we must have
z_{j}=w_{j}
.
Thus, by the triangular inequality, those terms must sum to something bigger than
d(x_{\mathrm{max}}(y),x_{\mathrm{min}}(y))
. Observing that those sequences are disjoint for distinct values of
y\in Y_{n-m-1}
gives

\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\ell(P_{j})\geq\sum_{y\in Y_{n-m-1}}d(x_{\mathrm{max}}(y),x_{\mathrm{min}}(y))

Now, by assumption, each of the
P_{j}
is a simple merge, and thus
\card(Y_{n-m-1})=n-m-1
. Thus, the partition of
X
,
\{p^{-1}(y)\mid y\in n-m-1\}
must satisfy :

\sum_{y\in Y_{n-m-1}}\left(\card(p^{-1}(y))-1\right)=n-(n-m-1)=m+1

Now, given some
y\in Y_{n-m-1}
whose preimage under
p
is not reduced to a singleton, we can consider the order on
p^{-1}(y)=\{x_{i_{1}}<\dots<x_{i_{k_{y}}}\}
with
x_{i_{1}}=x_{\mathrm{min}}(y)
and
x_{i_{k_{y}}}=x_{\mathrm{max}}(y)
. Observe that

d(x_{\mathrm{max}}(y),x_{\mathrm{min}}(y))=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{y}-1}(x_{i_{j+1}}-x_{i_{j}})

and that exactly
\card(p^{-1}(y))-1
term appear in this sum. Furthermore, if
y^{\prime}\not=y\in Y_{n-m-1}
, then
d(x_{\mathrm{max}}(y^{\prime}),x_{\mathrm{min}}(y^{\prime}))
is equal to a similar sum, but with no
x_{i}
in common. We can summarize this as follows. Consider
S
the set of pairs
(x,x^{\prime})
where
x,x^{\prime}\in p^{-1}(y)
for some
y\in Y_{n-m-1}
, and
x
is the successor of
x^{\prime}
in
p^{-1}(y)
. By the previous observations,
\card(S)=m+1
, each
x\in X
appears at most once as the first coordinate in a pair in
S
, and

\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\ell(P_{j})\geq\sum_{(x,x^{\prime})\in S}d(x,x^{\prime}).

Now, let
T=\{(x_{j},x_{j-1})\mid n\geq j\geq n-m\}
. We have, by construction

\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\ell(R_{j})=\sum_{(x,x^{\prime})\in T}d(x,x^{\prime}).

To conclude the first part of this proof, define orders on
S
and
T
, via
(x,x^{\prime})\preceq(z,z^{\prime})\Leftrightarrow x\geq z
. Then, listing the pair in order, the
k
-th pair in
S
,
(x,x^{\prime})
must satisfy
x\leq x_{n-k+1}
, and
x^{\prime}<x
. Thus, by hypothesis on
X
, we must have
d(x,x^{\prime})\geq d(x_{n-k+1},x_{n-k})
. And finally, the
k
-th pair in
S
is further apart than the
k
-th pair in
T
, which gives the desired inequality :

\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\ell(P_{j})\geq\sum_{j=n-m}^{n}\ell(R_{j}).

We can now rearrange a sum:

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(k)\ell(R_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left[\omega(1)+\sum_{m=2}^{k}\left(\omega(m)-\omega(m-1)\right)\right]\ell(R_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{n}\left[\sum_{m=2}^{k}\left(\omega(m)-\omega(m-1)\right)\right]\ell(R_{k})+\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(1)\ell(R_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{m=2}^{n}\left[\left(\omega(m)-\omega(m-1)\right)\sum_{k=m}^{n}\ell(R_{k})\right]+\omega(1)\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ell(R_{k})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{m=2}^{n}\left[\left(\omega(m)-\omega(m-1)\right)\sum_{k=m}^{n}\ell(P_{k})\right]+\omega(1)\sum_{k=1}^{n}\ell(P_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(P_{n},\dots,P_{1}).

and finally
\ell^{\omega}(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})\leq\ell^{\omega}(P_{n},\dots,P_{1})
thus
(R_{n},\dots,R_{1})
is indeed a geodesic chain, and
d^{\omega}(X,\{0\})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(k)d(x_{k-1},x_{k})
.

We now have everything to define a sequence of configurations of increasing cardinality, that converges to a singleton for a given weight, but that may not converge for other, larger, weights. This is done in the following example and will be useful later as it will allow to distinguish between weighted topologies associated to different weights.

Example 4.4
.

Let
\omega
be a weight, and let
M=[0,1]
. Let us fix a sequence of positive real numbers which converges to
0
,
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, and let us define, for
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
1\leq k\leq n
, and
1\leq i\leq n
the sequences

\displaystyle\epsilon_{n,k}

\displaystyle=\frac{u_{n}}{n\omega(k)}

\displaystyle x_{n,i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{i}\epsilon_{n,k}

and consider the sequence of configurations in
M
,

X_{n}=\left\{x_{n,i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\right\}

A sketch is given on
Fig.

5
.

Observe that we have, for any
n\geq 1
,
x_{n,2}-x_{n,1}\geq x_{n,3}-x_{n,2}\geq\dots\geq x_{n,n}-x_{n,n-1}
. We can thus use
4.3
to show that

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X_{n},\{0\})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega(i)d(x_{n,i-1},x_{n,i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\omega(i)\frac{u_{n}}{n\omega(i)}

\displaystyle=u_{n}

which converges to zero. Thus, the sequence
X_{n}
converges to
\{0\}
for the topology
\tau^{\omega}
. Note however that
\card(X_{n})=n
diverges to infinity.

Finally, remark that the sequence
(X_{n})
may not converge for other weighted topologies. For example if we have an increasing sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
in
\mathbb{R}
such that
(u_{n}v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does not go to zero, and if we define the weight
\chi(n)=v_{n}\omega(n)
then
d^{\chi}(X_{n},\{0\})
does not go to zero, so the sequence
(X_{n})
does not converge towards
\{0\}
for the topology
\tau^{\chi}
. This will be useful in the following section, when we compare the different weighted topologies.

\epsilon_{5,1}
\epsilon_{5,2}
\epsilon_{5,5}

Figure 5:
Sketch of two configurations of
4.4
,
\{0\}
as a red square
{\color[rgb]{0.9336,0.3984,0.4648}\blacksquare}
and
X_{5}
as black dots

.

This example illustrates that, similarly to the Hausdorff topology and unlike the final topology, the weighted topology may have sequences that converge even though their cardinality goes to infinity. The subtlety is that, as we will see in
5.11
, for any such sequence, we can always construct a weighted topology for which it will not converge. This will ensure that the only sequences that converge for all the weighted topology have a bounded cardinality. In particular, the weighted topologies collectively mimic the behavior of the final topology. However, since the data of converging sequences is not sufficient to characterize a topology in general, some more work will be needed to show that the final topology is the limit of the weighted topologies.

Another similarity between the weighted and Hausdorff topology is that the metric space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
is not complete. This is illustrated in the following example by defining a Cauchy sequence for
d^{\omega}
that converges for
d_{\mathrm{H}}
to a Cantor set. Consequently, it cannot converge for
d^{\omega}
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. The convergence and limits of Cauchy sequences for various weights will be more thoroughly studied in
Section

6
. In particular we will show later that the completion of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
, as a set, may be identified with a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
.

Example 4.5
.

0
1
X_{0}
X_{1}
X_{2}
X_{3}
S_{1}
S_{2}
S_{3}

Figure 6:
Sketch of the Cauchy sequence in
4.5
. The "left" sequence
L_{n}
are drawn as red dots

, the "right" sequence
R_{n}
as blue squares
{\color[rgb]{0,0.4453,0.6992}\blacksquare}
, and the split
S_{n}
from
X_{n-1}
to
X_{n}
as purple arrows
{\color[rgb]{0.6006,0.3545,0.4922}\to}
.

Here we consider
M=[0,1]
and we give an example of a Cauchy sequence in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
that does not converge. Note that this proves that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is not complete.

Let
\omega
be a weight. We will define our Cauchy sequence as the union of two "left" and "right" sequences. Note that, in the case where
\omega
is the constant weight equal to one, then these sequences actually correspond to the left and right boundaries of the intervals used to define the Cantor set. Let us define
L_{0}=\{0\}
,
R_{0}=\{1\}
, and, for any
n\geq 1

\displaystyle L_{n}

\displaystyle=L_{n-1}\bigcup\left(R_{n-1}-\frac{1}{3^{n}\omega(2^{n+1})}\right)

\displaystyle R_{n}

\displaystyle=R_{n-1}\bigcup\left(L_{n-1}+\frac{1}{3^{n}\omega(2^{n+1})}\right).

Finally, for any
n\geq 0
, let us define
X_{n}=L_{n}\cup R_{n}
. Note that
\card(X_{n})=2^{n+1}
. Let us first show that it is indeed a Cauchy sequence. Let
n\geq 1
, then there exists a split
S_{n}
from
X_{n-1}
to
X_{n}
that send every point in
X_{n-1}
to its closest point in
X_{n}
. This corresponds to splitting each point in
L_{n-1}
into itself plus its copy shifted by
\frac{1}{3^{n}\omega(2^{n+1})}
, and symmetrically for
R_{n-1}
. This reads

\displaystyle S_{n}=\left\{\left(x,x+\frac{1}{3^{n}\omega(2^{n+1})}\right)\mid x\in L_{n-1}\right\}\bigcup\left\{\left(x,x-\frac{1}{3^{n}\omega(2^{n+1})}\right)\mid x\in R_{n-1}\right\},

where we omitted the identity terms. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\leq\ell^{\omega}(S_{n})=\omega(2^{n+1})\sum_{(x,y)\in S_{n}}d(x,y)=\omega(2^{n+1})\frac{2^{n}}{3^{n}\omega(2^{n+1})}=\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{n}.

Since this is geometric,
X_{n}
is indeed a Cauchy sequence for
d^{\omega}
.

Let us now show that this sequence cannot converge in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
. Let us suppose that
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a limit of
X_{n}
, and let us denote
m=\card(X)
, and let
N\geq\log_{2}(m)
. Since
\card(X_{N})=2^{N+1}>m
, there must exist
x_{N}\in X_{N}
such that
x_{N}\not\in X
. Now, for any
n\geq N
, we have
X_{N}\subset X_{n}
, so that we also have
x_{N}\in X_{n}
. So that
d^{\omega}(X_{n},X)\geq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},X)\geq d(x_{N},X)
, which cannot converge to zero. So that
X_{n}
cannot converge to
X
.

Note that all the previously defined sequences are also Cauchy sequences for the Hausdorff distance. In that case, we can show that
X_{n}
converges in the set of compact subset of
M
, for the Hausdorff distance.

Remark 4.6
.

We can generalize
4.2
,
4.3
,
4.4
and
4.5
to the case where
M
is a length space such that there exist
x_{0}\neq x_{1}\in X
and an admissible path
\gamma\colon[a,b]\to M
such that
\gamma(a)=x_{0}
,
\gamma(b)=x_{1}
, and
\ell(\gamma)=d(x_{0},x_{1})=L
. We will say that such a space is
equipped with a geodesic
. Up to a rescaling of the length structure on
M
we can suppose that
L=1
. Then, remark that, for any
0\leq t\leq 1
there exists a unique
x_{t}\in\gamma([a,b])
such that
d(x,x_{0})=t
. Let us write
f\colon[0,1]\to M
the application that assigns
x_{t}
to
t\in[0,1]
. Finally, we just need to apply
f
to the previous examples.

Finally this last example illustrates why
(\mathrm{Ran}(-),\tau^{\omega})
is not functorial on Top, and why it is not, in general, an invariant of the homeomorphism type of
M
. This constitutes a remarkable distinction with the Hausdorff and final topology.

Example 4.7
.

Let
M=\mathbb{R}^{+}
, and let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be the square root function. Then
f
is indeed an homeomorphism, but it is not locally Lipschitz at zero. Let us show that there exists a weight
\omega
such that the map

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(f)\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})

\displaystyle\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto f(X)

is not continuous at
\{0\}
.

Let us define the sequence of configurations
(X_{n})
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, where for any
n\in\mathbb{N}

X_{n}=\{ku_{n}\mid 1\leq k\leq n\}
,
u_{n}=\e^{-3n/2}
. And let us define the weight
\omega
as
\omega(n)=\e^{n}
for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Remark that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
, we can apply
4.3
to get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X_{n},\{0\})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(k)u_{n}

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)nu_{n}=n\e^{-n/2}.

Therefore,
X_{n}
converges to
\{0\}
in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
.

Now, remark that we can also apply
4.3
to compute the distance from
f(X_{n})
to
f(\{0\})=\{0\}
to get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(f(X_{n}),\{0\})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(k)\left(\sqrt{ku_{n}}-\sqrt{(k-1)u_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\omega(n)\sqrt{u_{n}}\left(\sqrt{n}-\sqrt{(n-1)}\right)=\omega(n)\frac{\sqrt{u_{n}}}{\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{n-1}}

\displaystyle\geq\omega(n)\frac{\sqrt{u_{n}}}{2\sqrt{n}}=\frac{\e^{n/4}}{2\sqrt{n}},

so that
f(X_{n})
does not converge to
f(\{0\})
. Therefore,
\mathrm{Ran}(f)
is not continuous at
\{0\}
.

4.2
Local computations

In this section we define the notion of locality, that is very handy to make calculations close to configurations of cardinality greater that 1. The idea is to identify the size of neighborhoods
U
of
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
under which the configurations
Y\in U
can be considered "localized" around the points of
X
, i.e. every point in
Y
is close to a unique point in
X
and every point in
X
is close to at least a point in
Y
. In the following, we will call
clusters
the elements of the induced partition on
Y
.

We also extend the notion of locality to combinatorial chains by asking that the chain only sends points localized around a given
x\in X
to points localized around the same
x\in X
. This will be useful to lift local properties of
M
and of applications on
M
to local properties of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and of applications on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Throughout this section, we fix
(M,d)
a metric space.

Let us state a useful lemma, that, together with the following corollary, give the intuition why any small enough neighborhood of a given configuration
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
only contains configurations of cardinality greater than
M
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. If
\card(X)>\card(Y)
then

d^{\omega}(X,Y)\geq\omega(\card(X))\min_{x\neq z\in X}\{d(x,z)\}.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
. There exists a MBS-chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})
from
X
to
Y
such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\epsilon
. Since
\card(X)>\card(Y)
, then
R_{0}
must be a simple merge between two points
x\neq w\in X
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\geq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})-\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})-\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq\omega(\card(X))d(x,w)-\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq\omega(\card(X))\min_{x\neq z\in X}\{d(x,z)\}-\epsilon

Since this is true for any
\epsilon>0
, we have the desired result.
∎

This lemma allows to derive the following corollary, which is a concrete way of interpreting
1.24
.

Corollary 4.9
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration, with
\card(X)=n
. Then any small enough neighborhood of
X
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, is contained in
\mathrm{Ran}_{\geq n}(M)
.

Proof.

Let
r=\omega(\card(X))\min_{x\neq y\in X}\{d(x,y)\}.
By
4.8
, any
Y\in B^{\omega}(X,r)
must have cardinality at least
n
.
∎

The notion of locality actually derives from the Hausdorff distance. It will therefore be useful to compare the weighted distance to the Hausdorff distance.

Proposition 4.10
.

Let
\omega\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[0,\infty)
be a weight, and
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Then
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)
. In particular, for any
r>0
,
B^{\omega}(X,r)\subset B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
.

Proof.

There exists
(x,y)\in X\times Y
such that
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)=d(x,y)
. By symmetry, we may assume that
d(x,y)=d(x,Y)
, so that we have
d(x,y^{\prime})\geq d(x,y)
for any
y^{\prime}\in Y
. Let
\epsilon>0
. There exists a combinatorial chain
((X=X_{0},\dots,X_{k}=Y),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
from
X
to
Y
such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\epsilon
. By surjectivity of
R_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
, there must exist a sequence
(x=x_{0},\dots,x_{k})
such that
x_{i}\in X_{i}
for any
0\leq i\leq k
and
x_{i}R_{i}x_{i+1}
for any
0\leq i\leq k-1
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\epsilon

\displaystyle\geq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(\card(R_{i}))d(x_{i},x_{i+1})

\displaystyle\geq d(x,x_{k})\geq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)

Since this is true for any
\epsilon>0
, we finally get
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)
.
∎

We arrive at the core notion of this section. Intuitively, we use the notion of locality to lift local properties of
M
onto
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We start by estimating the size of a neighborhood
X\in U
we need to ensure that the distance between configurations in
U
can be computed from combinatorial chains that stay sufficiently close to
X
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
\epsilon>0
, and
\omega
be a weight. Then, for any
Y,Z\in B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
, the distance
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)
can be computed from chains in
B^{\omega}(X,2\epsilon)
. In particular it can be computed from chains in
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2\epsilon)
.

Proof.

This is an easy application of the triangular inequality. Since
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)\leq d^{\omega}(Y,X)+d^{\omega}(Y,Z)<2\epsilon
, there must be some combinatorial chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{n})
between
Y
and
Z
whose
\omega
-length is strictly bounded by
2\epsilon
. Assume that some intermediary configuration,
Y_{k}
lies outside of
B^{\omega}(X,2\epsilon)
. Then,

\displaystyle 2\epsilon

\displaystyle>\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k})+\ell^{\omega}(R_{k+1},\dots,R_{n})

\displaystyle\geq d^{\omega}(Y,Y_{k})+d^{\omega}(Y_{k},Z)

\displaystyle>2\epsilon

Hence, the distance
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)
can be computed as the infimum of the
\omega
-length of chains between
Y
and
Z
which lie entirely in
B^{\omega}(X,2\epsilon)
. Finally, we get the special case by observing that, by
4.10

B^{\omega}(X,2\epsilon)\subset B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2\epsilon)

∎

We can deduce the following corollary that will be useful in
Section

7
.

Corollary 4.12
.

Let
(M,d)
be a metric space, and
V\subset M
be an open subspace. Then, the inclusion
V\to M
induces a map,
\mathrm{Ran}(V)\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, which is continuous for the
\omega
topologies, for any weight
\omega
. Furthermore, it is an open embedding.

Proof.

We extend the inclusion
V\to M
to a map between Ran spaces in the obvious way. That is, we consider the map sending a configuration
X\subset V
to the configuration
X\subset M
. Now, to show that it is continuous, consider
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(V)
a configuration. Since
V
was assumed to be open, there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that we have an inclusion
\cup_{x\in X}B(x,2\epsilon)\subset V
. This means that
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(V)
. But now, by
4.11
, we know that
B^{\omega}_{V}(X,\epsilon)=B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon)
, and that distances in both coincide. Thus, the inclusion induces an isometry when restricted on
B^{\omega}_{V}(X,\epsilon)
. In particular, it is continuous at
X
.
∎

To estimate if the points in
Y
are localized around the points in
X
we need an estimate of the minimal distance between distinct points in
X
. We call it the
merging radius
.

Definition 4.13
.

We define the
merging radius
function as

\displaystyle\merg\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle\to(0,+\infty]

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}\min_{x\neq y\in X}\{d(x,y)\}\quad&\text{if }\card(X)>1\\
1&\text{if }\card(X)=1\end{cases}.

Remark 4.14
.

Note that the merging radius function has discontinuities around merges. Note also that the value chosen for cardinality one is arbitrary and doesn’t affect any of the following proofs.

We can now define more precisely the locality of configuration, relations, and combinatorial chains.

Definition 4.15
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
0<r\leq\merg(X)
. We say that a configuration
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local
if
Y\subset\bigcup_{x\in X}B(x,r)
and
Y\cap B(x,r)\neq\emptyset
for all
x\in X
.

If
Y,Z\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
are two
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local configuration, we say that a relation
R\subset X\times Y
between
Y
and
Z
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local if
yRz
implies that there exists
x\in X
such that
y,z\in B(x,r)
.

We say that a combinatorial chain
((X_{0},\dots,X_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
in
M
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local if
X_{i}
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local for any
0\leq i\leq k
and if
R_{i}
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local for any
0\leq i\leq k-1
.

Remark 4.16
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
0<r\leq\merg(X)
. A configuration
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local if and only if
Y\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
. Moreover, if
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local then
\card(Y)\geq\card(X)
.

The previous
4.11
gives a condition that ensures that we can compute the length from chains whose configurations are local. In the following lemma we extend this result to the relations of the chains, to ensures that we can compute the length from chains that are local. Note that the hypothesis is slightly stronger.

Lemma 4.17
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
0<r\leq\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
and
\omega
a weight. For any
Y,Z\in B^{\omega}(X,r)
, the distance
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)
can be computed over the MBS-chains from
Y
to
Z
that are
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2r)
-local.

Proof.

Let
Y,Z\in B^{\omega}(X,r)
. First, remark that
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)\leq d^{\omega}(Y,X)+d^{\omega}(X,Z)<2r=\merg(X)
. Let
0<\epsilon<2r-d^{\omega}(Y,Z)
. By
4.11
there exists a MBS chain
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
from
Y
to
Z
such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq d^{\omega}(Y,Z)+\epsilon
and such that
Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}\in B^{\omega}(X,2r)\subset B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2r)
.

Let
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, and
(y_{i},y_{i+1})\in R_{i}
. Since
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
is a combinatorial chain from
Y
to
Z
, there exists
(y_{0},\dots,y_{k})\in Y_{0}\times\dots\times Y_{k}
such that
y_{j}R_{j}y_{j+1}
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
. In addition, for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
, since
Y_{j}
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2r)
-local, there exists a unique
x_{j}\in X
such that
d(y_{j},x_{j})=\min_{x\in X}\{d(y_{j},x)\}\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(Y_{j},X)\leq d^{\omega}(Y_{j},X)<2r
. If
\card(X)=1
, then
x_{j}=x_{0}
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
. Otherwise

\displaystyle d(x_{0},x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq d(x_{0},y_{0})+d(y_{0},y_{j})+d(y_{j},x_{j})

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{j-1})+d^{\omega}(Y_{j},X)

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)+\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{j-1})+d^{\omega}(Y_{j},Z)+d^{\omega}(Z,X)

\displaystyle<2\merg(X)=\min_{z\neq z^{\prime}\in X}\{d(z,z^{\prime})\}.

and thus
x_{j}=x_{0}
for all
j\in\{0,\dots,k\}
. In particular
x_{i}=x_{i+1}
, so that
(y_{i},y_{i+1})\in\left(B(x_{i},2r)\right)^{2}
and
R_{i}
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2r)
-local. The MBS chain
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
is thus
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2r)
-local. We can thus compute the infimum over the
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2r)
-local MBS-chains.
∎

Finally, the following lemma will be useful to show that we can lift a locally Lipschitz map on
M
to a locally Lipschitz map on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. It will also be useful in
Section

7
to compute the distance between configurations that are transformed under a homothety or a scaling on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
0<r<\merg(X)
, and let
\phi\colon\bigsqcup_{x\in X}B(x,r)\to M
be a map such that there exists
s\in[0,+\infty)
such that
d(\phi(y),\phi(z))\leq sd(y,z)
for all
(y,z)\in\bigsqcup_{x\in X}B(x,r)^{2}
. Let
Y,Z\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local configurations.

If the
\omega
-distance between
Y
and
Z
can be computed over
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local combinatorial chains from
Y
to
Z
then
d^{\omega}(\phi(Y),\phi(Z))\leq sd^{\omega}(Y,Z)
.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon>0
and let
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{k}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}))
be a
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local combinatorial chain from
Y
to
Z
such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq d^{\omega}(Y,Z)+\epsilon
. Let us denote
P_{i}=\phi^{2}(R_{i})
for all
0\leq i\leq k-1
, and let show that
((\phi(Y_{0}),\dots,\phi(Y_{k})),(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1}))
is a combinatorial chain from
\phi(Y)
to
\phi(Z)
. Let
i\in\{0,\dots,k-1\}
, and let
z\in\phi(Y_{i})
, there exists
y\in Y_{i}
such that
\phi(y)=z
. Since
R_{i}
is surjective, there exists
y^{\prime}\in Y_{i+1}
such that
yR_{i}y^{\prime}
, and thus
zP_{i}\phi(y^{\prime})
. Similarly, we show that for any
z\in\phi(Y_{i+1})
, there exists
z^{\prime}\in\phi(Y_{i})
such that
z^{\prime}P_{i}z
. This proves that
P_{i}
is surjective, and that
((\phi(Y_{0}),\dots,\phi(Y_{k})),(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1}))
is indeed a combinatorial chain from
\phi(Y)
to
\phi(Z)
. We can compute its length:

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(\card(P_{i}))\sum_{(z,z^{\prime})\in P_{i}}d(z,z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(\card(R_{i}))\sum_{(y,y^{\prime})\in R_{i}}d(\phi(y),\phi(y^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}\omega(\card(R_{i}))\sum_{(y,y^{\prime})\in R_{i}}sd(y,y^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq s\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1}).

Finally

d^{\omega}(\phi(X),\phi(Y))\leq\ell^{\omega}(P_{0},\dots,P_{k-1})\leq s\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k-1})\leq sd^{\omega}(X,Y)+s\epsilon

Since this is true for any
\epsilon>0
, we have
d^{\omega}(\phi(X),\phi(Y))\leq sd^{\omega}(X,Y)
.
∎

4.3
Functoriality and invariance

We can now use the previous examples and computations to show some general properties of the weighted topologies. In particular
4.7
illustrates that the lift
\mathrm{Ran}(f)
of a continuous map
f
is not always continuous. From this, we deduce two important differences with the Hausdorff and final topologies. Firstly the weighted topologies do not, in general, induce a functor on the category of metric spaces and continuous maps. Nevertheless, if one considers the subcategory of metric spaces and locally Lipschitz maps,
\mathcal{C}
, then for any weight,
\omega
, we do have a functor
\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}\colon\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
. Secondly, the homeomorphism type of
\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(M)
is not necessarily an invariant of the homeomorphism type of
M
, however it is still invariant in a more rigid sense, see
4.21
.

Proposition 4.19
.

Let
(M,d_{M})
and
(N,d_{N})
be two metric spaces and
f\colon M\to N
a locally Lipschitz map. Then for any
\omega
, the lifted map

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}(f)\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(N)

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto f(X)

is locally Lipschitz for the distances induced by
\omega
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
\mathrm{Ran}(N)
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. For any
x\in X
there exists
\epsilon_{x}>0
and
p_{x}
such that for any
y,z\in B_{M}(x,\epsilon_{x})
,
d_{N}(f(y),f(z))\leq p_{x}d_{M}(y,z)
. Let us define
\epsilon=\min\left(\{\epsilon_{x}\mid x\in X\}\cup\{\merg(X)\}\right)
, and
p=\max\{p_{x}\mid x\in X\}
. Then
f_{|\bigcup_{x\in X}B(x,\epsilon)}
is such that for any
(y,z)\in\bigcup_{x\in X}\left(B(x,\epsilon)^{2}\right)
, we have
d_{N}(f(y),f(z))\leq pd_{M}(y,z)
.

Let
Y,Z\in B^{\omega}(X,\frac{\epsilon}{2})
. By
4.17
, the distance
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)
can be computed over
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\epsilon)
-local MBS-chains, so that we can apply
4.18
to directly get that
d^{\omega}(f(Y),f(Z))\leq pd^{\omega}(Y,Z)
.
∎

Corollary 4.20
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be the category whose objects are metric spaces, and whose morphisms are locally Lipschitz maps, then, for any weight
\omega
the following functor is well defined

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}\colon\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{C}

\displaystyle(M,d)

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})

\displaystyle f\colon M\to N

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{Ran}(f)\colon\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(M)\to\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(N)

Remark 4.21
.

The previous corollary implies, in particular, that if
(M,d)
and
(N,d^{\prime})
are two metric spaces related by a locally bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism, then
\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(M,d)
and
\mathrm{Ran}^{\omega}(N,d^{\prime})
are related by a locally bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism.

5
Distances and topologies

Now that we defined and characterized new metrics on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, we spend some time, in this section, to compare the induced topologies between themselves, and to the previously known Hausdorff and final topologies. In particular, we show that there are uncountably many distinct weighted topologies on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
(
5.5
), that they all refine the Hausdorff topology and are all refined by the final topology
5.7
and
5.8
. If the case where the metric space
M
is locally compact, we also show that the weighted balls
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
form a basis of the final topology, i.e. that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is the limit in
\mathrm{Top}
of the
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})

5.9
. Finally, we use these tools to give new interpretations of some known properties of the final topology.

5.1
Weighted topologies

We first compare the weighted topologies.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mathbb{W}
be the preorder whose objects are weights,
\omega\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[1,\infty)
and where there is a relation
\omega\preceq\chi
if and only if, there exists some
N\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq N
,
\omega(n)\leq\chi(n)
. We also write
\mathbb{W}
for the categorification of the preorder
\mathbb{W}
, and define the functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Top}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})

\displaystyle\omega\preceq\chi

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{Ran}(\mathrm{Id})\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\chi})

Proposition 5.2
.

If
\omega,\chi\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[1,\infty)
are two weights such that
\omega\preceq\chi
, then the identity induces a continuous map

(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\chi})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega}).

In particular, the functor
\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)
is well-defined.

The proof will follow immediately from the following lemma

Lemma 5.3
.

Let
\omega,\chi\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[1,\infty)
be two weights. If the sequence
\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}
is bounded, then
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathrm{Ran},\tau^{\chi})\to(\mathrm{Ran},\tau^{\omega})
is Lipschitz and
\tau^{\chi}
is finer than
\tau^{\omega}
. In particular, if
\omega(n)=\chi(n)
for all but finitely many
n
, then the topologies
\tau^{\omega}
and
\tau^{\chi}
coincide.

Proof.

Let
\omega,\chi\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[1,\infty)
be two weights such that the sequence
\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}
is bounded. There exists
m>0
such that, for all
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\omega(n)\leq m\chi(n)
Now, let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be two configurations,
\epsilon>0
, and
(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})
be a chain whose
\chi
-length is at most
d^{\chi}(X,Y)+\epsilon
.
Then, we compute

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\omega(\card(R_{k}))\ell(R_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}m\chi(\card(R_{k}))\ell(R_{k})

\displaystyle=m\ell^{\chi}(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})

\displaystyle\leq md^{\chi}(X,Y)+m\epsilon

Since this holds for any
\epsilon>0
, we must have
d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq md^{\chi}(X,Y)
, which proves that
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\chi)\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is Lipschitz and that
\tau^{\chi}
is finer than
\tau^{\omega}
.

If
\omega(n)=\chi(n)
for all but finitely many
n
then we can define
m_{1}=\max\{\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\}
and
m_{2}=\max\{\frac{\chi(n)}{\omega(n)}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}\}
. Thus both
\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}
and
\frac{\chi(n)}{\omega(n)}
are bounded, so that
\tau^{\chi}
is finer than
\tau^{\omega}
and
\tau^{\omega}
is finer than
\tau^{\chi}
, i.e. they coincide.
∎

Proof of
5.2
.

Let
\omega
and
\chi
be two weights, and assume that
\omega\preceq\chi
. Then the sequence
\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}
is bounded so that, by
5.3
,
\tau^{\chi}
refines
\tau^{\omega}
and
\mathrm{Ran}(\mathrm{Id})
is indeed continuous.∎

Proposition 5.4
.

Let
M
be a length space equipped with a geodesic. Let
\omega,\chi
two weights. If the sequence
(v_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}}
defined as

\displaystyle v_{n}=\frac{\chi(n)}{\omega(n)}

goes to infinity, then
\tau^{\chi}
is stricly finer than
\tau^{\omega}
. In particular, for any weight
\omega
, there exists a weight
\chi
such that
\tau^{\chi}
is strictly finer than
\tau^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Since
\frac{\chi(n)}{\omega(n)}
goes to infinity, then
\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}
is bounded. Thus, by
5.3
,
\tau^{\chi}
is finer that
\tau^{\omega}
. Let us show that the two topologies differ.
For this, let us define the sequence
u_{n}=\frac{\omega(n)}{\chi(n)}
which converges to zero. By
4.6
, we can generalize
4.4
to define a sequence
X_{n}
of configurations in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
that converges towards
\{x_{0}\}
for the topology
\tau^{\omega}
, using the sequence
u_{n}
.

Besides, as shown in
4.4
, we have, for any
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle d^{\chi}(X_{n},\{x_{0}\})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\chi(i)d(x_{i-1},x_{i})=u_{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\chi(i)}{\omega(i)}\geq u_{n}\frac{\chi(n)}{\omega(n)}\geq 1

which does not go to zero. Therefore, the sequence
X_{n}
does not converge towards
\{x_{0}\}
for the
\tau^{\chi}
topology, so that these two topologies do not coincide.
∎

Remark 5.5
.

There exists an uncountable infinity of distinct topologies (we can for example take the geometric progressions of common ratio
q\geq 1
).

We also show that sequences of unbounded cardinality cannot converge for all weights.

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
(M,d)
be a metric space and let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of elements of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Assume that the sequence of cardinalities
(\card(X_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is unbounded, then there exists some weight
\omega
such that the sequence
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is not Cauchy for
d^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume that
(\card(X_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is strictly increasing, and write
n_{k}
for the corresponding sequence of cardinalities. Now, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
d_{k}=\merg(X_{k})
. By
2.16
, for any weight
\omega
and any
k>0
, the distance
d^{\omega}(X_{k},X_{k-1})
can be computed from MBS-chains from
X_{k}
to
X_{k-1}
. The first step must be a merge, since
n_{k}>n_{k-1}
, and thus the length of the first step is at least
\omega(n_{k})d_{k}
. Now, we see that defining
\omega
such that for all
k
,
\omega(n_{k})\geq\frac{1}{d_{k}}
gives a distance for which the sequence
(X_{k})
verifies
d^{\omega}(X_{k},X_{k-1})\geq 1
for any
k>0
, which cannot be Cauchy.
∎

5.2
Comparison to classical topologies

We now compare the weighted topologies to the previously known Hausdorff and final topologies. We first show that the weighted topologies lie in between the Hausdorff and the final topology.

Proposition 5.7
.

Let
\omega
be a weight. The topology
\tau^{\omega}
is finer that
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
.

Proof.

This a direct consequence of
4.10
.
∎

Proposition 5.8
.

Let
\omega
be a weight. The topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
is finer that
\tau^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
\epsilon>0
and let us show that
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
is open in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Let us fix
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and let us show that
\pi_{n}^{-1}(B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
is an open set of
M^{n}
. Let
\underline{y}=(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)})\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
,
Y=\pi_{n}(\underline{y})
, and let us define
\delta=\frac{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y)}{\omega(n)}
. Let
\underline{z}=(z^{(1)},\dots,z^{(n)})\in M^{n}
such that
d_{\Sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{z})<\delta
. We have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\underline{z}),X)

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\underline{z}),Y)+d^{\omega}(Y,X)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{n}\omega(n)d(z^{(j)},y^{(j)})+d^{\omega}(Y,X)

\displaystyle\leq\omega(n)d_{\Sigma}(\underline{z},\underline{y})+d^{\omega}(Y,X)

\displaystyle<\omega(n)\delta+d^{\omega}(Y,X)

\displaystyle<\epsilon.

So that
\pi_{n}(\underline{z})\in B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
and thus
B_{\Sigma}(\underline{y},\delta)\subset\pi_{n}^{-1}(B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
. This proves that
\pi_{n}^{-1}(B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
is indeed open for any
n\geq 1
and thus that
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
is open in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
∎

We now show that in the case where
M
is locally compact then the final topology is the limit of the weighted topologies.

Theorem 5.9
.

If the metric space
(M,d)
is locally compact, then the functor
\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)
defined in
5.1
admits
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
as a limit.

Remark 5.10
.

The result of
5.9
actually induces a uniformity on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. We detail this point of view in the following section.

Proof.

Given that all maps in the diagram
\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)
are underlied by identities, one needs to show that any map
f\colon N\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is continuous for the final topology if and only if it is continuous for
\tau^{\omega}
for all
\omega
. One direction is clear, since the map
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is always continuous. For the reciprocal, it will be sufficient to show that the collection of open balls for all weights form a basis for the final topology.

To show this, let us first assume that
M
is compact. Let
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be an open for the final topology, and
X\in U
. We need to exhibit a weight
\omega
and some
\epsilon>0
such that
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\subset U
. We will proceed inductively. Given a weight
\omega
,
\epsilon>0
and
k\geq 1
, let us denote
B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)
for the intersection
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq k}(M)
. Observe that, to define
B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)
one needs only know the value of
\omega
up to
k
. In particular, we may construct
\omega
inductively, and prove that
B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)\subset U
before constructing all of
\omega
. We will proceed as follows.

Let
n=\card(X)
. The case
n=1
is somewhat of a special case, so we leave it for the end. Assume
n\geq 2
, and set
\epsilon=\min_{x\not=y\in X}\{d(x,y)\}
. Then, if
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is such that
d^{\omega}(X,Y)<\epsilon
, for some weight
\omega
, then we must have
\card(Y)\geq n
by
4.8
. Thus, we see that whatever the weight
\omega
may be, we will have
B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)=\emptyset
, for any
k<n
. For convenience, we may thus define
\omega(n-1)=\dots=\omega(1)=1
.
Now, we will construct subsets
K_{k}\subset M^{k}
, together with
\omega(k)
, such that, for all
k\geq n
.

•

K_{k}
is compact

•

B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)\subset\pi_{k}(K_{k})\subset U

•

\pi_{k}(K_{k})\subset\pi_{k+1}(K_{k+1})
.

Which will give the claim, since the inclusion
B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)\subset U
for all
k
gives
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\subset U
.

For
k=n
, let
\underline{x}\in M^{n}
be such that
\pi_{n}(\underline{x})=X
. Since
U
is open in the final topology,
\pi_{n}^{-1}(U)
must be an open subset of
M^{n}
, containing
\underline{x}
.
We will denote it
U_{n}
from now on for simplicity. Thus, there exists some
\epsilon_{n}>0
such that
\overline{B_{\Sigma}}(\underline{x},\epsilon_{n})\subset U_{n}
.
Set
K_{n}=\overline{B_{\Sigma}}(\underline{x},\epsilon_{n})
. It is indeed compact since it is a closed subset of the compact space
M^{n}
(recall that we assumed
M
to be compact for now).
Set
\omega(n)=\max\{\frac{\epsilon}{\epsilon_{n}},1\}
, and let
Y\in B^{\omega}_{\leq n}(X,\epsilon)
. We have already seen that we must have
\card(Y)=n
, and the distance between
X
and
Y
can be computed through bijections only. But since there are finitely many of those, there must be some bijection
\phi\colon X\to Y
such that

d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\omega(n)\sum_{x\in X}d(x,\phi(x))<\epsilon

From which we deduce that

\sum_{x\in X}d(x,\phi(x))<\frac{\epsilon}{\omega(n)}\leq\epsilon_{n}

On the other hand, from
\phi
, we deduce a preferred lift of
Y
to
M^{n}
:
\underline{y}=(\phi(x^{(1)}),\dots,\phi(x^{(n)}))
, and thus we have

d_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\underline{y})=\sum_{x\in X}d(x,\phi(x))\leq\epsilon_{n}

This proves that
Y=\pi_{n}(\underline{y})\in\pi_{n}(K_{n})
, and thus that
B^{\omega}_{\leq n}(X,\epsilon)\subset\pi_{n}(K_{n})
.

Now, assume that the
K_{i}
and
\omega(i)
have all been constructed up to some value
k
, and let us extend the construction to
k+1
. First, observe that as the image of a compact,
\pi_{k}(K_{k})
is compact, thus closed in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
for any weighted topology. In particular,
C=\pi_{k+1}^{-1}(\pi_{k}(K_{k}))
must be a closed subset of
M^{k+1}
thus a compact subspace, since
M^{k+1}
is compact by assumption. Let us now consider the following subspaces of
M^{k+1}
for
i\geq 1

C_{i}=\{\underline{y}\in M^{n+1}\mid d_{\Sigma}(\underline{y},C)\leq\frac{1}{i}\}

By construction, the
C_{i}
are closed, thus compact, and we have
\cap_{i\geq 1}C_{i}=C
. Furthermore,
C\subset U_{k+1}
since by hypothesis,
\pi_{k}(K_{k})\subset U
. Thus, there must exist some
i\geq 1
such that
C_{i}\subset U_{k+1}
. We set
K_{k+1}=C_{i}
and
\omega(k+1)=\max\{i\epsilon,\omega(k)\}
.
K_{k+1}
is compact, and satisfies
\pi_{k+1}(K_{k+1})\subset U
by construction. Furthermore, we have
\pi_{k+1}^{-1}(\pi_{k}(K_{k}))\subset K_{k+1}
, thus
\pi_{k}(K_{k})\subset\pi_{k+1}(K_{k+1})
. Finally, let
Y\in B^{\omega}_{\leq k+1}(X,\epsilon)
. If
\card(Y)\leq k
, then
Y\in\pi_{k}(K_{k})\subset\pi_{k+1}(K_{k+1})
. Otherwise,
\card(Y)=k+1
. Consider a MBS-chain between
Y
and
X
,
(R_{0},\dots,R_{l})
, such that
\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{l})<\epsilon
. Since
\card(Y)>\card(X)
, the first step must be a merge, giving an intermediate configuration
Z
, with
\card(Z)\leq k
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(Y,X)\leq d^{\omega}(Y,Z)+d^{\omega}(Z,X)<\epsilon

In particular,
d^{\omega}(Z,X)<\epsilon
and thus
Z\in B^{\omega}_{\leq k}(X,\epsilon)\subset\pi_{k}(K_{k})
. On the other hand, if we chose a lift
\underline{y}\in M^{k+1}
of
Y
and let
f\colon Y\to Z
stand for the map underlying the merge
R_{0}
, we may take
\underline{z}=(f(y^{(1)}),\dots,f(y^{(k+1)}))
as a lift for
Z
. This gives

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0})=\omega(k+1)\sum_{y\in Y}d(y,f(y))=\omega(k+1)d_{\Sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{z})<\epsilon

From which we deduce that

d_{\Sigma}(\underline{y},\underline{z})<\frac{\epsilon}{\omega(k+1)}

Since we already know that
Z\in\pi_{k}(K_{k})
this gives
\underline{y}\in K_{k+1}
and thus
Y\in\pi_{k+1}(K_{k+1})
, which proves that
B^{\omega}_{\leq k+1}(X,\epsilon)\subset\pi_{k+1}(K_{k+1})
.
This completes the induction step, and we have thus constructed
\omega
and
\epsilon
such that
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\subset U
in the case where the ambient space
M
is compact and
\card(X)\geq 2
.

If
\card(X)=1
, the first step in the induction needs to be changed. But in that case, there is some
x\in M
such that
X=\{x\}
. We thus have,
U_{1}=\pi_{1}^{-1}(U)\subset M
is an open subset containing
x
, thus there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that
B(x,\epsilon)\subset\overline{B}(x,\epsilon)\subset U_{1}
, and we may take
K_{1}=\overline{B}(x,\epsilon)
. The rest of the proof goes through unchanged.

Finally, if
M
is not compact, but only locally compact. Let
X\in U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a point in an open subset for the final topology. Then,
X=\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}\}
for some points
x^{(i)}\in X
. Since
M
is locally compact, each of those points admits a compact neighborhood
K_{i}\subset M
for
1\leq i\leq n
. Fix in addition, for each
1\leq i\leq n
some open neighborhood of
x^{(i)}
,
V_{i}
such that
V_{i}\subset K
, and such that
V_{i}=B(x^{(i)},\epsilon_{i})
, for some
\epsilon_{i}>0
. Now consider the compact subset
K=\cup_{i}K_{i}\subset M
, and the subset
V=\cup_{i}V_{i}\subset K
which is open, both in
K
and in
M
, and let
U^{\prime}=U\cap\mathrm{Ran}(V)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. It is an open subset of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
since
\mathrm{Ran}(V)
is one (indeed, it is an open subset of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
for the Hausdorff topology).

Now, we need to distinguish
a priori
between open balls computed in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and open balls computed in
\mathrm{Ran}(K)
, since the distance between two configurations depends on the ambient space. Let us denote
B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon)
and
B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon)
for the balls computed in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
\mathrm{Ran}(K)
respectively. Consider
\epsilon=\frac{1}{2}\min\{\epsilon_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
. Now, observe that the Hausdorff distances coincide, whether they are computed in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
or in
\mathrm{Ran}(K)
, and that by construction,
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(K)
. Let us show the equality
B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon)=B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon)
. If
Y\in B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon)
, we have
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and
d^{\omega}_{M}(X,Y)\leq d^{\omega}_{K}(X,Y)<\epsilon
, which gives the inclusion
B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon)\subset B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon)
. On the other hand, if
Z\in B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon)
, then
Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(K)
. Furthermore,
d^{\omega}_{M}(X,Z)
can be computed in
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,2\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(K)
, by
4.11
, thus we have
d^{\omega}_{M}(X,Z)=d^{\omega}_{K}(X,Z)
, and the inclusion
B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon)\subset B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon)
.

We can now apply the already proven compact case to
U^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{Ran}(K)
. There exists
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, and we may assume
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
, and
\omega
such that
B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon^{\prime})\subset U^{\prime}
. By the above discussion, we have
B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon^{\prime})=B^{\omega}_{K}(X,\epsilon^{\prime})
, thus we have

B^{\omega}_{M}(X,\epsilon^{\prime})\subset U^{\prime}\subset U

Which is precisely what we set out to prove.
∎

We can now use the weighted distances and topologies to re-show some properties of the final topology. In particular, we first re-show that only the sequence of bounded cardinality can converge.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
M
be a metric space. Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
such that
\card(X_{n})
is unbounded. Then
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
does not converge in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
.

Proof.

By
5.6
, there exists a weight
\omega
such that
(X_{n})
does not converge for
\tau^{\omega}
. Since
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
is finer that
\tau^{\omega}
, the sequence
(X_{n})
does not converge for
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
∎

Finally,
5.9
gives an interesting interpretation for the observation that the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
is not first countable, at least when the underlying space
M
is suitably nice: given an arbitrary countable collection of open neighborhoods
(U_{i})
, a diagonalization argument allows one to construct neighborhoods which contain none of the
U_{i}
.

Proposition 5.12
.

Let
M
be a locally compact length space equipped with a geodesic, then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is not first countable.

Proof.

Let
X_{0}=\{x_{0}\}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a one point configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
a countable collection of neighborhoods of
X_{0}
. Let us show that it cannot constitute a basis of neighborhoods.

By
5.9
, for any
i\in\mathbb{N}
, there exist a weight
\omega_{i}
and
\epsilon_{i}>0
such that
B^{\omega_{i}}(X_{0},\epsilon_{i})\subset U_{i}
. We then define a weight
\chi
as

\chi(k)=\max\{{\omega_{i}(k)k\mid i\leq k}\},

for all
k\geq 1
. By construction, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
, and for all
k\geq i
, we have

\frac{\chi(k)}{\omega_{i}(k)}\geq k

so that, by
5.4
, the topology
\tau^{\chi}
is strictly finer that
\tau^{\omega_{i}}
, for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
. We deduce that for any
\epsilon>0
, we have for all
i\in\mathbb{N}
:

B^{\omega_{i}}(X_{0},\epsilon_{i})\not\subset B^{\chi}(X_{0},\epsilon).

Indeed, using
4.4
, we can construct points arbitrarily close to
X_{0}
for
d^{\omega_{i}}
which are arbitrarily far away from
X_{0}
for
d^{\chi}
.
We conclude that
(U_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
is not a basis of neighborhoods of
X_{0}
.

∎

6
Completeness of the final topology

The previous
5.9
is worth interpreting from the perspective of uniform spaces
[
8
,
18
]
: it directly allows to construct a uniformity on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
by defining entourages from the weighted distances (see
6.13
). Therefore, even if
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is not metrizable, we can still meaningfully discuss its completeness. However, the notion is a bit more subtle for uniform spaces since it does not only require Cauchy sequences to converge, but also Cauchy filters. The convergence of Cauchy sequences is actually a direct consequence of
5.6
:

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
M
be a complete metric space, and let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. If
(X_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence for all distances
d^{\omega}
, then
(X_{n})
converges in
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Proof.

By
5.6
, if
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence for all the distances
d^{\omega}
, then there exists
N\geq 1
such that
\card(X_{n})\leq N
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
By
4.10
,
(X_{n})
is a Cauchy sequence for the Hausdorff distance.
By
1.27
the Hausdorff topology is complete on the truncation
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq N}(M)
, so that
(X_{n})
converges in
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq N}(M)
for
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
. Besides, by
1.25
,
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
and
\tau_{\mathrm{H}}
coincide on the truncations, so that
(X_{n})
converges for
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
∎

On the other hand, convergence of Cauchy filters requires some more work. Our proof relies on
[
8
, Corollary of Prop. 10, Chap II.3.5]
and shows that we can obtain
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as a limit of complete uniform spaces. This requires a few definitions and lemmas to better characterize Cauchy sequences and their limits for the weighted topologies.

We start by extending the notion of combinatorial chain to define chains with an infinite number of relations and a finite length. This allows to extend the weighted distance
d^{\omega}
to the completion
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
and thus evaluate the distance between elements of
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
not only abstractly but from actual chains that we can manipulate. This then allows us to show in
6.10
that if two sequences are Cauchy for
d^{\omega}
and converge, for the Hausdorff distance, to the same compact subspace
K\subset M
, then their
\omega
-distance goes to zero. We can thus interpret
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
as a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
, and if
\omega\preceq\chi
then we can interpret
\mathcal{R}_{\chi}
as a subset of
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
. It therefore makes sense to consider the intersection
\bigcap_{\omega\in\mathbb{W}}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
. Moreover we also show in
6.12
that any element in this intersection must actually have a finite cardinality, so that
\bigcap_{\omega\in\mathbb{W}}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}=\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Finally, we show that the uniformity on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
obtained from
5.9
, is the same as the one obtained on
\bigcap_{\omega\in\mathbb{W}}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
by taking the limit of the
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
in the category of uniform spaces and uniformly continuous maps, which allows us to conclude.

For the entirety of
Section

6
, we assume that
M
is a complete and locally compact metric space.

6.1
Infinite Chains in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)

We start by defining infinite chains and use them to give upper bounds for the
\omega
-distance between their end points, or finite subsets of their end points, in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Definition 6.2
.

Let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite sequence of elements of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and
(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
a sequence such that for all
k
,
R_{k}
is a surjective relation from
X_{k}
to
X_{k+1}
. We say that
((X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})
is an
infinite
\omega
-chain
if

\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(R_{k})<\infty

If, in addition, there exists a compact subset
X_{\infty}\subset M
such that
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X_{\infty})\to 0
, then we say that
((X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})
is an infinite
\omega
-chain
between
X_{0}
and
X_{\infty}
.

In any case, we define the
\omega
-length of the chain by

\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(R_{k})

Remark 6.3
.

We show in the following proposition that all infinite
\omega
-chains are actually infinite
\omega
-chains between some
X_{0}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and some
X_{\infty}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
. We also give an explicit construction of the limit
X_{\infty}
from sequences extracted from the infinite chain.

Proposition 6.4
.

Let
((X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})
be an infinite
\omega
-chain. Let
S
denote the set of sequences
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
x_{k}\in X_{k}
and
x_{k}R_{k}x_{k+1}
for all
k
, and let us define

X_{\infty}=\{\lim(x_{k})\mid(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in S\}.

Then

•

(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence for
d^{\omega}

•

all the sequences in
S
are Cauchy sequences

•

((X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})
is an infinite
\omega
-chain between
X_{0}
and
X_{\infty}

Proof.

For the first claim, observe that for any
n>k
,
(R_{k},\dots,R_{n-1})
is a combinatorial chain, in the sense of
2.2
, between
X_{k}
and
X_{n}
, and we have
d^{\omega}(X_{k},X_{n})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{k},\dots,R_{n})\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{i})_{i\geq k})
, the latter tends to
0
as
k
goes to infinity, which proves that
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is Cauchy for
d^{\omega}
.

For the second claim, first note that if
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence in
S
, and
k<n
, then since
x_{i}R_{i}x_{i+1}
for any
k\leq i<n
, we have

\displaystyle d(x_{k},x_{n})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}d(x_{i},x_{i+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k}^{n-1}\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})

Since this last terms converges to
0
as
k
grows,
(x_{k})
is Cauchy, and
thus, all elements of
S
are Cauchy sequences in
M
.

Lastly, for the third claim, first remark that
M
was assumed to be complete, we thus may define
X_{\infty}=\{\lim(x_{k})\mid(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in S\}\subset M
. As a set of limits,
X_{\infty}
is automatically a complete and thus closed subspace of
M
, as can be deduced by diagonalizing sequences of sequences. Moreover, recalling the proof of
1.17
, we see that
X_{\infty}\subset K
where
K\subset M
is the compact subset containing all limits of all Cauchy sequences
(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
with
x_{k}\in X_{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since we know
K
to be compact,
X_{\infty}
must also be compact.

Now, let us prove that
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X_{\infty})
converges to
0
, by showing that

d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X_{\infty})\leq\sum_{i\geq k}\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})

Indeed, consider a sequence
(x_{i})_{i\geq k}
with
x_{i}R_{i}x_{i+1}
. Such a sequence must be Cauchy, and thus admits a limit,
x_{\infty}\in X_{\infty}
, and by the triangular inequality, we have

\displaystyle d(x_{k},x_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}d(x_{i},x_{i+1})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=k}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(R_{i})

Since every element of
X_{k}
can be completed into such a sequence, and every element in
X_{\infty}
is reached as the limit of such a sequence, this gives the desired inequality, and concludes this proof.
∎

We now show that, if the end points of the infinite chain have a finite cardinality, then their distance minors the length of the chain. This shows that we can extend the set over which the infimum is taken in
2.7
to infinite chains.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be an infinite chain between
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
X_{\infty}
. Assume that
X_{\infty}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, then

d^{\omega}(X,X_{\infty})\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})

Proof.

Let us fix some ordering of the points of
X_{\infty}=\{y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)}\}
. Then, by
6.4
, there exists
n
sequences
(x^{(1)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},\dots,(x^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
x^{(i)}_{k}\in X_{k}
and
x^{(i)}_{k}R_{k}x^{(i)}_{k+1}
for all
k\geq 0
and all
1\leq i\leq n
and such that
x^{(i)}_{k}
converges to
y^{(i)}
for all
i
. Now, let us construct a chain between
X_{0}
and
X_{\infty}
as follows. First, observe that since for any
i\not=j
the sequence
x^{(i)}_{k}
and
x^{(j)}_{k}
are Cauchy sequences converging to distinct limits, there must be some
k_{i,j}
such that for all
k^{\prime}>k_{i,j}

x^{(i)}_{k^{\prime}}\not=x^{(j)}_{k^{\prime}}
. Take
k_{0}
to be the max of all such
k_{i,j}
.
We already have a (finite) chain from
X=X_{0}
to
X_{k_{0}}
, together with
n
distinct elements in
X_{k_{0}}
, given by the
x^{(i)}_{k_{0}}
.
Let
m\geq n
be the cardinality of
X_{k_{0}}
, and let us order its elements
\{x^{(1)}_{k_{0}},\dots,x^{(n)}_{k_{0}},\dots,x^{(m)}_{k_{0}}\}
.
We may choose Cauchy sequences
(x^{(i)}_{k})_{k\geq k_{0}}
for all
n<i\leq m
which converge to some point
x^{(i)}_{\infty}
in
X_{\infty}
and satisfy
x^{(i)}_{k}R_{k}x^{(i)}_{k+1}
for all
k\geq k_{0}
.
For now, assume that for all
k\geq k_{0}
,
\{x^{(1)}_{k},\dots,x^{(n)}_{k},\dots,x^{(m)}_{k}\}
is of cardinality
m
. Then, for any
k\geq k_{0}
, we have

\ell^{\omega}(R_{k})\geq\sum_{i=1}^{m}d(x^{(i)}_{k},x^{(i)}_{k+1}),

and in particular
\card(R_{k})\geq m
.
Now, define the surjective map
f\colon X_{k_{0}}\to X_{\infty}
sending
x^{(i)}_{k_{0}}
to the limit of the sequence
x^{(i)}_{k}
for all
1\leq i\leq m
. Its graph gives a surjective relation
R^{\prime}
and we have a chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1},R^{\prime})
between
X_{0}
and
X_{\infty}
which satisfies

\displaystyle\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1},R^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1})+\omega(m)\sum_{i=1}^{m}d(x^{(i)}_{k_{0}},x^{(i)}_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1})+\omega(m)\sum_{i=1}^{m}\left(\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}d(x^{(i)}_{k},x^{(i)}_{k+1})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1})+\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(R_{k})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})

which completes the special case.

Now, if there exists some
k>k_{0}
such that
\{x^{(1)}_{k},\dots,x^{(n)}_{k},\dots,x^{(m)}_{k}\}
is of cardinality strictly less than
m
, let
k_{1}
be the smallest such
k
. For all
k_{0}\leq k<k_{1}
, define
Y_{k}=\{x^{(i)}_{k}\mid 1\leq i\leq m\}
.
Consider the partition of
\{1,\dots,m\}
defined by the equivalence relation
i\sim j\Leftrightarrow x^{(i)}_{k_{1}}=x^{(j)}_{k_{1}}
. This partition is non-trivial, by definition of
k_{1}
and for any
i<j
with
i\sim j
, we must have
j>n
by definition of
k_{0}
. Now, let
I_{1}\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
be the strict subset containing only the least element in each set of the partition.
By the previous remark,
\{1,\dots,n\}\subset I_{1}
.
Let
Y_{k_{1}}=\{x^{(i)}_{k_{1}}\mid i\in I_{1}\}
. Now, either the sequence of sets
\{x^{(i)}_{k}\mid i\in I_{1}\}
is of constant cardinality, for
k\geq k_{1}
, or we may iterate the process finitely many times to obtain some
k_{\infty}\in\mathbb{N}
,
\{1,\dots,n\}\subset I_{\infty}\subset\{1,\dots,m\}
, and such that the sequence
\{x^{(i)}_{k}\mid i\in I_{\infty}\}
has constant cardinality for all
k\geq k_{\infty}
.
In either case, to get a finite chain between
X_{0}
and
Y_{k_{\infty}}
, first consider the chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1})
from
X_{0}
to
Y_{k_{0}}=X_{k_{0}}
.
Then consider the sequence of bijections, followed by a single merge between
Y_{k_{j}}
and
Y_{k_{j+1}}
given by the maps sending
x^{(i)}_{k}
to
x^{(i)}_{k+1}
for all
k_{j}\leq k\leq k_{j+1}-1
concatenating each of those gives a chain
(P_{k_{0}},\dots,P_{k_{\infty}-1})
. The key observation is that, by construction
Y_{k}\subset X_{k}
for all
k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{\infty}
and
P_{k}\subset R_{k}
for all
k_{0}\leq k\leq k_{\infty}-1
.
Thus, by
2.9
, we get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X_{0},Y_{k_{\infty}})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1},P_{k_{0}},\dots,P_{k_{\infty}-1})

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}-1})+\ell^{\omega}(R_{k_{0}},\dots,R_{k_{\infty}-1})

Finally, the argument of the special case still applies to give a chain
R^{\prime}
between
Y_{k_{\infty}}
and
X_{\infty}
whose length is bounded by
\sum_{k\geq k_{\infty}}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(R_{k})
, which completes the proof.
∎

Now, given an infinite
\omega
-chain between
X_{0}
and
X_{\infty}
, if
X_{\infty}
is not in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
we cannot directly compute the distance
d^{\omega}(X_{0},X_{\infty})
. Nevertheless, the distance
d^{\omega}
can formally be extended to the completion
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
, and we will see in the following that it actually makes sense to think of the end-point
X_{\infty}
as the limit, for
d^{\omega}
, of the infinite
\omega
-chain.

First we can compute the distance between the starting point
X_{0}
and some finite subsets
Y\subset X_{\infty}
of the end-point, and show that it minors the length of the chain.

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
((X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})
be an infinite
\omega
-chain between
X=X_{0}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
X_{\infty}
. For each
x\in X
, choose a sequence
(u^{(x)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
u^{(x)}_{0}=x
,
u^{(x)}_{k}\in X_{k}
and
u^{(x)}_{k}R_{k}u^{(x)}_{k+1}
for all
k\geq 0
. Then for any
Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
such that

•

\{\lim(u^{(x)}_{k})\mid x\in X_{0}\}\subset Y

•

Y\subset X_{\infty}

we have

d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})

Proof.

Let us fix some ordering of the points of
Y=\{y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)}\}
. Then, by
6.4
, there exists
n
sequences
(x^{(1)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},\dots,(x^{(n)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
x^{(i)}_{k}\in X_{k}
and
x^{(i)}_{k}R_{k}x^{(i)}_{k+1}
for all
k\geq 0
and all
1\leq i\leq n
and such that
x^{(i)}_{k}
converges to
y^{(i)}
for all
i
.
Now, let
Y_{k}=\{x^{(i)}_{k}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\subset X_{k}
, and
Z_{k}=\{u^{(x)}_{k}\mid x\in X_{0}\}\cup Y_{k}
. Observe that by construction,
Z_{0}=X_{0}
, and
Z_{k}\subset X_{k}
. Furthermore, for
k\geq 0
, we define
Q_{k}\subset Z_{k}\times Z_{k+1}
as
Q_{k}=\{(u^{(x)}_{k},u^{(x)}_{k+1})\mid x\in X_{0}\}\cup\{(x^{(i)}_{k},x^{(i)}_{k+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
. Observe that
Q_{k}\subset R_{k}
, by construction, and
Q_{k}
is indeed a surjective relation between
Z_{k}
and
Z_{k+1}
, thus, by
2.9
,
\ell^{\omega}(Q_{k})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{k})
. Let us show that
(Q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an infinite chain from
X
to
Y
. First of all
\ell^{\omega}((Q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})<\infty
by the previous observation. Let
Z_{\infty}\subset M
be defined as in
6.4
. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
x^{(i)}_{k}Q_{k}x^{(i)}_{k+1}
, for all
i
, and thus,
Y\subset Z_{\infty}
.
On the other hand, assume that
(v_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence in
M
such that
v_{k}\in Z_{k}
and
v_{k}Q_{k}v_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then since
Z_{k}
is of bounded cardinality, there must either be some
x\in X
or some
1\leq i\leq n
such that some subsequence of
v_{k}
is equal to some subsequence of either
(u_{k}^{(x)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
or
(x^{(i)}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
.
In either case, we can conclude that
\lim(v_{k})\in Y
. Thus
Z_{\infty}=Y
, and
(Q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is an infinite chain between
X
and
Y
. The inclusion
Q_{k}\subset R_{k}
, together with
6.5
gives

d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}((Q_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})

∎

We can now show that if two infinite
\omega
-chains have the same end-point then we can minor the sum of their length by the distance of their starting points.

Lemma 6.7
.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(X)
and
K\subset M
be a compact, and
(R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(P_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be two infinite chains from
X
to
K
and from
Y
to
K
respectively. Then,

d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})+\ell^{\omega}((P_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}).

Proof.

Fix orderings on
X
and
Y
,
X=\{x^{(1)}_{0},\dots,x^{(n)}_{0}\}
and
Y=\{y^{(1)}_{0},\dots,y^{(m)}_{0}\}
and choose sequences
x^{(i)}_{k}\in X_{k}
and
y^{(j)}_{k}\in Y_{k}
, such that
x^{(i)}_{k}R_{k}x^{(i)}_{k+1}
and
y^{(j)}_{k}P_{k}y^{(j)}_{k+1}
, for all
k\geq 0
,
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq m
. As observed in
6.4
those sequence admit limits in
K
that we denote
x^{(i)}_{\infty}
and
y^{(j)}_{\infty}
respectively. Let
Z=\{x^{(i)}_{\infty}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}\cup\{y^{(j)}_{\infty}\mid 1\leq j\leq m\}\subset K
. Then,
Z
together with either infinite chain satisfies the hypothesis of
6.6
, thus we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq d^{\omega}(X,Z)+d^{\omega}(Z,Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})+\ell^{\omega}((P_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}})

∎

6.2
Cauchy sequences in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and their limits

We now use infinite
\omega
-chains to characterize the behavior of Cauchy sequences in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
. Specifically, we use it to give a precise meaning to the idea that cauchy sequences in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
converge to compact subsets of
M
. This allows us to interpret the completion
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
as a subset of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
. Then, we show that if a compact
K\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
belongs to all of the
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}
, it must have a finite cardinality.

Definition 6.8
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and
K\subset M
be a compact. If
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},K)
tends to
0
as
n
grows, we say that the sequence
X_{n}
converges to
K
for the Hausdorff topology. If, in addition
(X_{n})
is a Cauchy-sequence for
d^{\omega}
, for some weight
\omega
, we say that
(X_{n})

converges to

K

for

d^{\omega}
.

Remark 6.9
.

By
6.4
, if
((X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}),(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})
is an infinite
\omega
-chain between
X_{0}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
K\subset M
, then
(X_{n})
converges to
K
for
d^{\omega}
.

Furthermore, if
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is a Cauchy sequence for
d^{\omega}
, it must be one for
d_{\mathrm{H}}
, and thus it converges to some compact
K\subset M
by
1.17
for both the Hausdorff topology and
d^{\omega}
.

The next proposition shows that the terminology "
(X_{n})
converges to
K
for
d^{\omega}
" is reasonable.

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be two sequences in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
that converge to the same compact subset
K\subset M
for
d^{\omega}
, then the distance
d^{\omega}(X_{n},Y_{n})
goes to zero as
n
grows.

Proof.

Since the sequences
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are Cauchy, we can extract two subsequences
(X_{\varphi(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{\varphi(n)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that we have both
\sum d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(n)},X_{\varphi(n+1)})<\infty
and
\sum d^{\omega}(Y_{\varphi(n)},Y_{\varphi(n+1)})<\infty
. Now, by construction, the quantities

\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(n)},X_{\varphi(n+1)})\text{ and }\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}d^{\omega}(Y_{\varphi(n)},Y_{\varphi(n+1)})

both tend to
0
as
k
goes to infinity. Now, let
k\geq 0
. For any
n\geq k
, we may construct a chain
(R_{0},\dots,R_{l})
between
X_{\varphi(n)}
and
X_{\varphi(n+1)}
,
whose length can be chosen to be arbitrarily close to
d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(n)},X_{\varphi(n+1)})
.
Concatenating each of those, for
n\geq k
gives an infinite
\omega
-chain between
X_{\varphi(k)}
and
K
whose length can be assumed to be arbitrarily close to
\sum_{n\geq k}d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(n)},X_{\varphi(n+1)})
.
Proceeding similarly for
(Y_{\varphi(n)})_{n\geq k}
, we can apply
6.7
, to deduce that, for any
k\geq 0
,

d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(k)},Y_{\varphi(k)})\leq\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(k)},X_{\varphi(k+1)})+\sum_{n=k}^{\infty}d^{\omega}(Y_{\varphi(k)},Y_{\varphi(k+1)})

Since the right hand sides tends to
0
as
k
grows, we have that
d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(k)},Y_{\varphi(k)})
does too. But now, for any
k\geq 0
,

d^{\omega}(X_{k},Y_{k})\leq d^{\omega}(X_{k},X_{\varphi(k)})+d^{\omega}(X_{\varphi(k)},Y_{\varphi(k)})+d^{\omega}(Y_{\varphi(k)},Y_{k}).

Since
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are Cauchy sequences, the first and last terms in the right hand side tends to
0
too as
k
grows, which gives that
d^{\omega}(X_{k},Y_{k})\to 0
.
∎

We can identify the limits of Cauchy sequences in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
with compact subsets of
M
. So that it makes sense to consider the intersection of those, for varying
\omega
, in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
, i.e. the compact sets
K
such that, for any weights
\omega
, we can find a sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}
that converges to
K
. The following couple lemmas show that such compact sets must have a finite cardinality, allowing to identify this intersection, as a set, with
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Lemma 6.11
.

Let
K\subset M
be a compact, and
L\subset K
a compact subspace. If there exists a sequence
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which converges to
K
for
d^{\omega}
, then there exists a sequence
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
which converges to
L
for
d^{\omega}
.

Proof.

Once more, up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume that for all
n\geq 0
,
d^{\omega}(X_{n},X_{n+1})<\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}
. Now, for each
n\geq 0
there exists a chain
(R^{n}_{0},\dots,R^{n}_{k_{n}})
between
X_{n}
and
X_{n+1}
whose
\omega
length may be assumed to be smaller than
d^{\omega}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}
, in particular,

\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\sum_{i=0}^{k_{n}}\ell^{\omega}(R^{n}_{i})<\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(d^{\omega}(X_{n},X_{n+1})+\frac{1}{2^{n+1}}\right)\leq 2

so that
(R_{0}^{0},R_{1}^{0},\dots,R_{k_{0}}^{0},R^{1}_{0},\dots)
forms an infinite
\omega
-chain, between
X_{0}
and
K
. For convenience, we relabel the sequence of relations
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and the intermediate configurations
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
.
Now by
6.4
, for each
y\in L
, we may chose a sequence
(x_{n}^{(y)})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
where
x^{(y)}_{n}\in Z_{n}
, and
x^{(y)}_{n}P_{n}x^{(y)}_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
, and such that
x_{n}^{(y)}
converges to
y
. For
n\geq 0
, define
Y_{n}=\{x^{(y)}_{n}\mid y\in L\}
and
Q_{n}=\{(x^{(y)}_{n},x^{(y)}_{n+1})\mid y\in L\}
. By construction
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
forms an infinite
\omega
-chain, since
Q_{n}\subset P_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
, and by
2.9
and
6.4
, it forms an infinite
\omega
-chain from
Y_{0}
to
Y_{\infty}=\{\lim x_{k}\mid(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}},x_{k}\in Y_{k}\text{ and }x_{k}Q_{k}x_{k+1}\forall k\geq 0\}
. By construction
L\subset Y_{\infty}
.
To prove the other inclusion, let
(z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence such that
z_{n}\in Y_{n}
, and
z_{n}Q_{n}z_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
. By
6.4
, such a sequence must be Cauchy, thus it converges to some limit
z_{\infty}\in K
. On the other hand, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
z_{n}=x^{(y)}_{n}
for some
y\in L
,
thus we may define a sequence
y_{n}
such that
z_{n}=x^{(y_{n})}_{n}
. Since
L
is compact, up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume that
y_{n}
converges to some
y_{\infty}\in L
. But now, we have

\displaystyle d(z_{\infty},y_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq d(z_{\infty},z_{n})+d(z_{n},y_{n})+d(y_{n},y_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq d(z_{\infty},z_{n})+\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}d(x^{(y_{n})}_{k},x^{(y_{n})}_{k+1})+d(y_{n},y_{\infty})

\displaystyle\leq d(z_{\infty},z_{n})+\sum_{k=n}^{\infty}\ell^{\omega}(Q_{k})+d(y_{n},y_{\infty})

Since all three terms on the right hand side converge to zero, we must have
z_{\infty}=y_{\infty}
and
z_{n}
does converge to a limit in
L
. Thus,
(Q_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is indeed an infinite
\omega
-chain between
Y_{0}
and

L
, and the sequence
(Y_{n})
converges to
L
for
d^{\omega}
.
∎

Proposition 6.12
.

Let
K\subset M
be a compact subset. Assume that for all weight
\omega
, there exists some sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
(X_{n}^{\omega})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
(X^{\omega}_{n})
converges to
K
for
d^{\omega}
, then
K
is finite.

Proof.

Let
K\subset M
be an infinite compact subspace. Then there exists a sequence of pairwise distinct points in
K
,
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
. Since
K
is compact, up to extracting a subsequence, we may assume that
(u_{n})
converges to some point
u_{-1}\in K
so that
\{u_{n}\mid n\in\mathbb{N}\}\cup\{u_{-1}\}\subset K
. Now, for all
n\geq 1
, define

\epsilon_{n}=\min\{d(x,y)\mid x\not=y\in\{u_{-1},u_{0},\dots,u_{n}\}\}

Then
\epsilon_{n}
is non-increasing, and converges to
0
since
(u_{n})
must be a Cauchy sequence. Let us consider the weight
\omega(n)=\max(1/\epsilon_{n},1)
, and show that no sequence in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
converges to
K
for
d^{\omega}
.
By
6.11
, if there exists a sequence converging to
K
there also exists one converging to
\{u_{n}\mid-1\leq n\}
thus we may assume without loss of generality that
K=\{u_{n}\mid-1\leq n\}
.
By contradiction, assume that such a sequence exits.
As we have seen several times already, we may transform it into an infinite
\omega
-chain
((X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},((R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}))
between
X_{0}
and
K
whose
\omega
-length is less than
1/2
. Now, let
N=\card(X_{0})
, and enumerate the elements of
X_{0}
,
\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(N)}\}
,
consider sequences
x^{(i)}_{n}
where
x^{(i)}_{0}=x^{(i)}
, and for all
n\geq 0

x^{(i)}_{n}\in X_{n}
and
x^{(i)}_{n}R_{n}x^{(i)}_{n+1}
. By
6.4
, those sequences exist and converge to limits,
x^{(i)}_{\infty}
in
K
. Now, let
I=\max\{k\mid\exists i,\ x^{(i)}_{\infty}=u_{k}\}
and
J=\max\{I,N\}
. Let
Y_{\infty}=\{u_{k}\mid-1\leq k\leq J\}
. Once more, by
6.4
, for each
-1\leq k\leq J
, there exists a sequence
(y^{(k)}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
y^{(k)}_{n}\in X_{n}
and
y^{(k)}_{n}R_{n}y^{(k)}_{n+1}
for all
n\geq 0
and such that
y^{(k)}_{n}
converges to
u_{k}
. Now for
n\geq 0
, define

Y_{n}=\{y^{(k)}_{n}\mid-1\leq k\leq J\}\cup\{x^{(i)}_{n}\mid 1\leq i\leq N\}

P_{n}=\{(y^{(k)}_{n},y^{(k)}_{n+1})\mid-1\leq k\leq J\}\cup\{(x^{(i)}_{n},x^{(i)}_{n+1})\mid 1\leq i\leq N\}

Then,
P_{n}\subset R_{n}
for all
n\geq 0
, and thus, by
2.9
,
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is an infinite
\omega
-chain. Furthermore, by construction
Y_{0}=X_{0}
, and
(Y_{n})
converges to
Y_{\infty}
. Thus, by
6.5
, we must have

d^{\omega}(X_{0},Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}((P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})\leq\ell^{\omega}((R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})\leq\frac{1}{2}

On the other hand,
\card(Y)>\card(X_{0})
, thus by
4.8

d^{\omega}(Y,X_{0})\geq\omega(J+2)\epsilon_{J}\geq 1
, which gives the desired contradiction.
∎

6.3
Uniform structure and completeness

We are now equipped to show that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
can be interpreted as the limit of the completion of the
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
in the category of uniform spaces.

Throughout,
\mathrm{Uni}
denotes the category of uniform spaces and uniformly continuous maps
[
8
]
.

Given a space
U
and some (pseudo)-metric on
U
,
d
, we will simply write
(U,d)
for the uniformity induced on
U
by the (pseudo)-metric
d
. Explicitly, a basis of this uniformity is given by the set
\{\{(x,y)\mid d(x,y)<\epsilon\}\mid\epsilon>0\}
.

In
5.9
, we showed that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is the limit of a diagram
\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathrm{Top}
. This diagram takes values in metric spaces and uniformly continuous maps, and can thus be interpreted as a diagram
\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathrm{Uni}
instead. Doing so equips
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
with a uniformity, which we now describe explicitly.

Definition 6.13
.

Let
\omega
be a weight and
\epsilon>0
, define the entourage
V^{\omega}_{\epsilon}
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, as follows.

V^{\omega}_{\epsilon}=\{(X,Y)\mid d^{\omega}(X,Y)<\epsilon\}\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)\times\mathrm{Ran}(M)

And define the following basis of uniformity on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

\mathcal{B}=\{V^{\omega}_{\epsilon}\mid\omega\in\mathbb{W},\epsilon>0\}

Proposition 6.14
.

The basis
\mathcal{B}
is a basis of the uniformity of the limit of
\mathrm{Ran}^{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}\to\mathrm{Uni}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
[
8
, chapter II.2.7 proposition 10]
.
∎

We now define the completion functor
\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)
whose value on a weight
\omega
is the completion of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
. Since the completion of a metric space can be seen as a quotient of its space of Cauchy sequences, it is also useful to define the corresponding functor
\mathcal{C}_{-}(M)
.

Definition 6.15
.

For any weight
\omega\in\mathbb{W}
we define the uniform space
(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
of sequences in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
that are Cauchy for
d^{\omega}
, with the uniform structure induced by the pseudometric
d^{\omega}((X_{n}),(Y_{n}))=\lim(d^{\omega}(X_{n},Y_{n}))
. We define the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Uni}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})

We also define
(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
the completion of the uniform space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
, and the functor

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathrm{op}}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Uni}

\displaystyle\omega

\displaystyle\mapsto(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})

Remark 6.16
.

The space
(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
can be seen as the quotient of
(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
by the equivalence relation
(X_{n})\sim_{\omega}(Y_{n})\Leftrightarrow d^{\omega}(X_{n},Y_{n})\to 0\Leftrightarrow d^{\omega}((X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}})=0
. The associated quotient map
\pi_{\omega}\colon(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})\to(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
is uniformly continuous. This construction gives a complete uniform space precisely because
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
was a metric space. For uniform spaces in general, it is not enough to add limits of Cauchy sequences and one needs to consider limits of Cauchy filters.

Proposition 6.17
.

If
\omega,\chi\colon\mathbb{N}^{*}\to[1,\infty)
are two weights such that
\omega\preceq\chi
, then the identity
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\chi})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
induces a uniformly continuous inclusion

(\mathcal{C}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega}).

and a uniformly continuous injection

(\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})\to(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega}).

In particular, the functors and
\mathcal{C}_{-}(M)
and
\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)
are well-defined.

Proof.

By
5.2
the identity
\mathrm{Id}\colon(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\chi})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is Lipschitz so that it indeed induces a uniformly continuous inclusion
(\mathcal{C}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
.

In addition, the composition

\displaystyle(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\chi})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Id}}(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})

is a uniformly continuous map from
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\chi})
to a complete uniform space. The universal property of the completion
(\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})
gives a unique uniformly continuous map
f\colon(\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})\to(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})
such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\chi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

Remark that
f
also makes the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})}
