{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M),d^{\chi})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),d^{\omega})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\chi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

To show that
f
is injective, we thus need to show that if two sequences
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{C}_{\chi}(M)
and are such that
d^{\omega}(X_{n},Y_{n})
goes to zero, then
d^{\chi}(X_{n},Y_{n})
also goes to zero. Let us take two such sequences. By
4.10
the two sequences are also Cauchy for
d_{\mathrm{H}}
, and
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{n},Y_{n})\leq d^{\omega}(X_{n},Y_{n})
goes to zero. By
1.17

X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
converge to the same compact subset
K\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
for
d_{\mathrm{H}}
. And finally, by
6.10
,
d^{\chi}(X_{n},Y_{n})
goes to zero, i.e.
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(Y_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
correspond to the same element in
\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M)
.
∎

We can finally combine our previous results to show that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, seen as a uniform space, can be seen as the limit of the completion of the
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
.

Proposition 6.18
.

The limit of the functor
\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Uni}
is
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
equipped with the uniformity generated by
\mathcal{B}
.

Proof.

Let us write
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(M)
for the limit of the functor
\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)
.
First, observe that the compositions

(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathcal{B})\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})\to\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M),

for all
\omega
, produce a unique map to the limit
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(M)
which we will prove to be an isomorphism.

Let
\omega
be a weight, and
(X_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a Cauchy sequence for
d^{\omega}
. Then, by
6.9
,
X_{n}
must converge to some compact
K\subset M
giving a well-defined map of sets,
\mathcal{C}_{\omega}(M)\to\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
. By
6.10
, this map descends to the quotient to give an injective map
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
. Since, by
6.17
, any map
\chi\to\omega
in
\mathbb{W}^{\mathrm{op}}
induces an injective map
\mathcal{R}_{\chi}(M)\to\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M)
, which commute with the inclusions into
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
, we may freely identify the underlying sets of
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M)
with subsets of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)
, corresponding to compact subsets of
M
appearing as the limit of some Cauchy sequence for
d^{\omega}
. After such an identification, we see that the underlying set of
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(M)
is given by

\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(M)=\bigcap\limits_{\omega\in\mathbb{W}}\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M)\subset\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{compact}}(M)

By
6.12
, this intersection reduces to finite subset of
M
, or in other words, to
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and thus the map
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathcal{B})\to\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(M)
is a bijection. Now, by
[
8
, chapter II.2.7 proposition 10]
, a basis of the uniformity on
\mathcal{R}_{\infty}(M)
is given by the set

\mathcal{B}^{\prime}=\{U^{\omega}_{\epsilon}\cap(\mathrm{Ran}(M))^{2}\mid\omega\in\mathbb{W},\epsilon>0\}

where, for any
\omega\in\mathbb{W}
,
\{U^{\omega}_{\epsilon}\mid\epsilon>0\}
is the basis of uniformity generated by the metric
d^{\omega}
. That is,

U^{\omega}_{\epsilon}=\{(X,Y)\mid d^{\omega}(X,Y)<\epsilon\}\subset(\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M))^{2}.

But now, observe that the distance
d^{\omega}
on
\mathcal{R}_{\omega}(M)
restricts to
d^{\omega}
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
(and the abuse in notation does not in fact induce contradictions). In particular, we have
U^{\omega}_{\epsilon}\cap(\mathrm{Ran}(M))^{2}=V^{\omega}_{\epsilon}
, and thus
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{B}^{\prime}
.
∎

We can now conclude.

Corollary 6.19
.

The uniform space
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathcal{B})
is a complete uniform space.

Proof.

By
6.18
,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathcal{B})
is the inverse limit of the functor
\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)\colon\mathbb{W}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{Uni}
. Since
\mathcal{R}_{-}(M)
takes value into complete Hausdorff uniform spaces, and since
\mathbb{W}
is a directed set, by
[
8
, Corollary of Prop. 10, Chap II.3.5]

(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\mathcal{B})
is a complete uniform space.
∎

Remark 6.20
.

There is another common point of view on uniform spaces, for which uniform spaces are spaces equipped with a collection of continuous pseudo-metrics, which jointly induce the topology. Given such a collection of pseudo-metrics on a space
U
,
(d_{i})_{i\in I}
, a basis of uniformity is then given by the set

\{\{(x,y)\mid d_{i}(x,y)<\epsilon\}\mid i\in I,\epsilon>0\}.

It can be shown (see for example the proof of
[
18
, theorem 16 p 35]
) that any uniformity on a Hausdorff space can in fact be specified through a family of pseudo-metrics. In particular, the fact that the uniformity we obtain on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is induced by a collection of (pseudo)-metrics is not surprising, though the fact that those pseudo-metrics all happen to be metrics deserves mention.

7
Conicality

This section is dedicated to showing that if
M
is a Riemannian manifold then
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
with the weighted topology is conically stratified. Since this is a local property on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, the section is written in the case where
M
is a Euclidean space, and the generalization to manifolds is done only at the very end (see
7.38
). We first recall the definition of a conically stratified space, and give a brief summary of the proof. Sections
7.3
and
7.4
are devoted to introducing technical tools. Specifically, we define a generalization of homotheties to
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
that we call
\Shift
and show that it is continuous. This function is in turn used in section
7.5
to construct a stratified homeomorphism between neighborhoods in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and a stratified cone which proves that
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is a conically stratified space when
M
is either a Euclidean space (
7.37
) or a Riemannian manifold (
7.38
). The very last subsection shows that while
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
equipped with the final topology is not conically stratified, it satisfies a close analogue to this property.

7.1
Preliminaries

We start by recalling the definition of a conically stratified space.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to P
be a stratified space. The
cone
on
X
, denoted
c(X)
is a space stratified over
P\cup\{-\infty\}
, where
-\infty<p
for any
p\in P
. Its underlying space is defined as
X\times[0,1)/_{X\times\{0\}}
equipped with the
teardrop
topology defined from the following basis
\{\pi(U)\mid U\in\mathrm{open}(X\times(0,1))\}\cup\{\pi(X\times[0,\epsilon))\mid 0<\epsilon<1\}
, where
\pi\colon X\times[0,1)\to c(X)
is the quotient map. The stratification on the cone is defined as

\displaystyle c(\varphi)\colon c(X)

\displaystyle\to P\cup\{-\infty\}

\displaystyle[(x,t)]

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\varphi(x)\quad&\text{if }t>0\\
-\infty&\text{if }t=0\end{cases}

Definition 7.2
.

Let
P
be a poset, and
p\in P
. We define the following subsets of
P
:

\displaystyle P_{>p}=\{q\in P\mid q>p\}

\displaystyle P_{\geq p}=\{q\in P\mid q\geq p\}

Remark that
P_{>p}\cup\{-\infty\}
is isomorphic to
P_{\geq p}
.

Definition 7.3
.

Let
\varphi\colon X\to P
be a stratified space. We say that it is
conically stratified
if, for any
x\in X
,
p=\varphi(x)
, there exists

•

a neighborhood
U\subset X
of
x
.

•

an open neighborhood of
x
in
\varphi^{-1}(p)
,
E\subset\varphi^{-1}(p)

•

a stratified space
\varphi_{L}\colon L\to P_{>p}

•

a stratified homeomorphism
E\times c(L)\xrightarrow{\simeq}U
, i.e. an homeomorphism such that the following diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E\times c(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P_{\geq p}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces P}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c(\varphi_{L})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{|U}}

Remark 7.4
.

In this section, we restrict to the case where
M
is a manifold. Specifically,
M
will be a Euclidean space for most of the section, and finally, a Riemannian manifold in
7.38
. In both cases,
\mathrm{Ran}_{=n}(M)
is a manifold of dimension
n
times that of
M
and so each point admits a neighborhood isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k
. We aim to prove that in both those cases
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, equipped with the weighted topology
\tau^{\omega}
, is a conically stratified space. This amounts to exhibiting, for every points
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
specific neighborhoods in both
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
\mathrm{Ran}_{=n}(M)
, where
\card(X)=n
. By the previous observation, we may think of
E
as a finite-dimensional vector space, instead of as an explicit neighborhood in
\mathrm{Ran}_{=n}(M)
. Furthermore, in the case where
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
, this vector space will be homeomorphic to
(\mathbb{R}^{p})^{n}
, but equipped with a specific norm
\left\lVert-\right\rVert_{\Sigma}
(see
Section

7.3
), instead of the usual one, which means that
E
will be homeomorphic to a Euclidean space, though not isometric to one. We will still use the terminology of Euclidean space in the following.

Remark 7.5
.

Readers familiar with pseudo-manifolds may wonder, in view of the previous remark, why we did not state the definition of a pseudo-manifold instead, since we will be considering conically stratified spaces with manifold strata. Observe that we aim to prove that
\mathrm{Ran}(M)\to\mathbb{N}
is conically stratified, and notably
\mathbb{N}
does not have a maximal element. In particular, there is no regular stratum in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
whose closure would recover all of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and thus it is not, in fact, a pseudo-manifold. However, proving that
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is conically stratified implies that so is
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. From which we can immediately recover the fact that the latter is in fact a pseudo-manifold (see for example
[
17
, Definition 2.4.1]
).

7.2
Sketch of the proof

Since this section is long and technical, we give here a brief summary of the proof of the main result, with a few drawings, for the case where
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
is a Euclidean space.

We want to construct, for any
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
n=\card(X)

(a)

an open neighborhood
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
of
X
. In fact, by
4.9
, we may chose
U
such that
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}_{\geq n}(M)
.

(b)

a cone
c(L)
with the adequate stratification

(c)

a Euclidean space
E
of dimension
\dim(M^{n})

(d)

a stratified homeomorphism
E\times c(L)\xrightarrow{\simeq}U
.

x^{(1)}
x^{(2)}
x^{(3)}
Y^{(1)}
Y^{(2)}
Y^{(3)}

Figure 7:
Sketch of the clustering and scaling functions for
M=\mathbb{R}^{2}
equipped with the Euclidean distance. The reference configuration
X=\{x^{(1)},x^{(2)},x^{(3)}\}
is displayed as red dots

for a cardinality
n=3
. The Hausdorff sphere
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
is sketched as dotted circles around the points of
X
. A local configuration
Y\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
is displayed as blue triangles
{\color[rgb]{0.2656,0.4648,0.668}\blacktriangle}
, the orientation of the triangles is different for each of the three clusters
(Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Y^{(3)})=\ClustX(Y)
, and the scaled configuration
Z=\ScaleX(2,Y)
is displayed as violet squares
{\color[rgb]{0.668,0.1992,0.4648}\blacksquare}
.

The construction revolves around the central idea of
locality
which we introduced in
Section

4.2
and refine in
Section

7.3
: when we work in a sufficiently small neighborhood of
X
, then every local configuration
Y
is composed of
clusters
of points localized around the points in
X
, and any geodesic chain between two sufficiently close local configurations is also local, i.e. it does not mix the clusters.
We introduce in
7.9
a clustering function
\ClustX
that gives the clusters of a local configuration. We can then apply some affine transformations to those clusters, using the corresponding points in
X
as centers.

Using this idea, we introduce in
7.22
the scaling of local configurations around a fixed configuration
X
. This is sketched on
Fig.

7
, where we fixed a configuration
X
of cardinality
n=3
, and show a local configuration
Y
, its three clusters
(Y^{(1)},Y^{(2)},Y^{(3)})=\ClustX(Y)
and the rescaled configuration
Z=\ScaleX(2,Y)
. Observe that such a
Z
may not always be local anymore and could potentially have a cardinality smaller than that of
Y
, if the scaling factor is too big. This is why we define the notion of bijective shift system in
7.13
, for which we prove that the scaling function does not mix clusters (see
7.17
).

We then show in
7.26
that this scaling function directly gives a homeomorphism from the cone on a sphere
S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
to a ball
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
. This homeomorphism is not stratified however, since the cone contains a one point stratum, its vertex, whereas the ball
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
contains many points of the same cardinality as
X
, its center.

We thus define a subspace of the ball,
C_{\underline{x}}
, whose elements are such that each of their clusters is centered around a point of
X
. This naturally excludes all configurations of cardinality exactly
\card(X)
except
X
itself. Since the teardrop topology allows to identify the cone on a subspace with a subspace of the cone, we then obtain a stratified homeomorphism between the cone on
C_{\underline{x}}\cap S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
and
C_{\underline{x}}
(
7.42
)
Note that, for the above line of reasoning to work, one needs the definition of the "center" of a cluster to be continuous and thus, one cannot simply use the iso-barycenter. We therefore define in
7.27
and
7.28
a more suitable notion of center that we call
midpoint
.

, its two clusters are centered around

.

At this stage we are still not finished because
C_{\underline{x}}
is not a neighborhood of
X
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
(in particular, it is not an open subset of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
). However, we proceed with the proof as follows.

•

We introduce a generic configuration of cardinality
n
,
G\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
so that the subspace
C_{\underline{g}}\subset B^{\omega}(G,\epsilon)
is a cone, by the aforementioned Proposition. This is the needed cone.
Item

(b)

•

E
will be a ball in
M^{n}
around
\underline{x}
, of adequately small radius.
Item

(c)

•

We define the needed homeomorphism in
7.36
in two steps:

–

We introduce a map
f\colon E\times C_{\underline{g}}\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, show that it is open, and define
U
to be its image
Item

(a)
.

–

We show that the (co)-restriction
f\colon E\times C_{\underline{g}}\to U
is a stratified homeomorphism
Item

(d)
.

The map
f^{-1}
can be understood as returning two things. The midpoints of a collection of clusters, giving a point in
M^{n}
close to
\underline{x}
, i.e. an element of
E
. And the local positions of the clusters around their midpoints, which can be encoded generically as positions around the configuration
G
, whose midpoints are
G
, i.e. elements of
C_{\underline{g}}
. This is sketched in
Fig.

8
.

7.3
Locality

From now on, we fix
p>0
an integer, and set
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
, equipped with the Euclidean distance:

\displaystyle d\colon M\times M

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle(x,y)

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\lVert x-y\right\rVert.

For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
we equip
M^{n}
with the norm

\displaystyle\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\colon M^{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\lVert x^{(i)}\right\rVert,

so that the distance
d_{\Sigma}
on
M^{n}
is inherited from
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{\Sigma}
as

\displaystyle d_{\Sigma}\colon M^{n}\times M^{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathbb{R}_{+}

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y})

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{y}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}.

We fix
\omega
a weight. Throughout this section
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
will always be equipped with the
\tau^{\omega}
topology, unless explicitly stated. We will denote continuity with respect to the topology
\tau^{\omega}
as
\omega
-continuity for short. Note that, when there is no ambiguity, we will also call
\omega
-continuous any continuous maps defined on a product of space, in which every occurrence of a Ran space is equipped with the topology
\tau^{\omega}
.

In this section and the next, we set-up the technical results that will be needed to prove both
7.38
and
7.43
. Since the latter deals with the final topology, we need to be slightly more careful with our technical lemmas. In particular, in this section, we prove a slightly stronger version of
4.17
, to extend its result from the
\omega
ball onto the Hausdorff ball. This is not strictly needed to prove
7.38
, but in order to investigate the final topology, we need to work on a neighborhood that is common to all weighted topologies. This role will be played by the Hausdorff ball.

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. For any
r<\merg(X)
, there exists
\epsilon\geq\merg(X)-r>0
such that for all
Y,Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
, if
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)<\epsilon
, then any geodesic chain from
Y
to
Z
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local.

Proof.

Let
\epsilon=\merg(X)-r
. Since
Y
and
Z
are in the open ball
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
, we may find
r^{\prime}<r
such that
Y
and
Z
are contained in the closed ball
\overline{B}_{H}(X,r^{\prime})
. We will prove that any geodesic chain between
Y
and
Z
is contained in
\overline{B}_{H}(X,r^{\prime})
. Consider a minimal combinatorial chain
((Y_{0},\dots,Y_{n}),(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1}))
between
Y
and
Z
. By
2.16
, we may assume that it is a sequence of simple merges, a bijection and simple splittings. Let
R\subset Y\times Y^{\prime}
be the composition of all merges,
B\subset Y^{\prime}\times Z^{\prime}
be the bijection and
S\subset Z^{\prime}\times Z
the composition of all splittings, such that
(R,B,S)
is still a combinatorial chain between
Y
and
Z
, and consider the following equivalence relation on
Y
.

y\sim y^{\prime}\Leftrightarrow\exists z\in Y^{\prime},\ yRz\text{ and }y^{\prime}Rz

Now, let
y,y^{\prime}\in Y
. There must be
x,x^{\prime}\in X
such that
y\in\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})
and
y^{\prime}\in\overline{B}(x^{\prime},r^{\prime})
. Now observe that if
y\sim y^{\prime}
, we must have
x=x^{\prime}
. Indeed, if it were not the case, we would have
d(y,y^{\prime})\geq d(x,x^{\prime})-2r^{\prime}\geq 2\merg(X)-2r>\epsilon
, which would imply
d^{\omega}(Y,Y^{\prime})>\epsilon
. However, by construction
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)=d^{\omega}(Y,Y^{\prime})+d^{\omega}(Y^{\prime},Z^{\prime})+d^{\omega}(Z^{\prime},Z)<\epsilon
. A symmetric reasoning on
S
gives that no point in
Z^{\prime}
splits into 2 different clusters in
Z
, and we may show similarly that
B
exchange points in
Y^{\prime}
and
Z^{\prime}
living in the neighborhood of the same points of
X
. Thus, we may write a partition

R_{i}=\coprod_{x\in X}R_{i}^{x}\subset\coprod Y_{i}^{x}\times Y_{i+1}^{x}

Now, for
x\in X
, consider the map
\varphi_{x}\colon M\to\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})
, defined by

\displaystyle\varphi_{x}\colon M

\displaystyle\to\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}y&\text{ if $y\in\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})$}\\
x+r^{\prime 2}\frac{y-x}{d(x,y)^{2}}&\text{ otherwise}\end{array}\right.

By
7.7
, the application
\varphi_{x}
contracts distances, and takes values in
\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})
, furthermore, it leaves
\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})
unchanged. Thus, we may define for
0\leq i\leq n

Y^{\prime}_{i}=\coprod_{x\in X}\varphi_{x}(Y_{i}^{x})

R^{\prime}_{i}=\coprod_{x\in X}\varphi_{x}(R_{i}^{x})\subset Y^{\prime}_{i}\times Y^{\prime}_{i+1}

Observe that
Y^{\prime}_{0}=Y_{0}=Y
and
Y^{\prime}_{n}=Y_{n}=Z
, since
Y,Z\in\overline{B}_{H}(X,r^{\prime})
, and each
\varphi_{x}
restrict to the identity on each
\overline{B}(x,r^{\prime})
, for
x\in X
. Furthermore, the new chain
(R^{\prime}_{0},\dots,R^{\prime}_{n-1})
is
\overline{B}_{H}(X,r^{\prime})
-local, and since
\varphi_{x}
contracts distances, it is shorter than
(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
. However, since we assumed that
(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
was a geodesic chain, we must have equality between the length of the two chains, which is only possible if
(R_{0},\dots,R_{n-1})
was already
\overline{B}_{H}(X,r^{\prime})
-local.
∎

Lemma 7.7
.

Let
x\in M
and
r>0
, the map

\displaystyle\varphi_{x}\colon M

\displaystyle\to M

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle\mapsto\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}y&\text{ if $d(x,y)\leq r$}\\
x+r^{2}\frac{y-x}{d(x,y)^{2}}&\text{if $d(x,y)>r$}\end{array}\right.

satisfies

•

\varphi_{x}
restricts to the identity on the closed ball
\overline{B}(x,r)
.

•

\varphi_{x}
takes values in the closed ball
\overline{B}(x,r)

•

for all
y,z\in M
,
d(y,z)\geq d(\varphi_{x}(y),\varphi_{x}(z))
. With equality if and only if
y,z\in\overline{B}(x,r)

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we may assume that
x=0
, and consider the sphere
S(0,r)=\{v\in M\mid d(0,v)=r\}
. We will simply write
\varphi
for
\varphi_{0}
. Then, any point
y\in M\setminus\{0\}
can be written uniquely as
y=\lambda v
, with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
and
v\in S(0,r)
. The map
\varphi
then sends
\lambda v
to either
\lambda v
, if
\lambda\leq 1
or
\frac{1}{\lambda}v
if
\lambda\geq 1
. This shows the first two points. For the last part, it is clear that
\varphi_{0}
preserves distances in the closed ball, since it preserves all points. Now, let
y\in\overline{B}(0,r)
and
z\in M\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)
, and consider
y^{\prime}
the orthogonal projection of
y
on the line
(0,z)
. By construction, the triangles
(y,y^{\prime},z)
and
(y,y^{\prime},\varphi(z))
are both rectangle at
y^{\prime}
thus it is enough to show that
d(y^{\prime},\varphi(z))<d(y^{\prime},z)
. We have, by construction
z=\lambda v
for some
\lambda>1
and
v\in S(0,r)
,
\varphi(z)=\frac{1}{\lambda}v
and
y^{\prime}=\lambda^{\prime}v
, with
\lambda^{\prime}\in[-1,1]
. If
\lambda^{\prime}\leq\frac{1}{\lambda}
,
\varphi(z)
is in between
y^{\prime}
and
z
, and thus there is nothing to prove. Otherwise, We compute

\displaystyle d(z,y^{\prime})-d(\varphi(z),y^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda-\lambda^{\prime}\right)r-\left(\lambda^{\prime}-\frac{1}{\lambda}\right)r

\displaystyle=\frac{r}{\lambda}\left(\lambda^{2}-2\lambda\lambda^{\prime}+1\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r}{\lambda}\left(\lambda^{2}-2\lambda+1\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{r}{\lambda}(\lambda-1)^{2}>0

which concludes this case.

If
y,z\in M\setminus\overline{B}(0,r)
, we first consider two particular cases.

•

if
y=\lambda_{1}v
and
z=\lambda_{2}v
, with
v\in S(0,r)
,
\lambda_{1}>1
and
|\lambda_{2}|\geq\lambda_{1}>1
. (This covers all cases where
0,y
and
z
are collinear, up to exchanging
y
and
z
and replacing
v
by
-v
). Then

\displaystyle d(\varphi(y),\varphi(z))

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{r}{\lambda_{1}}-\frac{r}{\lambda_{2}}\right|

\displaystyle=\frac{|r(\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1})|}{|\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}|}

\displaystyle\leq r|\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}|=d(y,z)

Where the equality is obtained only if
y=z
.

•

if
y=\lambda v_{1}
, and
z=\lambda v_{2}
, with
\lambda>1
and
v_{1},v_{2}\in S(0,r)
. Then,
\varphi(z)=\frac{z}{\lambda^{2}}
and
\varphi(y)=\frac{y}{\lambda^{2}}
and thus,
\varphi(z)-\varphi(y)=\frac{z-y}{\lambda^{2}}
, which implies that
d(\varphi(z),\varphi(y))\leq d(y,z)
with equality if and only if
y=z
.

Now, for the general case, consider
y=\lambda_{1}v_{1}
and
z=\lambda_{2}v_{2}
. We may assume,
\lambda_{1}\not=\lambda_{2}
and that they are both positive, and that
v_{1}\not=\pm v_{2}
, since those cases have been addressed. By symmetry, consider
\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}
, and consider
y^{\prime}=\lambda_{1}v_{2}
and
z^{\prime}=\lambda_{2}v_{1}
. Now, observe that
(y,y^{\prime},z,z^{\prime})
is an isosceles trapezoid, and that
d(y,z)
corresponds to the length of a diagonal. Furthermore,
(\varphi(y),\varphi(y^{\prime}),\varphi(z),\varphi(z^{\prime}))
is also an isosceles trapezoid, and each of its side is strictly shorter than the corresponding side in
(y,y^{\prime},z,z^{\prime})
, by the two previous special cases. Furthermore, the sides of the two trapezoid are parallel, directed by
v_{1}
,
v_{2}
and
v_{2}-v_{1}
respectively. This implies that
d(\varphi(y),\varphi(z))<d(y,z)
.

This proof is represented
Fig.

9

∎

0
z
\varphi(z)
y
y^{\prime}
0
y
z
y^{\prime}
z^{\prime}
\varphi(y^{\prime})
\varphi(y)
\varphi(z^{\prime})
\varphi(z)

Figure 9:
An illustration of the proof of
7.7

7.4
Scaling

In this section, we use the notion of locality to define the equivalent of a homothety on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We will use two slightly different points of view of this notion: (i) in
7.12
we define an application called
\Shift
that has four variables: the "centers" of the antecedent and image configurations, the scaling factor, and the configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
to which the homothety is applied; (ii) alternatively, in
7.13
and
7.15
we define an application on
M
indexed by a set of parameter
\Sigma
called a
shift system
. The two point of view are closely related, as outlined in
7.17
but they serve different purposes. The first point of view is better suited to describe the continuity of the homothety, and the second is better suited to describe bijectivity and inverses. Those are the two properties that we will need to build our homeomorphism. Moreover, the second point of view also enables us to apply
7.6
directly. This ultimately justifies this somewhat double-definition. Note that, what we call a generalized homothety is in fact the composition of a homothety with a translation.

We first generalize some elementary transformations on
M
onto
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Definition 7.8
.

We define translation and scaling function as

\displaystyle+\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\times M

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(Y,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto Y+x=\{y+x|y\in Y\}

\displaystyle-\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)\times M

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(Y,x)

\displaystyle\mapsto Y-x=\{y-x|y\in Y\}

\displaystyle\scale\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(s,X)

\displaystyle\mapsto s\times X=\{sx|x\in X\}

and, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}

\displaystyle+\colon\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}\times M^{n}

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}

\displaystyle(\underline{Y},\underline{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\underline{Y}+\underline{x}=\left(Y^{(1)}+x^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)}+x^{(n)}\right)

\displaystyle-\colon\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}\times M^{n}

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}

\displaystyle(\underline{Y},\underline{x})

\displaystyle\mapsto\underline{Y}-\underline{x}=\left(Y^{(1)}-x^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)}-x^{(n)}\right)

\displaystyle\scale\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}

\displaystyle\to\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}

\displaystyle(s,\underline{Y})

\displaystyle\mapsto s\times\underline{Y}=\left(s\times Y^{(1)},\dots,s\times Y^{(n)}\right)

Then we define the
clustering
function, that gives a partition of a configuration with respect to a reference configuration
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We also define the inverse application that gives the union of a family of clusters.

Definition 7.9
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
n=\card(X)
, and
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(\{X\})
. The
\underline{x}
-clustering function is defined as

\displaystyle\ClustX\colon B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))

\displaystyle\to\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{Ran}(B(x^{(i)},\merg(X)))

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(Y\cap B(x^{(i)},\merg(X))\right)_{1\leq i\leq n}

We also note, for any
\underline{Y}=(Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)})\in\left(\mathrm{Ran}(M)\right)^{n}
,

\displaystyle\bigcup\underline{Y}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}Y^{(i)}

\displaystyle\bigsqcup\underline{Y}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}Y^{(i)}\quad\text{ if }Y^{(i)}\cap Y^{(j)}=\emptyset,\forall i\neq j\in\{1,\dots,n\}.

Remark 7.10
.

For any
Y\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
, we have
Y=\bigsqcup\ClustX(Y)
.

Then we show that this clustering function is continuous.

Proposition 7.11
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
n=\card(X)
, and
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(\{X\})
. The
\underline{x}
-clustering function is
\omega
-continuous on
\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
.

Proof.

Let
(Y_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
, be a sequence that converges to
Y\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
for the distance
d^{\omega}
, and let
\left(Y^{(1)},\dots,Y^{(n)}\right)=\ClustX(Y)
and
\left(Y_{k}^{(1)},\dots,Y_{k}^{(n)}\right)=\ClustX(Y_{k})
for all
k\geq 0
, and let us show that
\ClustX(Y_{k})\to\ClustX(Y)
, i.e. that
Y_{k}^{(j)}\to Y^{(j)}
for all
1\leq j\leq n
. By
7.6
there exists
\epsilon>0
such that for all
Z\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})\subset B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{3\merg(X)}{4})
, if
d^{\omega}(Y,Z)<\epsilon
then any geodesic chain from
Y
to
Z
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{3\merg(X)}{4})
-local, and thus
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
-local. But since
Y_{k}\to Y
, there exists
K\geq 0
such that for any
k\geq K
,
d^{\omega}(Y_{k},Y)<\epsilon
. Let
k\geq K
, then, by
2.24
, there exists a geodesic chain
((Y_{k}=Z_{0},\dots,Z_{l}=Y),(R_{0},\dots,R_{l-1}))
from
Y_{k}
to
Y
, which will necessarily be
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
-local.

Let
1\leq j\leq n
, and let
Z_{m}^{(j)}=Z_{m}\cap B(x^{(j)},\merg(X))
for all
0\leq m\leq l
and
R_{m}^{(j)}=R_{m}\cap B^{2}(x^{(j)},\merg(X))
for all
0\leq m\leq l-1
. Then
((Z_{0}^{(j)},\dots,Z_{l}^{(j)}),(R_{0}^{(j)},\dots,R_{l-1}^{(j)}))
is a combinatorial chain from
Y_{k}^{(j)}
to
Y^{(j)}
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(Y_{k}^{(j)},Y^{(j)})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0}^{(j)},\dots,R_{l-1}^{(j)})\leq\ell^{\omega}(R_{0},\dots,R_{l-1})=d^{\omega}(Y_{k},Y)

which converges to zero, so that indeed
\ClustX(Y_{k})\to\ClustX(Y)
. Since
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is metric, this concludes this proof.
∎

We now have everything to define the generalized homothety on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
from both perspectives.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and
A_{n}=\{(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\in M^{n}\times M^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{Ran}(M)\mid\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))=n,Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{x})))\}
. We define the
n
-
shifting function
as

\displaystyle\Shift_{n}\colon A_{n}

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\bigcup\left(s\scale(\ClustX(Z)-\underline{x})+\underline{y}\right)

In the following, the index
n
will often be omitted.

Definition 7.13
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. A
n
-
shift system
is a quadruplet
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)\in M^{n}\times M^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
such that
\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))=n
, and
0<r\leq\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{x})).

A
n
-shift system
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)
is
bijective
if
\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))=n
and
0<s\leq\frac{\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))}{r}
.

The
dual system
of a bijective
n
-shift system
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)
is
\overline{\Sigma}=(\underline{y},\underline{x},\frac{1}{s},sr)
.

Remark 7.14
.

We will often drop the
n
when there is no ambiguity.

Definition 7.15
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)
be a
n
-shift system. The
\Sigma
-shifting function
is

\displaystyle\phi_{\Sigma}\colon\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}B(x^{(i)},r)

\displaystyle\to\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}B(y^{(i)},sr)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto s(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)}\quad\text{ if }z\in B(x^{(i)},r).

Remark 7.16
.

This function is well defined. Indeed, since
r\leq\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))
, the
B(x^{(i)},r)
are pairwise disjoint, and since
d
is induced by the Euclidean norm
d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),y^{(i)})=sd(z,x^{(i)})<sr
for any
z\in B(x^{(i)},r)
, and any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.

These two points of view are actually equivalent, and we detail the relation from one to the other in the following proposition.

Proposition 7.17
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, let
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)
be a
n
-shift system, and let
Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),r)

(a)

\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)=\{\phi_{\Sigma}(z)\mid z\in Z\}=\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z)
.

(b)

If
\Sigma
is a bijective shift system, then

(i)

\phi_{\Sigma}
is a bijection of inverse
\phi_{\overline{\Sigma}}

(ii)

\card(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z))=\card(Z).

(iii)

\Shift\left(\underline{y},\underline{x},\frac{1}{s},\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\right)=Z.

Proof.

Let
X=\pi_{n}(\underline{x})
. By unpacking
7.12
, we have

\displaystyle\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{s(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)}\mid z\in Z\cap B(x^{(i)},\merg(X))\}.

Since
Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
, and
r\leq\merg(X)
, for any
z\in Z
, there is a unique
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
d(z,x^{(i)})<\merg(X)
, and
d(z,x^{(i)})=\min_{x\in X}\{d(z,x)\}\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(Z,X)<r
. So that
Z\cap B(x^{(i)},\merg(X))=Z\cap B(x^{(i)},r)
and
Z=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}Z\cap B(x^{(i)},r)
. We thus have

\displaystyle\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\{\phi_{\Sigma}(z)\mid z\in Z\cap B(x^{(i)},r)\}=\{\phi_{\Sigma}(z)\mid z\in Z\},

which proves
Item

(a)
.

Let us suppose now that
\Sigma
is bijective. In that case
\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))=n
so that the
y^{(i)}
are pairwise distinct, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Moreover
sr\leq\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))
, so that the balls
B(y^{(i)},sr)
are pairwise disjoint, for
1\leq i\leq n
. Moreover, for any
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, the restriction
\phi_{\Sigma}|_{B(x^{(i)},r)}^{B(y^{(i)},sr)}
is a bijection, so that
\phi_{\Sigma}
is a bijection of inverse

\displaystyle\phi_{\overline{\Sigma}}\colon\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}B(y^{(i)},sr)

\displaystyle\to\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}B(x^{(i)},r)

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{s}(z-y^{(i)})+x^{(i)}\quad\text{ if }z\in B(y^{(i)},sr).

By the previous argument, we thus have

\card(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z))=\card(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z))=\card(Z),

and

\Shift\left(\underline{y},\underline{x},\frac{1}{s},\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\right)=\bm{\phi}_{\overline{\Sigma}}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z))=Z.

∎

Remark 7.18
.

Let
(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\in A_{n}
. If
\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))=n
and
0<sd_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),Z)<\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))
, then, we can set
r=\min\{\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{x})),\frac{\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))}{s}\}
, so that
(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)
is a bijective system such that
d_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),Z)<r
and we can apply
7.17
.

We now take advantage of both points of view to show that the shifting function is continuous. We will need the following upper bounds.

Proposition 7.19
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and
(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\in A_{n}
, and
X=\pi_{n}(\underline{x})
.

(a)

For any
\underline{\hat{x}}\in M^{n}
such that
(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z)\in A_{n}
and
\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\leq\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)
,

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z))\leq s\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\card(Z)\omega(\card(Z))

(b)

For any
\underline{\hat{y}}\in M^{n}
,

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{\hat{y}},s,Z))\leq\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{\hat{y}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\card(Z)\omega(\card(Z))

(c)

For any
\hat{s}\geq 0
,

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},\hat{s},Z))\leq\left\lvert s-\hat{s}\right\rvert\card(Z)\omega(\card(Z))d^{\omega}(X,Z)

(d)

For any
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)<r<\merg(X)
, there exists
\epsilon\geq\merg(X)-r>0
such that for any
\hat{Z}\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
, if
d^{\omega}(Z,\hat{Z})<\epsilon
then

d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,\hat{Z}))\leq sd^{\omega}(Z,\hat{Z})

Proof.

Let
\underline{\hat{x}}\in M^{n}
such that
(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z)\in A_{n}
and
\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\leq\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)
, and let
\hat{X}=\pi_{n}(\hat{x})
. Let us define the shift systems
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,\merg(X))
and
\hat{\Sigma}=(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,\merg(\hat{X}))
. Then, by
7.17

Item

(a)
, we have

\displaystyle\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)

\displaystyle=\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z)

\displaystyle\Shift(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z)

\displaystyle=\bm{\phi}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(Z).

We can thus define the following surjective relation between
\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)
and
\Shift(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z)
:

\displaystyle R=\{(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z))\mid z\in Z\}.

We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z))

\displaystyle\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)\leq\omega(\card(Z))\sum_{z\in Z}d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z)).

For any
z\in Z
, there is a unique
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
d(z,x^{(i)})\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)<\merg(X)
, and a unique
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
d(z,\hat{x}^{(j)})=\min_{k\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\{d(z,\hat{x}^{(k)})\}<\merg(\hat{X})
. Let us take
1\leq k\leq n
, such that
k\neq i
, then

\displaystyle d(z,\hat{x}^{(k)})

\displaystyle\geq d(z,x^{(k)})-d(x^{(k)},\hat{x}^{(k)})

\displaystyle\geq d(x^{(i)},x^{(k)})-d(z,x^{(i)})-d(x^{(k)},\hat{x}^{(k)})

\displaystyle\geq 2\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)-\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\geq\merg(X)

\displaystyle\geq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)+\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\geq d(z,x^{(i)})+d(x^{(i)},\hat{x}^{(i)})

\displaystyle\geq d(z,\hat{x}^{(i)}).

Therefore
d(z,\hat{x}^{(i)})=\min_{k\in\{1,\dots,n\}}\{d(z,\hat{x}^{(k)})\}
, and thus
i=j
.
We thus have

\displaystyle d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z))=d(s(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)},s(z-\hat{x}^{(i)})+y^{(i)})=sd(x^{(i)},\hat{x}^{(i)})\leq s\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma},

and finally

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{\hat{x}},\underline{y},s,Z))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Z))\sum_{z\in Z}s\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\leq s\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{\hat{x}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\omega(\card(Z))\card(Z),

which yields
Item

(a)
.

Let
\underline{\hat{y}}\in M^{n}
. Then again, let us define the shift system
\hat{\Sigma}=(\underline{x},\underline{\hat{y}},s,\merg(X))
, which, by
7.17

Item

(a)
satisfies
\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{\hat{y}},s,Z)=\bm{\phi}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(Z)
, and the relation

\displaystyle R=\{(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z))\mid z\in Z\}.

So that we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{\hat{y}},s,Z))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Z))\sum_{z\in Z}d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z)).

For any
z\in Z
, there exists a unique
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
z\in B(x^{(i)},\merg(X))
. So we have

\displaystyle d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z))=d(s(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)},s(z-x^{(i)})+\hat{y}^{(i)})=d(y^{(i)},\hat{y}^{(i)})\leq\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{\hat{y}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}.

So that finally

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{\hat{y}},s,Z))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Z))\sum_{z\in Z}\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{\hat{y}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{\hat{y}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\omega(\card(Z))\card(Z),

which gives
Item

(b)
.

Let
\hat{s}\geq 0
. Similarly, we can define the shift system
\hat{\Sigma}=(\underline{x},\underline{y},\hat{s},\merg(X))
, which, by
7.17

Item

(a)
satisfies
\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},\hat{s},Z)=\bm{\phi}_{\hat{\Sigma}}(Z)
, and the relation

\displaystyle R=\{(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z))\mid z\in Z\}.

So that we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},\hat{s},Z))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Z))\sum_{z\in Z}d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z)).

For any
z\in Z
, there exists a unique
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
z\in B(x^{(i)},\merg(X))
. So we have

\displaystyle d(\phi_{\Sigma}(z),\phi_{\hat{\Sigma}}(z))=d(s(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)},\hat{s}(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)})=\left\lvert s-\hat{s}\right\rvert d(z,x^{(i)})\leq\left\lvert s-\hat{s}\right\rvert d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z).

So that finally

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},\hat{s},Z))

\displaystyle\leq\omega(\card(Z))\sum_{z\in Z}\left\lvert s-\hat{s}\right\rvert d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert s-\hat{s}\right\rvert\omega(\card(Z))\card(Z)d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z),

which gives
Item

(c)
.

Finally, let
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)<r<\merg(X)
and let us define
\epsilon
as the one given by
7.6
. Then, for any
\hat{Z}\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
, if
d^{\omega}(Z,\hat{Z})<\epsilon
then, by
2.24
and
7.6
there exists a
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,r)
-local geodesic chain from
Z
to
\hat{Z}
. Moreover, by
7.17

Item

(a)
we directly have
\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,\hat{Z})=\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(\hat{Z})
. And finally by
4.18
, we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,\hat{Z}))=d^{\omega}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z),\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(\hat{Z}))\leq sd^{\omega}(Z,\hat{Z}),

which gives
Item

(d)
.
∎

Lemma 7.20
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
. The projection
\pi_{n}\colon M^{n}\to(\mathrm{Ran}(M),d^{\omega})
is
\omega(n)
-Lipschitz.

Proof.

Let
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}),\underline{y}=(y^{(1)},\dots,y^{(n)})\in M^{n}
. And let
R=\{(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
, which is a surjective relation from
X
to
Y
. We thus have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y)\leq\ell^{\omega}(R)=\omega(n)\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{y}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}.

∎

We can finally prove the continuity of the shifting function.

Proposition 7.21
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and let us define
B_{n}=\{(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\in M^{n}\times M^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{Ran}(M)\mid\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))=n,Z\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),\frac{\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))}{2})\}
The function
\Shift
is
\omega
-continuous on
B_{n}
.

Proof.

Let
(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence in
B_{n}
that converges to
(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)
, and let us show that the image by
\Shift
converges to
\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)
. Let us write
X=\pi_{n}(\underline{x})
and
X_{k}=\pi_{n}(\underline{x_{k}})
for all
k\geq 0
.

First, let us remark that, for any
k\geq 0
, we have
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},X)\leq\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}
. If
n=1
, then
\merg(X_{k})=\merg(X)
. Otherwise, let
i\neq j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. We have

\displaystyle d(x_{k}^{(i)},x_{k}^{(j)})\geq d(x^{(i)},x^{(j)})-d(x_{k}^{(i)},x^{(i)})-d(x_{k}^{(j)},x^{(j)})\geq 2\merg(X)-\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma},

so that, in both cases
\merg(X_{k})\geq\merg(X)-\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}
.

Since
(\underline{x_{k}})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\underline{x}
and
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
Z
, there exists
K\geq 0
such that, for any
k\geq K
, we have
\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}<\frac{\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)}{2}
,
d^{\omega}(Z,Z_{k})<\frac{d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)}{4}
and
\merg(X_{k})>\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
. Let
k\geq K
, then we have

\displaystyle d_{\mathrm{H}}(Z,X_{k})

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(Z,Z_{k})+d_{\mathrm{H}}(Z_{k},X_{k})

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(Z,Z_{k})+d_{\mathrm{H}}(Z_{k},X_{k})

by
4.10

\displaystyle<\frac{d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)}{4}+\frac{\merg(X_{k})}{2}

\displaystyle\text{since }(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z_{k})\in B_{n}

\displaystyle<\frac{\merg(X)}{4}-\frac{\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}}{2}+\frac{\merg(X_{k})}{2}

\displaystyle\text{since }\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}<\frac{\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)}{2}

\displaystyle<\merg(X_{k})-\frac{\merg(X)}{4},

\displaystyle\text{since }\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\geq\merg(X)-\merg(X_{k})

\displaystyle<\merg(X_{k})

so that
(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z)\in A_{n}
. Moreover, let
r_{k}=\merg(X_{k})-\frac{\merg(X)}{4}
, and
\epsilon_{k}\geq\merg(X_{k})-r_{k}\geq\frac{\merg(X)}{4}
. Observe that
\frac{\merg(X_{k})}{2}<r_{k}
. Thus, for any
\hat{Z}\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},\frac{\merg(X_{k})}{2})\subset B_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},r_{k})
, such that
d^{\omega}(Z,\hat{Z})<\frac{\merg(X)}{4}\leq\epsilon_{k}
, we may apply
7.19

Item

(d)
to get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},\hat{Z}))\leq s_{k}d^{\omega}(Z,\hat{Z})

We can thus define
\epsilon=\frac{\merg(X)}{4}\leq\inf_{k\geq K}{\epsilon_{k}}
. Since
(Z_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
Z
, there exists
K^{\prime}\geq K
such that, for any
k\geq K^{\prime}
, we have
d^{\omega}(Z_{k},Z)<\epsilon
.

Let
k\geq K^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z_{k}))\leq\;

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y},s,Z))

\displaystyle+d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s,Z))

\displaystyle+d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z))

\displaystyle+d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z_{k})).

Let us now show that we can apply
7.19
to these four terms.

Since
d_{\mathrm{H}}(Z,X_{k})<\merg(X_{k})
, we can apply
7.19

Item

(b)
and
Item

(c)
to the second and third term. Since, moreover,
\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}<\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Z)
,
we can apply
7.19

Item

(a)
to the first term.

Lastly, since
d^{\omega}(Z_{k},Z)<\epsilon
, and
Z_{k}\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},\frac{\merg(X_{k})}{2})
, by the above discussion, we can apply
7.19

Item

(d)
to the fourth term. We get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z),\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z_{k}))\leq\;

\displaystyle s\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{x_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\omega(\card(Z))\card(Z)

\displaystyle+\left\lVert\underline{y}-\underline{y_{k}}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\omega(\card(Z))\card(Z)

\displaystyle+\left\lvert s-s_{k}\right\rvert\omega(\card(Z))\card(Z)d_{\mathrm{H}}(X_{k},Z)

\displaystyle+s_{k}d^{\omega}(Z,Z_{k}).

We easily check that these four terms go to zero as
k
goes to infinity, which proves that
\Shift(\underline{x_{k}},\underline{y_{k}},s_{k},Z_{k})
converges to
\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)
. Since
B_{n}
is metrizable this concludes this proof.
∎

Finally, we define a restriction of the shifting function where the antecedent and image configurations have the same "center". In that case we only
scale
the configuration with respect to a reference configuration
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We will use this in the following to define an homeomorphism between the cone on a sphere and a ball.

Definition 7.22
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
n=\card(X)
, and
\underline{x}\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(\{X\})
. We define the
scaling with respect to

\underline{x}
as

\displaystyle\Scalex\colon\mathbb{R}^{+}\times B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(s,Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Scalex(s,Z)=\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{x},s,Z)

Remark 7.23
.

The scaling with respect to
\underline{x}
does not depend on the choice of
\underline{x}\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(X)
, we will thus call it the
scaling with respect to

X
, written
\ScaleX
in the following.

Continuity of the scaling function directly follows from continuity of the shifting function.

Proposition 7.24
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. The function
\ScaleX
is
\omega
-continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
.

Proof.

The function
\ScaleX
is a restriction of
\Shift
, which is continuous by
7.21
.
∎

If the scaling factor is small enough, the scaling function behaves very well with the
\omega
-distance. This will be useful to show the bijectivity of the homeomorphism between the cone on the sphere and the ball.

Proposition 7.25
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
Z\in B^{\omega}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
and
s\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
. If
sd^{\omega}(X,Z)<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
then

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\ScaleX(s,Z),X)=sd^{\omega}(Z,X).

Proof.

Let us first show that

(a)

For any
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and
Z\in B^{\omega}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
, we have

d^{\omega}(\ScaleX(s,Z),X)\leq sd^{\omega}(Z,X)

.

Let us define the shift system
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{x},s,\merg(X))
. By
7.17

Item

(a)
we have

\displaystyle\ScaleX(s,Z)=\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{x},s,Z)=\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z).

By
2.24
and
4.17
there exists a
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
-local geodesic chain from
Z
to
X
. So that, by
4.18
, we have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(\ScaleX(s,Z),X)=d^{\omega}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z),\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(X))\leq sd^{\omega}(Z,X),

which shows
Item

(a)
.

Now first remark that if
s=0
then
\ScaleX(s,Z)=X
. Now if
0<sd^{\omega}(X,Z)<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
, by
7.18
, since
d_{\mathrm{H}}\leq d^{\omega}
, we can apply
7.17

Item

(b)(b).(iii)
to get

\displaystyle Z=\ScaleX\left(\frac{1}{s},\ScaleX(s,Z)\right).

By applying
Item

(a)
to
\ScaleX(s,Z)
we thus get

\displaystyle sd^{\omega}(Z,X)=sd^{\omega}\left(\ScaleX\left(\frac{1}{s},\ScaleX(s,Z)\right),X\right)\leq d^{\omega}(\ScaleX(s,Z)),X).

which concludes this proof.
∎

7.5
Conicality

We now use the shifting function to define an homeomorphism from the product of a Euclidean space and a stratified cone to an open neighborhood of a configuration in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
. First we show that the cone on the sphere is homeomorphic to the ball.

Proposition 7.26
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and let
0<\epsilon<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
. The following map

\displaystyle f\colon c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))

\displaystyle\to B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)

\displaystyle[(t,Y)]

\displaystyle\mapsto\ScaleX(t,Y)

where
c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
is the cone on the sphere
S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)=\{Z\mid d^{\omega}(Z,X)=\epsilon\}
, equipped with the teardrop topology, is an homeomorphism.

Proof.

Let
[(t,Y)]\in c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
. We have
d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\epsilon<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
and
td^{\omega}(X,Y)=t\epsilon<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
, so that we can apply
7.25
to get
d^{\omega}(f(t,Y),X)=td^{\omega}(Y,X)=t\epsilon<\epsilon
, i.e.
f(t,Y)\in B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
. Moreover, if
t=0
, we have
f(0,Y)=X
, so that
f
is a well-defined map on the cone.

Let
Z\in B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{X\}
and
s=\frac{\epsilon}{d^{\omega}(X,Z)}
. Since
d^{\omega}(X,Z)<\epsilon<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
and
sd^{\omega}(X,Z)=\epsilon<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}
we can apply
7.25
to get
d^{\omega}(\ScaleX(s,Z),X)=sd^{\omega}(Z,X)=\epsilon
. We can thus define

\displaystyle g\colon B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)

\displaystyle\to c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\left[\left(\frac{d^{\omega}(X,Z)}{\epsilon},\ScaleX(\frac{\epsilon}{d^{\omega}(X,Z)},Z)\right)\right]&\text{ if }Z\neq X\\
[(0,Y)],\text{ where }Y\in S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\quad&\text{ if }Z=X\end{cases}.

Let us show that
f
is bijective, of inverse
g
.

Let
Z\in B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
. If
Z=X
, then
f\circ g(X)=f([(0,Y)])=X
. If
Z\neq X
then let
[(t,Y)]=g(Z)
. Since
0<t<1
,
7.18
applies to the tuple
(\underline{x},\underline{x},t,Z)
, and we can thus apply
7.17

Item

(b)(b).(iii)
to get:

\displaystyle f\circ g(Z)=f\left(t,Y\right)=\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{x},t,\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{x},\frac{1}{t},Z))=Z.

We therefore have
f\circ g=\mathrm{Id}_{B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)}
.

Let
[(t,Y)]\in c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
and
[(t^{\prime},Y^{\prime})]=g\circ f(t,Y)
. We can apply
7.25
again to get

\displaystyle t^{\prime}=\frac{d^{\omega}(f(t,Y),X)}{\epsilon}=\frac{td^{\omega}(Y,X)}{\epsilon}=t.

If
t=0
, then
[(0,Y)]=[(0,Y^{\prime})]
. If
t>0
, then
d^{\omega}(f(t,Y),X)=td^{\omega}(Y,X)=t\epsilon>0
, so that
f(t,Y)\neq X
and we can apply
7.17

Item

(b)(b).(iii)
to get

\displaystyle Y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\ScaleX\left(\frac{\epsilon}{d^{\omega}(f(t,Y),X)},f(t,Y)\right)

\displaystyle=\ScaleX\left(\frac{1}{t},\ScaleX(t,Y)\right)

\displaystyle=\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{x},\frac{1}{t},\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{x},t,Y))

\displaystyle=Y.

We therefore have
g\circ f=\mathrm{Id}_{c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))}
, so that
f
is bijective, of inverse
g
.

Let
U=c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{[(0,Y)]\}
and let
h
denote the following homeomorphism

\displaystyle h\colon U

\displaystyle\to(0,1)\times S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)

\displaystyle[(t,Y)]

\displaystyle\mapsto(t,Y).

The restriction
f_{|U}
can be factorized as

\displaystyle f_{|U}=\ScaleX\circ h.

By
7.24
, it is thus continuous as a composition of continuous functions. Since
U
is an open set of
c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
,
f
is continuous on
U
. Let us show that it is continuous at
[(0,Y)]
. Let
0<\theta
. Let
\zeta=\frac{\theta}{\epsilon}
, and
[(t,Y)]\in c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))
such that
t<\zeta
. Then
d^{\omega}(f(t,Y),X)=td^{\omega}(Y,X)=t\epsilon<\zeta\epsilon=\theta
. We conclude that
f
is continuous.

Similarly, the restriction
g_{|B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{X\}}
can be factorized. By defining the following map

\displaystyle\hat{g}\colon B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{X\}

\displaystyle\to(0,1)\times S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\frac{d^{\omega}(X,Z)}{\epsilon},\ScaleX\left(\frac{\epsilon}{d^{\omega}(X,Z)},Z\right)\right),

which is continuous by
7.24
. We get
g_{|B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{X\}}=h^{-1}\circ\hat{g}
, which is also continuous.
Since
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{X\}
is an open set of
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
, the map
g
is continuous on
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\setminus\{X\}
. Let us show that it is continuous at
X
. Let
0<\zeta<1
and let us show that
g^{-1}\left(\{[(t,Y)]\in c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)\mid t<\zeta\}\right)
is open. But
g^{-1}\left(\{[(t,Y)]\in c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)\mid t<\zeta\}\right)=f\left(\{[(t,Y)]\in c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)\mid t<\zeta\}\right)=B^{\omega}(X,\zeta\epsilon)
is indeed open. Therefore
g
is continuous, which concludes this proof.
∎

Remark 7.27
.

Now it is important to remark that, even if the homeomorphism from the teardrop cone to the ball preserves the cardinality in some sense, it is not the stratified homeomorphism we are looking for. Indeed, we need to consider the stratification on the cone given in
7.1
. However, this stratification contains a single point stratum, the apex of the cone. The homeomorphism
f
maps the apex to
X
, the center of the ball
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
, which does not lie alone in its stratum. On the contrary, there are many points in
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
with the same cardinality as that of
X
, specifically,
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap\mathrm{Ran}_{=}n(M)
is homeomorphic to a Euclidean space of dimension
p\card(X)
.

We will thus consider another cone, which will be homeomorphic to a slice of the ball, in which
X
is the only point with its cardinality. To achieve this, we will restrict our attention to configurations where each cluster, in the sense of
7.9
, is "centered" around the corresponding point in
X
.
In this way, we will produce subobjects
B\subset B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
and
S\subset S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
, which will be such that
f
restricts to a bijection from
c(S)
to
B
.
Since the teardrop cone satisfies the property that cone of subspaces are subspaces of the cone, this will be enough to conclude that the restriction of
f
to
c(S)
and
B
is in fact a homeomorphism.

Now, we need to define an appropriate notion for the "center" of a configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We cannot naively use the iso-barycenter, since it is not continuous on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, as can be seen by considering merges. We thus define the
mid-point
of a configuration, and show that it is continuous.

Definition 7.28
.

Recall that
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
. The
mid-point
function is defined as

\displaystyle\Mid\colon\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle\to M

\displaystyle X

\displaystyle\mapsto\frac{1}{2}\left(\min_{x\in X}\{x_{1}\}+\max_{x\in X}\{x_{1}\},\dots,\min_{x\in X}\{x_{p}\}+\max_{x\in X}\{x_{p}\}\right).

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
. The mid-point function is extended to
n
-tuples as

\displaystyle\Mid^{n}\colon\mathrm{Ran}^{n}(M)

\displaystyle\to M^{n}

\displaystyle(X^{(1)},\dots,X^{(n)})

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\Mid(X^{(1)}),\dots,\Mid(X^{(n)})\right).

Let
\underline{x}\in M^{n}
and
X=\pi_{n}(\underline{x})
. The
\underline{x}
-mid-point function is defined as

\displaystyle\MidX\colon B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))

\displaystyle\to M^{n}

\displaystyle Y

\displaystyle\mapsto\Mid^{n}(\ClustX(Y)).

Remark 7.29
.

We have
\MidX(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))=\underline{x}
.

Remark 7.30
.

For any
Y\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
,
\card(\pi_{n}(\MidX(Y)))=n
by
4.16
.

Proposition 7.31
.

The mid-point is
p
-Lipschitz for
d_{\mathrm{H}}
and thus for
d^{\omega}
. More specifically, it is continuous on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{H}})
and on
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
.

Proof.

Let
X,Y\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. By the triangle inequality, we have

\displaystyle d(\Mid(X),\Mid(Y))

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Mid(X)-\Mid(Y)\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{p}\left\lvert\min_{x\in X}\{x_{i}\}-\min_{y\in Y}\{y_{i}\}\right\rvert+\left\lvert\max_{x\in X}\{x_{i}\}-\max_{y\in Y}\{y_{i}\}\right\rvert.

Let
i\in\{1,\dots,p\}
. There exists
\widetilde{x}\in X
such that
\widetilde{x}_{i}=\min_{x\in X}\{x_{i}\}
and
\widetilde{y}\in Y
such that
\widetilde{y}_{i}=\min_{y\in Y}\{y_{i}\}
. Let us first suppose that
\widetilde{x}_{i}\leq\widetilde{y}_{i}
. There exists
z\in Y
such that
d(\widetilde{x},z)=d(\widetilde{x},Y)
. We have

\displaystyle\left\lvert\min_{x\in X}\{x_{i}\}-\min_{y\in Y}\{y_{i}\}\right\rvert

\displaystyle=\widetilde{y}_{i}-\widetilde{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq z_{i}-\widetilde{x}_{i}

\displaystyle\leq\left\lvert z_{i}-\widetilde{x}_{i}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq d(z,\widetilde{x})

\displaystyle\leq d(\widetilde{x},Y)

\displaystyle\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y).

By symmetry, we get the same results if
\widetilde{x}_{i}\geq\widetilde{y}_{i}
. Similarly we get

\displaystyle\left\lvert\max_{x\in X}\{x_{i}\}-\max_{y\in Y}\{y_{i}\}\right\rvert\leq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y).

Finally, we have

\displaystyle d(\Mid(X),\Mid(Y))\leq\sum_{i=1}^{p}d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)=pd_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\leq pd^{\omega}(X,Y).

∎

Corollary 7.32
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\underline{x}\in M^{n}
,
X=\pi_{n}(\underline{x})
. The function
\MidX
is continuous on
B^{\omega}(X,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
.

Proof.

By
7.11
,
\ClustX
is continuous, thus
\MidX
is continuous as a composition of continuous functions.
∎

If the scaling factor is small enough, more precisely if the shift system is bijective, then the mid-point, in some sense, commutes with the shifting function. This is the subject of the following proposition.

Proposition 7.33
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
, and let
\Sigma=(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,r)
be a bijective
n
-shift system. Then for any
Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),r)
, we have

\displaystyle\Mid_{\underline{y}}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z))=\phi^{n}_{\Sigma}(\MidX(Z)).

Proof.

Let
Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),r)
, and
(Z^{(1)},\dots,Z^{(n)})=\ClustX(Z)
. Then
Z=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{n}Z^{(i)}
and
\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z)=\bigcup_{i=1}^{n}\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z^{(i)})
. For all
1\leq i\leq n
,
\phi_{\Sigma}(Z^{(i)})\subset B(y^{(i)},\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{y})))
, so that
\Clust_{\underline{y}}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z))=(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z^{(1)}),\dots,\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z^{(n)}))
, and

\displaystyle\Mid_{\underline{y}}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z))=\left(\Mid(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z^{(1)})),\dots,\Mid(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z^{(n)}))\right).

Let
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, then, for any
z\in Z^{(i)}
,
\phi_{\Sigma}(z)=s(z-x^{(i)})+y^{(i)}
. Since addition with a constant term and scaling by a positive factor commute with taking the minimum (resp. the maximum) of a set, we get:

\displaystyle\Mid(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z^{(i)}))=\phi_{\Sigma}(\Mid(Z^{(i)})).

Therefore

\displaystyle\Mid_{\underline{y}}(\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z))=\left(\phi_{\Sigma}(\Mid(Z^{(1)})),\dots,\phi_{\Sigma}(\Mid(Z^{(n)}))\right)=\phi_{\Sigma}^{n}(\MidX(Z)).

∎

We deduce an important property of the mid-point that will be useful when defining inverses.

Corollary 7.34
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and
(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\in A_{n}
. If
\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))=n
,
sd^{\omega}(Z,\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))<\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}))
, and
\MidX(Z)=\underline{x}
, then

\displaystyle\Mid_{\underline{y}}(\Shift(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z))=\underline{y}.

We can now define a stratified homeomorphism between a stratified cone and a subspace of the ball by restricting the previous (unstratified) homeomorphism to "centered" configurations, i.e. configurations whose mid-point is the center of the ball/sphere.

Proposition 7.35
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration,
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
,
n=\card(X)
and
\underline{x}\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(\{X\})
. Let
C_{\underline{x}}=\{Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))\mid\MidX(Z)=\underline{x}\}
. The map

\displaystyle f\colon c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)

\displaystyle\to B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}

\displaystyle[(t,Y)]

\displaystyle\mapsto\ScaleX(t,Y)

is a stratified homeomorphism for the stratification induced by the cardinality map.

Proof.

Let us define the map
\tilde{f}
as in
7.26
:

\displaystyle\tilde{f}\colon c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)

\displaystyle\to B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)

\displaystyle[(t,Y)]

\displaystyle\mapsto\ScaleX(t,Y),

which is a homeomorphism. Let us define the following subset of the cone:

\displaystyle D=\left\{[(t,Y)]\in c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)\mid Y\in C_{\underline{x}}\right\}.

Note that, one of the key properties of the teardrop topology, is that, whenever we have a space and a subspace
L\subset L^{\prime}
, the inclusion
c(L)\hookrightarrow c(L^{\prime})
is a homeomorphism onto its image.
Hence, the following map

\displaystyle h\colon c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)

\displaystyle\to D

\displaystyle[(t,Y)]

\displaystyle\mapsto[(t,Y)].

is a homeomorphism.

We then construct
f
as suggested by the following diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c(S^{\omega}\cap C_{\underline{x}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}

By
7.34
, for any
Y\in\overline{B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)}\cap C_{\underline{x}}
and for any
t\leq\frac{\epsilon}{d^{\omega}(X,Y)}
, we have
\MidX(\ScaleX(t,Y))=\underline{x}
. Therefore,
\tilde{f}
restricts to a homeomorphism from
D
to
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
. We can thus define the homeomorphism
f
as the composition

f=\tilde{f}|_{D}^{B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}}\circ h.

One easily checks that this corresponds to the formula given in the statement.

Let us now make explicit the stratification on the cone. Let
Y\in S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
, and let us show that
\card(Y)\geq n+1
. Assume to the contrary that
\card(Y)\leq n
. Since
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\epsilon<\frac{\merg{X}}{2}
, we must have
\card(Y)\geq n
and thus
\card(Y)=n
. Furthermore, this immediately implies that for any
1\leq i\leq n
,
\card(Y\cap B(x^{(i)},\epsilon))=1
. But since, by assumption,
\Mid_{\underline{x}}(Y)=X
, we have that
Y\cap B(x^{(i)},\epsilon)=\{x^{(i)}\}
and finally, we get that
Y=X
, which contradicts the fact that
d^{\omega}(X,Y)=\epsilon>0
. Thus,
\card\colon S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq 1}
takes values into
\mathbb{N}_{\geq n+1}
and so we can consider
S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
as naturally stratified over
\mathbb{N}_{\geq n+1}
. Its cone is then stratified over the same poset, with an added minimal element, which we can identify with
n
, so that
c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)
is stratified over the poset
\mathbb{N}_{\geq n}
with
[(0,Y)]
being sent to
n
while every other point
[(t,Y)]
is sent to
\card(Y)
.

We can now show that
f
is a stratified homeomorphism. Let
[(t,Y)]\in c\left(S^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)
. If
t=0
, then
\card(f(t,Y))=\card(X)=n
. If
t>0
, by
7.17

Item

(b)
,
\card(f(t,Y))=\card(Y)
. Which concludes this proof.
∎

Note that we are not done yet, because the image of this homeomorphism is not open in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. We thus need to take the product with a Euclidean space that somewhat encodes the position of the mid-point of a configuration in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Proposition 7.36
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration, and
n=\card(X)
its cardinality. There exist
U
an open neighborhood of
X
in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
, E a Euclidean space of dimension
np
,
\varphi\colon L\to\{n+1,\dots\}
a stratified space and
h\colon U\to E\times c(L)
a stratified homeomorphism where the stratification on
U
is given by the cardinality
\card_{|U}\colon U\to\{n,\dots\}
.

Proof.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
n=\card{X}
,
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
and
\underline{x}=(x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)})\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(X)
. By
5.8
,
\pi_{n}^{-1}\left(B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)
is an open set of
M^{n}
containing
\underline{x}
, so that there exists
\theta\in(0,\epsilon]
such that
\underline{x}\in B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\theta)\subset\pi_{n}^{-1}\left(B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\right)
. Let
E=B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\theta)
, which is homeomorphic to a Euclidean space of dimension
np
.

Let
g^{(j)}=(4j,0,\dots,0)\in M
for all
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
be
n
points in
M
. Let
\underline{g}=(g^{(1)},\dots,g^{(n)})
,
G=\pi_{n}(\underline{g})
, and
V=\left\{Z\in B^{\omega}(G,1)\mid\Mid_{\underline{g}}\left(Z\right)=\underline{g}\right\}
.

We now define the following map

\displaystyle f\colon E\times V

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Ran}(M)

\displaystyle(\underline{y},Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}),X),Z\right).

Since
\merg(G)=2
, we have
V\subset B^{\omega}(G,\frac{\merg(G)}{2})\subset\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(G,\frac{\merg(G)}{2})
, and
f
is thus continuous by
7.21
.

We will now construct its inverse. Let us denote
U
the image of
f
, and let us first show that
U\subset B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
. Let
W\in U
, there exists
(\underline{y},Z)\in E\times V
such that
f(\underline{y},Z)=W
. Let us denote
Y=\pi_{n}(\underline{y})
. Since
Z\in B^{\omega}(G,\frac{\merg(G)}{2})
, we can apply
7.19

Item

(d)
to get:

\displaystyle d^{\omega}\left(W,Y\right)

\displaystyle=d^{\omega}\left(\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y,X),Z\right),\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y,X),G\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\epsilon-d^{\omega}\left(Y,X\right))d^{\omega}(Z,G)

\displaystyle<\epsilon-d^{\omega}\left(Y,X\right).

and

\displaystyle d^{\omega}\left(W,X\right)\leq d^{\omega}\left(W,Y\right)+d^{\omega}\left(Y,X\right)<\epsilon-d^{\omega}\left(Y,X\right)+d^{\omega}\left(Y,X\right)=\epsilon,

so that indeed
U\subset B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
.

Let us now compare the merging radii of
X
and
Y
. Let
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Since
Y
is
B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))
-local, there is a unique
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
d(y^{(i)},x^{(j)})<\merg(X)
. But we also have

\displaystyle d(y^{(i)},x^{(i)})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}d(x^{(i)},y^{(i)})=\left\lVert\underline{x}-\underline{y}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}<\theta\leq\epsilon<\merg(X),

so that
j=i
, and
d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\geq\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\{d(y^{(i)},x^{(i)})\}
. If
n=1
then
\merg(X)=\merg(Y)
. Otherwise, let
i\neq j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. We have

\displaystyle d(y^{(i)},y^{(j)})\geq d(x^{(i)},x^{(j)})-d(y^{(i)},x^{(i)})-d(x^{(j)},y^{(j)})\geq 2\merg(X)-2d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y),

so that

\displaystyle\merg(Y)\geq\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y).

(2)

Let us now show that
\MidX(W)=\underline{y}
. Let
w\in W=f(\underline{y},Z)
. Since
d^{\omega}(W,X)<\epsilon<\merg(X)
, there is a unique
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
such that
w\in B(x^{(i)},\epsilon)
. Let us define the shift system
\Sigma=(\underline{g},\underline{y},(\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y)),1)
. Since
Z\in B^{\omega}(G,1)
, by
7.17
,
f(\underline{y},Z)=\bm{\phi}_{\Sigma}(Z)
. Therefore, there exists
j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
z\in Z\cap B(g^{(j)},1)
such that

\displaystyle w=y^{(j)}+(\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y))(z-g^{(j)}).

But then

\displaystyle d(w,y^{(j)})=(\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y))d(z,g^{(j)})<\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y)<\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\leq\merg(Y),

and thus

\displaystyle d(x^{(i)},y^{(j)})\leq d(x^{(i)},w)+d(w,y^{(j)})<2\epsilon<\merg(X),

so that
j=i
. So that
\ClustX(W)=\Clust_{\underline{y}}(W)
, and, by
7.34
,

\displaystyle\MidX(W)=\Mid_{\underline{y}}(W)=\underline{y}.

Thus
\MidX(W)\in E
so that
\card(\pi_{n}(\MidX(W)))=n
and
d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\MidX(W)),X)<\epsilon
.
Moreover, we can apply
7.19

Item

(d)
to get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(W,Y)

\displaystyle=d^{\omega}(\Shift(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y),Z),\Shift(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y),G))

\displaystyle\leq(\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y))d^{\omega}(Z,G)

\displaystyle<\frac{\merg(X)}{2}-\frac{d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)}{2}\quad\text{since }d^{\omega}(X,Y)\geq d_{\mathrm{H}}(X,Y)\text{ and }d^{\omega}(Z,G)<1

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\merg(Y)}{2}\quad\text{here we use \eqref{eq:radX radY}.}

so that

\displaystyle\left(\MidX(W),\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\MidX(W)),X)},W\right)=\left(\underline{y},\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y,X)},W\right)\in B_{n}.

We can therefore define the following map

\displaystyle h\colon U

\displaystyle\to E\times V

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\MidX(W),\Shift\left(\MidX(W),\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\MidX(W)),X)},W\right)\right),

which is continuous as a composition of continuous functions by
7.21
and
7.31
.

Let us show that the image of
h
indeed lies in
E\times V
and that it defines an inverse of
f^{|U}
. We already showed that
\MidX(W)\in E
for any
W\in U
. Let
(\underline{y},Z)\in E\times V
and
Y=\pi_{n}(\underline{y})
. We already know that
\MidX(f(\underline{y},Z))=\underline{y}
. Moreover, as seen above
(\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y))d^{\omega}(Z,G)<\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y)<\merg(Y)
, so that by
7.18
we can apply
7.17

Item

(b)(b).(iii)
to get

\displaystyle\Shift\left(\MidX(f(\underline{y},Z)),\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(\pi_{n}(\MidX(f(\underline{y},Z))),X)},f(\underline{y},Z)\right)

\displaystyle=\Shift\left(\underline{y},\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y,X)},\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y,X),Z\right)\right)

\displaystyle=Z.

This shows indeed that
\Ima(h)\subset E\times V
and that
h\circ f=\mathrm{Id}_{E\times V}
. Injectivity of
h\circ f
allows to conclude injectivity of
f
, so that
f
is a bijection on its image, of inverse
h
. Therefore
f^{|U}
is a homeomorphism.
We can also apply
7.17

Item

(b)(b).(ii)
to get that

\displaystyle\card(f(\underline{y},Z))=\card\left(\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y,X),Z\right)\right)=\card(Z).

We conclude that
f^{|U}
is a stratified homeomorphism.

Finally, let us show that
U
is open in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Let
\underline{y}_{1}\in E=B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\theta)
,

Y_{1}=\pi_{n}(\underline{y}_{1})
,
Z_{1}\in V
, and
W_{1}=f(\underline{y}_{1},Z_{1})\in U
,
and let us find
N\subset U
an open neighborhood of
W_{1}
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.
As seen above,
7.19

Item

(d)
gives:

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(W_{1},Y_{1})

\displaystyle=d^{\omega}\left(\Shift(\underline{g},\underline{y}_{1},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},X),Z_{1}),\Shift(\underline{g},\underline{y}_{1},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},X),G)\right)

\displaystyle\leq(\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},X))d^{\omega}(W_{1},G)

\displaystyle<\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},X).

Since, by
7.32
,
\MidX
is continuous on
B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)
, we may choose
\zeta>0
such that, for any
W_{2}\in B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap B^{\omega}(W_{1},\zeta)
, the following holds:

\left\lVert\MidX(W_{1})-\MidX(W_{2})\right\rVert_{\Sigma}<\min\left\{\theta-\left\lVert\underline{y}_{1}-\underline{x}\right\rVert_{\Sigma},\frac{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},W_{1})}{4\omega(n)}\right\}.

(3)

We then choose

\displaystyle\delta=\min\left\{\zeta,\frac{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},W_{1})}{2}\right\}.

Let us define
N=B^{\omega}(X,\epsilon)\cap B^{\omega}(W_{1},\delta)
and let
W_{2}\in N
and let us show that
W_{2}\in U
, i.e. let us find
(\underline{y}_{2},Z_{2})\in E\times V
such that
f(\underline{y}_{2},Z_{2})=W_{2}
. Let us first define
\underline{y}_{2}=\MidX(W_{2})
, and
Y_{2}=\pi_{n}(\underline{y}_{2})
. We then have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\underline{y}_{2}-\underline{x}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}\leq\left\lVert\underline{y}_{2}-\underline{y}_{1}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}+\left\lVert\underline{y}_{1}-\underline{x}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}<\theta,

so that
\underline{y}_{2}\in E
, and, by
7.30
,
\card(Y_{2})=n
. Moreover,

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(X,Y_{2})

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})+d^{\omega}(Y_{1},Y_{2})

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})+\omega(n)\left\lVert\underline{y}_{1}-\underline{y}_{2}\right\rVert_{\Sigma}

by
7.20

\displaystyle<\frac{\epsilon}{4}+\frac{3}{4}d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},W_{1})

by
Eq.

3

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon.

And we also have

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(W_{2},Y_{2})

\displaystyle\leq d^{\omega}(W_{2},W_{1})+d^{\omega}(W_{1},Y_{1})+d^{\omega}(Y_{1},Y_{2})

\displaystyle<\delta+d^{\omega}(W_{1},Y_{1})+2d^{\omega}(Y_{1},Y_{2})+d^{\omega}(Y_{1},X)-d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X)

\displaystyle<\frac{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},W_{1})}{2}+d^{\omega}(W_{1},Y_{1})+\frac{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},W_{1})}{2}

\displaystyle\phantom{<\frac{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{1})-d^{\omega}(Y_{1},W_{1})}{2}}+d^{\omega}(Y_{1},X)-d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X)

\displaystyle<\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X)

\displaystyle<\merg(X)-d_{\mathrm{H}}(Y_{2},X)

\displaystyle\leq\merg(Y_{2}).

Where we first used the definition of
N
together with the triangle inequality applied to
d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X)
. Then, on the third line, we used the definition of
\delta
, together with (
3
). The last line is (
2
) applied to
Y_{2}
, which holds because
\underline{y_{2}}\in E
.

We have thus checked that we can define the following :

\displaystyle Z_{2}=\Shift\left(\underline{y}_{2},\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{2})},W_{2}\right).

Let us now show that
Z_{2}\in V
. We can apply
7.19
Item

(d)
to get

\displaystyle d^{\omega}(Z_{2},G)

\displaystyle=d^{\omega}\left(\Shift\left(\underline{y}_{2},\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{2})},W_{2}\right),\Shift\left(\underline{y}_{2},\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{2})},Y_{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(X,Y_{2})}d^{\omega}(W_{2},Y_{2})

\displaystyle<1,

and, by
7.34
,

\displaystyle\Mid_{\underline{g}}(Z_{2})=\Mid_{\underline{g}}\left(\Shift\left(\MidX(W_{2}),\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X)},W_{2}\right)\right)=\underline{g},

Finally, by
7.18
we can apply
7.17

Item

(b)(b).(iii)
to get

\displaystyle f(\underline{y}_{2},Z_{2})

\displaystyle=\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y}_{2},\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X),\Shift\left(\underline{y}_{2},\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\omega}(Y_{2},X)},W_{2}\right)\right)=W_{2}.

Therefore
N\subset U
so that
U
is open. We finally conclude this proof using
7.35
to show that
V
is indeed a cone with the appropriate stratification.

∎

Corollary 7.37
.

Let
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
, and
\omega
be a weight.
Then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is conically stratified.

Corollary 7.38
.

Let
(M,d)
be a Riemannian manifold, and
\omega
be a weight. Then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is conically stratified.

Proof.

Write
p=\dim(M)
, and let
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Order the elements of
X=\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}\}
. Given some
1\leq i\leq n
, and any sufficiently small neighborhood of
x^{(i)}
in
M
,
E_{i}
, there exists a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism
\varphi_{i}\colon E_{i}\to B((3i,0,\dots,0),1)
where
B((3i,0,\dots,0),1)
is the unit ball in the Euclidean space
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. An elementary proof of this fact can be found in
[
23
]
.
Furthermore, up to restricting the
\varphi_{i}
to smaller neighborhoods, we may assume that the
E_{i}
are disjoint. In fact, we will ask that each
E_{i}
is a subset of a neighborhood of the form
B_{M}(x^{(i)},r)
for some
r>0
such that
d(x^{(i)},x^{(j)})>3r
for all
1\leq i\not=j\leq n
. Here
B_{M}(x,r)
denotes the ball in
M
with center
x
and radius
r
.

Let
E=\coprod E_{i}
,
B=\coprod B((3i,0,\dots,0),1)
and denote
\varphi\colon E\to B
restricting to
\varphi_{i}
on
E_{i}
. Let us show that
\varphi
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism. First, note that for any
i
and any
(y,z)\in E_{i}^{2}
, there exists
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}>0
, such that
\alpha_{i}d(\varphi(y),\varphi(z))\leq d(y,z)\leq\beta_{i}d(\varphi(y),\varphi(z))
, by hypothesis on the
\varphi_{i}
.
On the other hand, if
y\in E_{i}
and
z\in E_{j}
for
i\not=j
, then
r<d(y,z)<\diam(X)+2r
, by construction, while
1<d(\varphi(y),\varphi(z))<3n-1
. Let
\beta=\max\{\diam(X)+2r,\beta_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
and
\alpha=\min\{\frac{r}{3n-1},\alpha_{i}\mid 1\leq i\leq n\}
. We get

\alpha d(\varphi(y),\varphi(z))\leq d(y,z)\leq\beta d(\phi(y),\phi(z)),\ \forall(y,z)\in E^{2}.

In particular,
\varphi
is a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism between
E
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
.

Now, the inclusions
E\subset M
and
B\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
induce open embeddings
\mathrm{Ran}(E)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ran}(M)
and
\mathrm{Ran}(B)\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
for the topology
\tau^{\omega}
, by
4.12
. Since being conically stratified is a local property, then
\mathrm{Ran}(B)
is conically stratified by
7.37
. Furthermore, by
4.21
,
\mathrm{Ran}(E)
and
\mathrm{Ran}(B)
are homeomorphic in the stratified sense, so
\mathrm{Ran}(E)
is also conically stratified, and it is a neighborhood of
X
for the Hausdorff topology, by construction. Since the topology
\tau^{\omega}
refines the Hausdorff topology, by
5.7
, then
E
is also a neighborhood of
X
for the topology
\tau^{\omega}
. Finally, we have showed that any
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
