admits a neighborhood for the topology
\tau^{\omega}
which is conically stratified, which proves the claim.
∎

Remark 7.39
.

Notice that in the previous proof, we didn’t use explicitly the Riemmanian metric on
M
, but only the existence of local homeomorphism to the Euclidean space that are bi-Lipschitz. This suggests a more general construction. Indeed, assume that, instead of a specific Riemannian metric on
M
one fixes an atlas, where for any charts
\varphi\colon U\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
\psi\colon V\to\mathbb{R}^{p}
the (partial) composition
\varphi\circ\psi^{-1}\colon\psi(V\cap U)\to\varphi(V\cap U)
is bi-Lipschitz. This is enough to define a topology
\tau^{\omega}
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
as follows. For
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, pick disjoint charts around each
x\in X
,
(U_{x},\phi_{x})
, and let
E=\coprod_{x\in X}U_{x}
be the disjoint union of the domains of those charts.
Up to translating their image, we may assume that we have a homeomorphism
\phi\colon E\to B
, where
B
is an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{p}
. We may then define a topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(E)
by transporting that of
(\mathrm{Ran}(B),\tau^{\omega})
along the homeomorphism. The condition on the atlas will guarantee that the topology around
X
does not depend on a choice of local charts, and since all such open neighborhoods cover
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, we have indeed defined a topology on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
.

Note that this kind of atlas corresponds to the structure of a Lipschitz manifold, as defined by Sullivan in
[
31
]
, thus we may rephrase the above remark as saying that for any Lipschitz manifold,
M
,
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
admits a topology
\tau^{\omega}
, which makes
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
into a conically stratified space.

Furthermore,
[
31
, Corollary 3]
, states that
any
topological manifold
M
, of dimension other than
4
admits a
unique
Lipschitz structure, up to a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism that is close to the identity. This means that given a topological manifold of dimension other than
4
, we may speak of the homeomorphism type of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
without the need for further data.

There are subtleties however. First, observe that if
(M,d)
is a metric space, such that
M
happens to be a topological manifold, this does not mean that there exists an atlas for
M
composed of maps which are locally bi-Lipschitz with respect to
d
. In particular, the distance
d^{\omega}
, induced by
d
may not induce the topology
\tau^{\omega}
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
defined in the previous paragraphs.

Secondly, while the homeomorphism type of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is well-defined knowing only that
M
is a manifold, if we are equipped with two distinct Lipschitz atlases on
M
, they will provide two distinct topologies
\tau^{\omega}_{1}
and
\tau^{\omega}_{2}
on
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. And while
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega}_{1})
and
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega}_{2})
will be homeomorphic stratified spaces, there is no reason to expect that the homeomorphism be given by the identity. Succinctly, while the homeomorphism type is well-defined, the topology may not be.

7.6
Naive conicality of the final topology

We showed in the previous section that given a Riemannian manifold
(M,d)
, the associated Ran space with the weighted topology,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is a conically stratified space for any weight
\omega
. A similar statement for the Hausdorff topology was proved by Čepek and Lejay in
[
10
]
. One may expect this statement to also hold for the final topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, but it is very easy to show that it cannot be true (see
7.40
). We show however that
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
satisfies a distinct, but somewhat similar property. Specifically, we can still find an open neighborhood of any configuration that is stratified homeomorphic to the product of a Euclidean space and a stratified cone, except the cone is now equipped with the quotient topology instead of the teardrop topology.

Proposition 7.40
.

Let
M
be a manifold of dimension at least
1
. Then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is not conically stratified.

Proof.

Assume to the contrary that a stratified homeomorphism
f\colon U\to E\times c(L)
exists, where
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
is any non-empty subset, open for the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
Since
U
is non-empty, there must be some
N\geq 1
such that
\pi_{N}^{-1}(U)\not=\emptyset
. Let
n
be the least such
N
. For any
k\geq n
,
\pi_{k}^{-1}(U)
is an open subset of
M^{k}
by assumption. But since
M^{k+1}\setminus M^{k}
is dense in
M^{k+1}
for all
k\geq n
,
U
must contain a configuration of cardinality
k
for all
k\geq n
.
In particular,
c(L)
must be stratified over
\mathbb{N}_{\geq n}
, and so
L
must be stratified over
\mathbb{N}_{\geq n+1}
, with at least one point in each stratum. Let us denote by
\varphi\colon L\to\mathbb{N}_{\geq n+1}
its stratification. Let
(X_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of elements in
L
such that
\varphi(X_{k})
is strictly increasing. Now, consider
(t_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
some sequence in
(0,1)
which decreases to
0
as
k
goes to infinity. By definition of the teardrop topology, the sequence
(t_{k},X_{k})
must converge to the apex in
c(L)
. On the other hand, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
f^{-1}(t_{k},X_{k})
has cardinality
\varphi(X_{k})
, thus the sequence of cardinalities
\card(f^{-1}(t_{k},X_{k}))
is unbounded, which implies, by
1.12
, that the sequence
(f^{-1}(t_{k},X_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
does not converge in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. This contradicts the assumption that
f
is a homeomorphism.
∎

Now, to construct our homeomorphism, we start by showing that the shifting function is continuous for the final topology.

Proposition 7.41
.

Let
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
,
n\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
and let us write, as in the previous section
B_{n}=\{(\underline{x},\underline{y},s,Z)\in M^{n}\times M^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times\mathrm{Ran}(M)\mid\card(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))=n,Z\in\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}),\frac{\merg(\pi_{n}(\underline{x}))}{2})\}
.
The function
\Shift
is continuous from
(B_{n},\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
to
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, where
(B_{n},\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is equipped with the product topology of
M^{n}\times M^{n}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}\times(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
.

Proof.

By
7.21
, for any weight
\omega
, the following composition is continuous

\displaystyle(B_{n},\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\overset{\mathrm{Id}}{\to}(B_{n},\tau^{\omega})\overset{\Shift}{\to}(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega}).

By
5.9
, we can directly conclude.
∎

The key technical step which distinguishes this proof from the one from
Section

7.5
is in the next proposition. In
Section

7.5
, we considered balls and spheres associated with a weight
\omega
, and considered continuity with respect to the topology
\tau^{\omega}
associated to the same weight. Here, we are working with the final topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, which is not metrizable and so we do not have access to balls. On the other hand, any ball for any weight
\omega
is an open set in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. Since we still need to define ball-shaped subspaces of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, in order to produce our homeomorphisms, we will consider the balls associated to the
fixed
weight
\chi
, which we define to be constant equal to
1
. However, those subspaces of
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
are not equipped with the topology induced from
d^{\chi}
but instead are equipped with the restriction of
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Now, we know from
7.40
that no open subset of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
can be conical, essentially because there are two many open subsets in the final topology. Thus, the maps appearing in
7.26
and
7.35
will still be open for the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, but they will fail to be continuous. Replacing the topology on the cone
c(L)
by the
quotient topology
, that is, the finest topology making
\pr\colon L\times[0,1)\to c(L)
continuous, will alleviate this obstruction, which we show in the following proposition. From now on, we write
c_{q}(L)
for the cone on
L
equipped with the quotient topology.

Proposition 7.42
.

Let
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration,
\epsilon\in(0,\frac{\merg(X)}{2})
,
n=\card(X)
and
\underline{x}\in\pi_{n}^{-1}(\{X\})
. Let
C_{\underline{x}}=\{Z\in B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\merg(X))\mid\MidX(Z)=\underline{x}\}
. The map

\displaystyle f\colon c_{q}\left(\left(S^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)

\displaystyle\to\left(B^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}

\displaystyle[(t,Y)]

\displaystyle\mapsto\ScaleX(t,Y)

is a stratified homeomorphism.

Proof.

Let us denote
\pr\colon[0,1)\times\left(\left(S^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)\to c_{q}\left(\left(S^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)
the quotient map. Then, by
7.41
, the composition
f\circ\pr
is continuous as a restriction of the
\Shift
function, and so is
f
by definition of the quotient topology.

Moreover, by
7.35
,
f
is a stratified bijection of inverse

\displaystyle g\colon\left(B^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}

\displaystyle\to c_{q}\left(\left(S^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)

\displaystyle Z

\displaystyle\mapsto\begin{cases}\left[\left(\frac{d^{\chi}(X,Z)}{\epsilon},\ScaleX\left(\frac{\epsilon}{d^{\chi}(X,Z)},Z\right)\right)\right]&\text{if }Z\neq X\\
[(0,Y)]\quad\text{where }Y\in S^{\chi}(X,\epsilon)&\text{if }Z=X\end{cases}

To show that
g
is continuous, remark that
\left(B^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
is a clopen in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
. We can thus use
1.14
, so that it is enough to show that the restrictions
g_{\leq N}
of
g
to the union of strata of cardinality
\leq N
are continuous for all
N\geq 1
.
Note that since
B^{\chi}(X,\epsilon)\subset B_{\mathrm{H}}(X,\epsilon)\subset\mathrm{Ran}_{\geq n}(M)
, we can restrict ourselves to
N\geq n
. Let
N\geq n
. Then, by
7.41
,
g_{\leq N}
is continuous on
\left(B^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\setminus\{X\},\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)
as a restriction and composition of continuous functions. Let us show that it is continuous at
X
. Let
U\subset c_{q}\left(\left(S^{\chi}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)
be an open neighborhood of the apex of the cone, and let us find
V
, an open neighborhood of
X
, in
g^{-1}_{\leq N}(U)\subset\left(B^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}\right)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
.

Remark that
S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
is a closed subset of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, so that its preimage by
\pi_{N}
is a closed subset of
M^{N}
. It is also bounded in
M^{N}
, so that it is compact.
Since
\pi_{N}
is surjective, we have
S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}=\pi_{N}(\pi_{N}^{-1}(S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}))
which is compact as the image of a compact by
\pi_{N}
.
Therefore
\{0\}\times\left(S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)\subset\pr^{-1}(U)
is compact,
hence there exists
0<\delta_{N}\leq 1
such that
[0,\delta_{N})\times\left(S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)\subset\pr^{-1}(U)
.
We can thus define
V=B^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon\delta_{N})\cap C_{\underline{x}}
, which is an open subset of
B^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
. We have indeed
X\in V
.
It remains to be showed that
V\subset g^{-1}_{\leq N}(U)
.
Let
Z\in V
and let us show that
Z\in g^{-1}_{\leq N}(U)
, i.e. that
g_{\leq N}(Z)=[(t,Y)]\in U
. There is nothing to check for
Y
, since we already know that
Y\in S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}
. Besides,
t=\frac{d^{\chi}(X,Z)}{\epsilon}<\delta_{N}
, so that
g_{\leq N}(Z)=[(t,Y)]\in\pr\left([0,\delta_{N})\times\left(S^{\chi}_{\leq N}(X,\epsilon)\cap C_{\underline{x}}\right)\right)\subset U
. We conclude that
g_{\leq N}
is continuous at
X
.

Hence
g_{\leq N}
is continuous for any
N\geq 1
, which, by
1.14
proves that
g
is continuous. Therefore
f
is indeed a stratified homeomorphism.
∎

The rest of the proof is mostly unchanged, so that we can finally conclude.

Theorem 7.43
.

Let
M=\mathbb{R}^{p}
and
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
be a configuration, and
n=\card(X)
its cardinality. There exist
U
an open neighborhood of
X
in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
, E a Euclidean space of dimension
np
,
\varphi\colon L\to\{n+1,\dots\}
a stratified space and
h\colon U\to E\times c_{q}(L)
a stratified homeomorphism where
c_{q}(L)
is the stratified cone on
L
equipped with the quotient topology.

Proof.

Recall, in the proof of
7.36
, we introduced a stratified homeomorphism (with an arbitrary weight
\omega
instead of the minimal weight
\chi
),

\displaystyle f\colon E\times V

\displaystyle\to U

\displaystyle(\underline{y},Z)

\displaystyle\mapsto\Shift\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\chi}(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}),X),Z\right)

of inverse

\displaystyle f^{-1}\colon U

\displaystyle\to E\times V

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle\mapsto\left(\MidX(W),\Shift\left(\MidX(W),\underline{g},\frac{1}{\epsilon-d^{\chi}(\pi_{n}(\MidX(W)),X)},W\right)\right).

where
E=B_{\Sigma}(\underline{x},\theta)
,
V=\left\{Z\in B^{\chi}(G,1)\mid\Mid_{\underline{g}}\left(Z\right)=\underline{g}\right\}
, and
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
was defined to be the image, which we proved to be open for the topology
\tau^{\chi}
.
This implies in particular that
U
is an open subset for the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
, by
5.8
. In addition, as shown in the proof of
7.36
,
V\subset\overline{B}_{\mathrm{H}}(G,\frac{\merg{G}}{2})
, so that for any
(\underline{y},Z)
in
E\times V
,
\left(\underline{g},\underline{y},\epsilon-d^{\chi}(\pi_{n}(\underline{y}),X),Z\right)
lies in
B_{n}
. Now, since
d^{\chi}
is continuous for the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
,
f
is a composition of the
\Shift
function with continuous functions, hence by
7.41
,
f
is still continuous when both
V
and
U
are equipped with the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.
Moreover, by
7.32
,
\MidX
is continuous for
\tau^{\chi}
hence also for
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
. Hence, by
7.41

f^{-1}
is also continuous, and hence
f
is indeed a stratified homeomorphism when
V
and
U
are equipped with the topology
\tau_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

We conclude with
7.42
to show that
V
is a stratified cone equipped with the quotient topology.
∎

Just as for the case of the weighted topologies, the generalization to the case of manifolds is immediate.

Corollary 7.44
.

Let
M
be a manifold, then
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is
naively conical
. More explicitly, for any
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, with
\card(X)=n
, there exists
U\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
a neighborhood of
X
,
E
a Euclidean space, and
L\to\{n+1,\dots\}
a stratified space together with a stratified homeomorphism
\varphi\colon U\to E\times c_{q}(L)
.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to that of
7.38
, so we only sketch it here. Given
X=\{x^{(1)},\dots,x^{(n)}\}\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, we may find disjoint open neighborhoods
x^{(i)}\in E_{i}\subset M
together with homeomorphisms
\varphi\colon E_{i}\to B_{i}\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
, which we may assemble to give a homeomorphism
E=\coprod E_{i}\to\coprod B_{i}=B\subset\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
\mathrm{Ran}(B)\subset\mathrm{Ran}(\mathbb{R}^{p})
is then an open subset for the final topology, thus, by
7.43
,
\mathrm{Ran}(B)
is naively conical. In turn,
\mathrm{Ran}(E)
is naively conical and thus all points in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
admit a naively conical neighborhood.
∎

Remark 7.45
.

A plausible, but false, interpretation of the previous result would be the following. Since for all weights
\omega
,
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\omega})
is conically stratified, then for any
X\in\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, we must have a neighborhood of
X
in
\mathrm{Ran}(M)
,
U
such that
U\simeq E\times c(L)
for some link
L
where both
U
and
L
are equipped with the topology
\tau^{\omega}
. Then, when passing to the limit over
\omega\in\mathbb{W}
, somehow the topology on
c(L)
must become the quotient topology. This interpretation is wrong in two places. First, upon inspection of the formulas, one notices that
L
,
U
and
f
all depend directly on
\omega
. Thus, there is no single link
L
for which to compute the limit
(c(L),\tau^{\omega})
.
But also, if one were to fix a subset
A\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
, and compute the limit of the teardrop cones
c(A,\tau^{\omega})
over
\omega\in\mathbb{W}
, one would get the teardrop cone
c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
(see the next proposition).

The phenomenon actually occurring has to do directly with the teardrop topology. Observe that, in the cone
c(L)
, the neighborhoods of the apex do not depend at all on the topology of
L
but only on its underlying set. Thus, no common link (even with varying topology) can be found for all topologies
\tau^{\omega}
since basis of neighborhoods vary when
\omega
varies. And no link at all can be found such that
E\times c(L)
embeds into
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
since whatever
L
could be, such an embedding would provide a countable basis of neighborhoods of some point in
M
, which we know do not exist by
1.13
.

This sheds no light on why local homeomorphism with quotient cones exist however. Observe that, while we used the minimal weight
\chi
to define the underlying sets of
U
and
L
and the map
f
, any other weight would have worked equally well. This phenomenon is not directly related to
5.9
, but rather to the definition of
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
as the increasing union of the truncations
\mathrm{Ran}_{\leq n}(M)
. Each of those space has an unambiguously defined topology, and any of the map showing the local conicality can be restricted to the truncations. Note that, on the truncations, the links become compact, and thus quotient and teardrop topologies coincide. But then, passing to the colimit, that is, considering the increasing union
\cup_{n\geq 1}(c(L))_{\leq n}
, one gets the set
c(L)
equipped with the quotient topology. Going through the above process, with conical neighborhoods in
(\mathrm{Ran}(M),\tau^{\chi})
gives exactly the homeomorphism of
7.43
.

Proposition 7.46
.

Let
M
be a locally compact metric space, and
A\subset\mathrm{Ran}(M)
. Then
\lim_{\omega}c(A,\tau^{\omega})\simeq c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
where
c
denotes the cone with the teardrop topology.

Proof.

Let us first remark that, by
5.8
and by functoriality of the teardrop cone,
\mathrm{Id}\colon c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\to c(A,\tau^{\omega})
is continuous for any weight
\omega
.

Now let us show that
c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
satisfies the universal property. Let
f\colon E\to c(A,\tau^{\omega})
such that
f
is continuous for any weight
\omega
, and let us show that
f\colon E\to c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is continuous.

First remark that
c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\setminus\{[(0,Y)]\}\simeq(0,1)\times(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})\simeq\lim_{\omega}(0,1)\times(A,\tau^{\omega})\simeq\lim_{\omega}c(A,\tau^{\omega})\setminus\{[(0,Y)]\}
. So that
f
is continuous on
E\setminus f^{-1}(\{[(0,Y)]\})

Now, for
\epsilon\in(0,1)
, let
U=\pi([0,\epsilon)\times A)\subset c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
and let us show that
f^{-1}(U)
is open in
E
. Since
U
is open in any
c(A,\tau^{\omega})
and
f\colon E\to c(A,\tau^{\omega})
is continuous,
f^{-1}(U)
is indeed open in
E
. Thus
f\colon E\to c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is continuous, and
c(A,\tau_{\mathrm{fin}})
is indeed the limit.
∎
